MONDAY.

Before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Aldermen Bolton, Smethurst and James Evans; Messrs. W. Furness, W. Roberts, G. Pilling, and T. Locker.

Given a Chance.—Elizabeth Lavin, 24, Port- land-street, was charged with stealing two earthenware dishes, the property of Thomas Oultram, and also one pair of brass candle- sticks, the property of Annie Paget.—The Chief Constable explained that the woman had pre- viously been charged with stealing two mir,rors, but the police had not discovered the owner. The two dishes were seen safe on the shop shelf by Mrs. Oultram, but were later missed. Mrs. Lavin borrowed a jug at the shop, but this was not returned. Inquiries revealed that the woman had sold the dishes to Beatrice Aspinall, 20, Brierley-street. Mrs. Annie Paget, 2, Enfield- street, saw the candlpsticks safe when she went to bed and the next morning missed them. The prisoner was found in the yard next morning, and had sold the sticks.—Prisoner, who made her ninth appearance, said she had been upset through trouble since Christmas.—The case was adjourned for two months, in order to give the woman a chance to keep sober and behave better.

" Losing His Sight."—John Heaton generally I causes great amusement when he appears at the Court, and on Monday there was no exception when he appeared on a charge of being drunk and disorderly and police assault—P.C. Dixon saw prisoner, who was drunk, in Friar's Gate, stopping different people. When arrested he became very violent, and kicked right and left, also sticking him (the officer) under the chin.— trhomas Rowlands, 9, Wash-lane, came on the scene and assisted the officer in a commendable manner. He deposed to the prisoner being very violent, and using bad language on the way to the Police Station.—Prisoner: I was waiting for my employer.—The Chief Constable: He was waiting to get his cards stamped. There are 16 vacant places on them.—Prisoner: I am losing my sight looking for work.—The Chief Constable: If that was the truth he would have been in a blind institution years ago. He has been 92 times previously before the Court, and it is a charity to send him away.—Prisoner: I am a friend to myself if you will leave me alone. If you send me to prison you want me to " snuff " it, and I am not going to die there, in spite of you.—The Chairman: Three months.—Prisoner: The Lord stiffen you. I mean that fellow; I don't mean you (the Chief), sir.

CATHOLIC LECTURES

- - --- Sir,—! beg to thank you for the publication of my letters re Catholic evidence, but am sorry that you have found it necessary to close the correspondence, as I am sure it was, and still is, a matter of the deepest interest and import- ance to a very large section of your readers. J. RICHARDSON. 86, Buttermarket-street.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY CLOSING.

Sir,—Whatever may be the shortcomings of Mr. Benjamin T. Parkes, excessive modesty is -Certainly not one of them. In referring to the above Bill he says: "I would like this moderate measure placed on our Statute Book at once," a peremptory flourish worthy of Great Czesar him- self. His demand is based mainly on the fact that he witnessed a drunken brawl in Horse- market-street on a recent Sunday evening. He says: "Restrict the supply and drunken- ness will cease on Sunday." Will he undertake to prove that restrictions on the supply on week-days have lessened drunkenness? The Head Constable of Liverpool said, in a recent report, that the 'football field, picture palaces and music halls had tended to lessen drunken- ness, but he said nothing about restrictions. The fact is, the Prohibitionist is wasting his time. He is only hindering real temperance reform. He persistently votes against the Pure Beer Bill in the House of Commons, and when any attempt to cripple the tied-house system is made he tacks on clause after clause, aimed deliberately to annoy and pin-prick the moder- ate drinker (the real bete noir of the Prohibi- tionist and Sabbatarian), thereby making the whole thing unpopular. I conclude by quoting from the speech made in the House of Commons on Friday last during the debate on Sunday Closing, by Mr. G. Roberts, the Labour Member for 'Norwich. He said: "I agree that the House has a right to legislate against the vicious, but this Bill does no such thing. It legislates against milliOlis of entirely worthy citizens." A RADICAL.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCIL "SCENES."

Sir,—l wonder when the "free and indepen- dent" of Warrington will awake from their slumbers and enter a strong and vigorous pro- test against the unseemly exhibitions of a couple who have by some unfortunate circum- stances been returned to guard the interests of the ratepayers. If they have been sent by the aid of the party caucus to the Town Council, then for the honour and good name of the Borough let the burgesses have sufficient sense and intelligence to sweep away party politics from municipal affairs entirely. If it is im- possible to get candidates that will not ‘aever connection with party in local matters, then, in the name of all that is glorious, pick men that will reflect. credit on our Council Chamber and our old industrial town. The frequent scenes and personal squabbles are a disgrace to every man jack of us, and a discredit to our intellect.

It is surprising to me why one of our most brilliant councillors is continually selected by these gentry for attack and abuse—a man who commenced at the very lowest rung of the in- dustrial ladder, and by his grit and enterprise has become a leader in the commercial life of Warrington. He went forth to succeed, not to yield; to work out his own fortune with his own hands and brains, without influence or encouragement. He has given the best of his life, his great financial abilities and industrial knowledge, his comfort and leisure to the interest and welfare of his natiye town. He has certainly 'made mistakes, but what man this side God's acre has not?

--- Mr. R. Roberts deserves well of his fellow- citizens. ' Let us hope our " city fathers" will realise that the "man in the street" expects them to conduct themselves like gentlemen. IVANRQE.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTERS BY AN IRISHMAN--continued.

by Irishmen, living in Ireland, religious differ- ences would have Obliterated themselves. But once again England interfered to stop the pro- cess of amalgamation.

THE " LOYAL "__ MINORITY.

Lord Fitzwilliazn, the Viceroy, had gone over in 1795 to declare, like Lord Dudley, that Ire- land should be governed according to Irish ideas, but the reaction soon set in. The "loyal" minority protested against the " betrayal"; and what a leading Unionist M.P. called in 1904 the "wretched, rotten, sickening policy of conciliation," was abandoned. Lord Fitz- william was called, and the "loyal" minority were given every encouragement to drive the majority into rebellion. This element of the "loyal" minority has always existed, and has been the main cause of trouble between England and Ireland. In Elizabeth's day they were called specially the "People of the Pale," and they were those who deliberately encouraged war between the races or clans in the .hope of profiting by confisca- tions. Even in Elizabeth's day certain rulers, notably Ormond and Essex, showed a desire for conciliation, and the "People of the Pale" secured their recall and impeachment., Sir John Perrott wrote to Walsingham : "There is no good meaning in the people of the Pale and the Borderers towards the composition in- tended, or anything else that is good. They have overthrown the repeal of Poyning's Act." In 1795 it was the people of the Pale—the "Eng- lish Garrison"—who set themselves against the peaceful policy of Fitzwilliam. Beresford, the leader of that faction, was mainly instrumental in securing Fitzwilliam's recall, as he was, two or three years later, foremost in the barbarities which goaded Ireland into insurrection; and down to the present day there has always been a faction in Ireland whose one remedy for Ire- land's troubles has been the application of. coercion whenever redress has been demanded. Within five years after the recall of Fitz- William the "loyal" minority and the English overnment between them had first goaded the emtirai!n subject race in Ireland into revolt, and had yacthhethde uo suppressed the liberties of the whole cciiintry is. But beforeatioßrioftlie ismdinocruitsysing jS et w we re e shall consider what English government did for Ireland in the period of the Penal Laws, and what Ireland did for herself in the brief era of partial freedom.

F. W. R.

Next Week: "Opinions on the Period of the Penal Laws and on Grattan's Parliament."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), and Messrs. Rymer Young, T. Locker, and C. Jack-

son Holmes.

Missing Jewellery.—Walter Raymond, a young electrician, of Toll House, Grappenhall, was charged with stealing from the Talbot Inn two ladies' gold guards, patent pipe lighter, and a pencil case, value in all £5, the property of Thomas Weir.—The Chief Constable said that the prosecutor was the licensee of the Talbot Inn, where the prisoner was working the pre- vious week, and the articles had been stolen between last Sunday and the previous Sunday. The matter was reported to the police and Sergeant Reddington made inquiries, with the result that he taxed the prisoner with the theft. He at first denied all knowledge, but subse- quently admitted it. Some of the articles were packed away in his box at home, whilst the gold guard he had given to his sister, who was in service in Altrincham, but the police there had not been able to recover the property. Prisoner had been working at a skating rink, from where property had been reported missing, and it was in the prisoner's interest that the whole affair could be cleared up that he (the Chief Constable) asked for a week's remand.—This was granted. Cruelty to a Horse.—Joseph Hodson, 9, Stan- ley-street, was charged with cruelty to a horse by working it in an•unfit condition, and Thomas Unsworth, contractor, Hall-street, was charged with permitting it to be worked.—From the evidence of Inspector Dee, N.S.P.C.A., and Acting-Sergeant Whitley, it appeared that on April 29th the Inspector saw that the horse, which was attached to a cart and driven by Hodson, was lame, and he pulled it up. Hodson said the horse had always been like that and that he had mentioned it to Mr. Unsworth a few days previous. This Unsworth admitted.— After the magistrates had viewed the horse a fine of 10s. and costs was imposed on each defendant.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes, J. W. Hayward, and W. T. Williams.

" Very Much Obliged."—T. Herbert Williams, 1, Lomax-yard, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Arpley-road.—P.C. Bennett said that when arrested prisoner threatened to jump into the river, but he (wit- ness) got hold of him and stopped him.— Prisoner said he had gone with a pal instead of going to work and had got drunk.—Superinten- dent Williamson said prisoner was a native of Warrington, and was sent to a reformatory five years ago, but absconded from his ship. Since then he had gone to the bad continually. In a terse sentence the prisoner said that was not the truth.7The Superintendent: I don't think he is quite sober now.—Williams was sent to prison for one month.—Prisoner: I am very much obliged. Drapery M issed.--Berthe Beesley, alias Jones, 25, Douglas-street, Wigan, was charged with stealing various articles of drapery, valued .21, from Annie Oultram, 14, ICing-street.—lt was alleged that prisoner, with a man named Jones, lodged with the prosecutrix since the 4th of April. The man only remained a short time, but Mrs. Oultram agreed to let the woman stay until he should return. She disappeared, and it was ascertained that she had forwarded two boxes to Wigan. She was arrested in a mill at Wigan and endeavoured to put pawn tickets relating to the articles stolen in a tea can. She denied having taken two of the articles, but ad- mitted the theft of the others.—Prisoner, who was remanded until to-day, said she had arranged to replace the things.

THURSDAY. Before Messrs. J. Rymer Young (in the chair), W. Roberts, A. J. Jolley, T. W. Williams, and Dr. Naden. A Bad Assault.—Tames Cooper, railway em- ploye, 91, Fox-street, and George Cooper, 81, Fox. street, were charged with assaulting Thomas Randles, jun., 23, Percy-street, and Robert Littlewood, 93, Fox-street.—Mr. A. Browne pro- secuted and from the evidence he called it appeared that on Friday night as Littlewood was on his way to work he called at his mother- in-law's shop. When he was mounting his cycle again one of the defendants made an attack upon him, and as Thomas Randles came out of the house to see what was the matter lie was hit in the mouth.—George Cooper said he was trying to do Littlewood a good turn by trying to nettle the bother, hut lie would do him a bad one next time.—The Chairman said it was a most brutal and cowardly assault, and the defendants would each be fined 40s. and costs, in all .0 3s 6d'' or one month's hard labour.

You can put it as you like, said Mr. Grierly, it is a. case of deception.

Tariff Reform missionaries had been going about amongst the farm labourers, talking to them in the fields, in the lanes; they found them in the "pubs" and talking to them out- side the vicarage. (Laughter.) The vicarage and "pubs" were two strongholds of Tariff Re- form. (More laughter.) Would Mr. Baguley contradict that?

He hoped Mr.-Baguley would read it as well.

Three Different Badges. He had been told of a Tariff " Reform " speaker who had three badges each of a different political creed, one of which he used according to the colour of the meeting he was addressing. (Laughter.) He was a thorough Free Trader, not because it was a party question, but because he thought it was beneficial, to working men. He would be a Tariff " Reformer " to-morrow if he thought it would benefit the workers. He looked at the men who financed this thing, and so.long as he could remember they had been the enemies to working men in general and trade unions in particUlar. These men have been enemies and now they wanted to pose as our friends.

POINT OF ORDER. Mr. Shimmins rose on a point of order. Ile said Mr. Grierly was not answering the ques- tion.

Mr. Grierly : I have finished with Mr. Baguley and his question, and I am giving some of nar reasons. I say these men are our natural enemies and have always been so. Some of those men had been the guests of the Duke of Westminster and had to pay £l,OOO to attend the banquet—these men are prepared to give .tl,OOO for our benefit! Do you believe them? Tariff Reform meant rising the prices equal to the amount of the tariff. That was all it meant. An extra tax of about 4s. would be Placed on a/sack of flour from the foreigner. That woad enable the home grower to raise his 4s. Who would pay for it? The foreigner? lie would never touch it. Mr. Shimmins : Are we discussing Tariff Re- form? I move we keep to the point. The Chairman said probably if Mr. Grierly kept up his speech a Tariff Reformer would want to reply.

111.-PERIOD OF THE PENAL LAWS

In the 18th century Ireland was owned virLually by the Protestant minority, the major- ity of whom were settlers. But the Catholics— as I have already shown—being driven out of the ownership of the land, threw themselves with zeal into trade and Ireland began to build up industries. This was seen with grudging eyes by English merchants, who appealed to the English Parliament, with the unfortunate result that the said Parliament crushed the Irish industries. , • .

By the Cattle Acts og 1665 and 1680' the im- portation in England, from Ireland, of all cattle, sheep, swine, beef, pork, bacon, mutton, or cheese, was prohibited. The immediate re- sult of these Acts was to restrict the trade with England, and to stimulate trade with foreign countries and with. the English Plantations— and perhaps to increase Irish shipping. In 1680 we read that for the last five years there : were seldom less than 20 Irish ship:, at Dun- kirk, laden with beef, mutton, tallow, hidei, leather and wool. Irish ships were seen at Ostend, Naples, La Rochelle, and in the I, rendi West Indies. (NOTE: The trade of these places with England herself fell off.—" Commercial Relations between England and Ireland," A. E. Murray.) But Ireland was not suffered long to enjoy this provision trade in peace. By a long series of Navigation Acts commerce was, as completely as the laws could affect their purpose, destroyed. "The conveniency of ports and harbours which Nature so liberally be- stowed upon this kingdom," says Swift, "is of no more use to us than a man shut up in a dungeon." Forbidden to export cattle, Irish- men now turned their attention to wool, Th:ch had long been their staple internal trade. "The quality of the wool," wrote Le,cky, the Unionist historian, "was supremely good. A real ind:is- trial enthusiasm arose in the Irish natioa. Great number's of English, Scotch, and evcr, foreign manufacturers, came over. Many thousands of men were employed in the trade; and all the signs of a rising industry were visible.". But this was not to be. The English House of Commons petitioned William 111. to discourage the woollen manufacture, an in 1699 the export from Ireland of manufactured wool to any country whatsoever was absolute7y prohibited, and that of raw wool, except to England. "So ended," continues Mr. Lecky, 'the fairest promise Ireland had ever known of becoming a prosperous andlhappy country. The ruin was absolute and final." Some half-hearted attempts were made .to replace the woollen industry by the manufacture of linen. As early as 1672 the Irish Parliament had voted a sum. of money to be applied for the encouragement of the manufacture of fine linen; and from the fourth year of Anne to the nine- teenth of George IL passed no less than four- teen Acts for the encouragement of Irish indus- try. But even this trade, though confined, to Ulster, and long conducted upon a very sniall -scale, indeed, was not allowed to remain un- hindered. Fierce opposition arose in England, when it was proposed, in 1708, to extend the manufacture to Leinster. It was feared that if the Irish linen should replace the Dutch linen in England, the Dutch might no longer care to admit English woollens into Holland. Prohibitive duties were laid on Irish linens, checked, striped, painted, or dyed, when im- ported into Great, Britain. So one might go on through the whole range of Irish industries, and the only conclusion and summing up is found in the Unionist Lecky : "That it became evident that England possessed both the will and the power to crush every form of Irish industry as soon as it became sufficiently prosperous to com- pete in any degree with her own manufacture., It appeared useless to persist, and a general commercial despondency prevailed"; and, adds Mr. A. J. Balfour, " that was an unhappy time when England thought fit to suppress Irish industries." Thousands emigrated to America and the Continent; the masses of the people, denied other employment, were driven back to . the land, and by their frantic competition for 1 this, the only means by which to live, raising rents and increasing the poverty of all save a . few.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
PROTESTANTS AND CATHOLICS

SUFFERED.

These restrictions were aimed, not at Irish Catholics or at the native Irish; they were part of " the government aCqording . to • English ideas." This meant the sacrificing of every Irish interest that could possibly compete with , . . an English one. Lecky, in his " History of Ireland," says "Protestants then began to find that they were as little thought of as Catholics. The suppres- sion of the woollen trade brought ruin upon 12,000 Protestant families in Dublin, and 20,000 in the rest of the country. By her commercial laws England deliberately crushed the pros- perity of the Protestant colony of Ireland, drove thousands of them into exile, arrested the influx of Protestant population from Great Britain, and inspii:ed the Presbyterians of the North with a bitter hatred of her rule."

_ -- So indiscriminating was the oppression that it began to bring Protestant and Catholic to- gether in Ireland in defence of the common interest. This was foreseen and deplored by the English Government. Archbishop Boutler —one of the chief agents of the Government in Ireland—wrote: "When Protestant and Papist unite, go:xi-bye to the English interest in Ire- land." Yet in spite of the English Government Protestant and Papist did unite and the Irish Parliament of Protestants began to repeal the Penal Laws.

In 1778 the Catholics were given leave to hold landed property. In 1779 the joint action of Catholics and Protestants secured Free Trade between England and Ireland. In 1781 Catho- lics were permitted to keep a school—if they could get leave from the Protestant Bishop of the diocese. This was not mere generosity, it was statesmanship. It was the beginning of the government of Ireland "according to Irish ideas" by Irishmen, who felt that Protestant and Catholic needed each other. Grattan said: "So long as the Penal Code remains we can never be a great nation I would not keep two millions of my fellow- countrymen in a state of slavery. I desire not a Protestant settlement, but an Irish nation."

POLITICAL AND RELIGIOUS DISABILITIES.

Political enfranchisement went hand in hand with the removal of religious disabilities. In 1782 the Irish Legislature was given sole power to make laws for Ireland. The first Act of the enfranchised Parliament was to repeal the Acts against hearing or celebrating Mass, and gener- ally to sweep away the last restrictions of the Penal Code. Amongst its laws was one to legalise the' marriages of Presbyterians, which the English Government had refused to recog- nise.—Judges were given security of tenure; the English Government had prepared to keep them subject to dismissal. Now an Irish Parliament was dealing with the Irish problem. It treated questions on their merits; it did not wait to be forced into con- cessions—as the English Government had been when it granted self-government under the armed menace of the Volunteers. The English Parliament had for 100 years denied to Roman Catholics, practically all civil rights. The Irish Protestant Parliament, within ten years after its establishment, passed a law giving to Roman Catholics the vote on a very liberal franchise. The next year saw almost all civil and military rights granted to Catholics. In 1795 a Bill to admit Catholics into Parliament was read for the first time. There is no doubt about the platter that if the process of amal- gamation had been allowed to go on, the Irish ,Protestant Parliament would have given full snd equal rights to their Catholic fellow- citizens and that, in Ireland, which was then governed more or less according to Irish ideas, _

MR. PLINSTON'S STATEMENT. Explains to Trades COunell His Attitude

Over Caretakers' Grievance.

TARIFF REFORMER'S LETTER.'

_ There were some stirring questions discussed at the monthly meeting of the Trades and Labour Council, held on Tuesday night. Mr. E. Plinston, the Council's representative on the Education Committee, made his statement with regard to his attitude over the caretakers' griev- ance, and Mr. Grierly made his reply to a letter received from the Tariff Reform League with reference to a 'statement he made at the pre- vious meeting of the Council. ' The Chairman (Mr. E. Randles) said that since the last meeting two more mieties had joined the Council. They were the Carpenters and Joiners' and the Clerks', to whom they offered congratulations. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140516_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
.THE CARETAKERS' GRIEVANCE.

Mr. E. Plinston very much regretted his en- forced absence from the past two meetings of that organisation. For no reasons but honest ones would he be absent, because the business was of as great an interest to himself as it was to anybody else. A few months ago a vote of thanks was passed by the Trades and Labour Council to Councillor Broadhurst, for what he had said with reference to the question of the overtime of caretakers. The full details of the question were not known by the Trades and Labour Council, or they would not have passed that vote of thanks.

In Warrington t 143, had three provided schools, where, he believed, for the services ren- dered outside the ordinary elementary work, a payment was made—at all events, caretakers at these schools were perfectly satisfied with their conditions. The trouble was with the non- provided schools, where the managers were en- tirely responsible for the payment or non-pay- ment of services rendered.

Not Responsible. He was not responsible for the matter being adjourned for two months. A request was made for the fullest details to be sent in, and at a meeting of the Finance Committee a fortnight .ago, ,the statements of the clerks of the different education authorities showed without exception that there was no power, no control, no payment made to caretakers for duties performed outside elementary work by the local education author- ity. All they could do was to request, and there were gentlemen present who knew what it was to request. During the eleven years he had been their representative on the Education Com- mittee he had attended the meetings very regu- lar and there were few men who had a better record of attendances.

r OUT OF THE RATES. Mr. Hewitt asked if the caretakers' wages came out of the rates?

Mr. Plinston : Yes. Mr. Hewitt said in 1907 they were paid in " sevenths," and it was illegal to do so out of the ratepayers' money. In Warrington there were ten schools where they had Sunday schools and twelve where they did not. They would be Pleased if the delegate of the Trades and Labour Council would try to do away with Sunday labour. It was a downright shame to be placed in sevenths.

WHO EMPLOYED THEM? Mr. Shimmins asked if the Education 'Com- mittee were the caretakers' employers, had not they power to alter the hours of labour? When the matter was brought forward, Mr. Plinston, according to the papers, proposed that .the matter lie on the table, as it was not a matter for them to dis Cuss. He (Mr. Shimmins) took exception to that, owing to the fairness of the application of the caretakers, and he could not understand why Mr. Plinston had taken that view of the matter. The caretakers in the non-provided schools worked seven days to the week, and he would like to know who ought to pay for the work on the seventh day—the ratepayers or the church?

Grossly Unfair. It was grossly uiifairthat the caretaker should not be paid for Sunday work. Ile was disap- pointed with Mr. Plinston's remarks, because he expected a full report. They were all satis- fied that there should be some method of dealing with the needy school children in Warrington. He had never seen where Mr. Plinston had brought that forward at the Education Com- mittee, although he might have done so. Fur- ther, there were school boys leaving at the age of 13. It was a shame to Warrington. From his own personal knowledge there were boys given permission to leave at that early age, whilst the parents were earning good wages. He had never heard of Mr. Plinston opposing that. They had a delegate from the Trades and Labour Council, not from a Sunday school, and they expected that delegate to take these matters up. He thought everyone would admit that the caretaker's' case was a glaring grievance and the 2s. increase was not as much as they were entitled to. How long was it since they had a rise in wages before? The cost of living had gone up considerably, house rents since January had risen from 5 to 28 per cent., and the caretakers deserved at least a 15 per cent. rise. He never saw Mr. Plinston advocate that, although he might have done so. He thought they should say from that Council what they would like Mr. Plinston to vote for on the Edu- cation Committee.

CARETAKERS' SECRETARY'S STORY. Mr. Hewitt said that some of the caretakers had not 5 per cent, rise and others not 10 per cent.

Mr. Baker asked if it was wise that a child should continue at school if it had passed the highest standard. Mr. Howard said he was delighted to hear Mr. Plinston's report and excuses, but he would like to say that it was not until some nine weeks ago that he learned that they had a delegate on the education authority of the town. During the last nine weeks a thousand men and lads had been on strike, and there would be over thousand children who would have suffered, for the men only received about 15s. a week. Dur- ing the course of the strike the voice of Labour had not been raised to bring about the feeding of the kiddies.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
"AN AWKWARD POSITION."

Mr. Helsby said it was putting Mr. Plinston in an awkward position, if he voted in favour of keeping children at school until they were 14 years of age. That helped to keep wages up, but some people could not see it. Some time ago, the Council refused to give Mr. Plinston a definite instruction in this matter. , The Chairman said if they had more Labour .representatives on the local bodies, perhaps they would be able to do something. •

at the mere MR. PLINSTON REPLIES. Mr. Plinston said that the caretakers were the servants of the local education anthority for just the elementary school work. They were outside their ordinary elementary work. He had fought for the Points raised by Mr. y of the managers for the payment Shimmins many times, but as they had not been, passed by the .committee they did not come before the Council. The meeting decided that a full report from Mr. 'Plinston be heard at the next monthly meeting. ,

===== 0002601_19140516_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WAKES WEEK.

Mr. Dukes gave a report of the Wakes Week Conference. It appeared to him that the humanitarian standpoint was overlooked, and that of business conveniences took its place. So far as the Territorial training was concerned, he thought that was looked upon as a national necessity, and as such the time for training should not be pilfered from the workers' holi- day. (Hear, hear.) He did not mind falling into line with the Chamber of Commerce to discuss this thing, but he suggested in all seriousness that they would never provide a solution to the problem by try- ing to meet all business conveniences in a town of multiple industries. He suggested to the delegates that in the trade unions there should be made provision for the workers to set aside a weekly sum to enable them to enjoy a week's holiday. By this means they would grow accus- tomed to the holiday, and create in the minds of the men the desire to obtain a pioportionate increase in their wages commensurate to the amount to take them away. The report was adopted.

PENSIONS FOR WIDOWS.

A letter was read from the Coventry Trades and Labour Council with reference to pensions for widows.

A resolution was carried in favour of granting pensions to widows who had been left with families, the payment to be at the rate of 10s. a week.

ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's Associa- tion.

N.A. CENTRE.

THE WEAVER. Up to the present we have nothing further to give members concerning the Weaver, but whether we accept terms or not, a general meet- ing will be held, so that all members may voice their opinion on the matter. The places for our matches must be selected in. the near future, and, according to the general opinion of mem- bers, one match at least will be fished in a canal. Whether this will do good or not, the majority desire a. change, so we cannot see where any great harm to the society can take place, whether terms are made with the North- wich officials or not. The terms offered are quite within reach of working men, but when they come to be reckoned collectively the amount is great. We wish to provide angling for our members without raising the subscriptions, but st the price offered by Northwich this cannot be done.

Our point is this—that out of a membership of, say, 150, there would not he a dozen fish the river more than twelve times in the season, and if we take both our matches there at the rate now offered we would find it very expensive. So matters will have to stand until we hear from the Northwich secretary, and then decide the whole affair at a general meeting.

RAMBLING MATCH.

Members who have booked their seats in the char-a-bane for the match on Sunday will start from, the Gladstone Arms at 8 a.m.: match starts at 11 a.m.; three hours' fishing. All mem- bers will start from the Shrewsbury Arms at 11 a.m. and most be back there at 2 p.m. to weigh in, no walking time allowed. Only trout and eels to weigh. Pike be rred. A.S.

Owing to had trade the Burnley Weavers' Association have decided to suspend the mem- bers' contributions this week-end.

Hints and Interesting Facts and

Chat for Amateurs.

The Black Leghorn will be found excellently suited for a backyard, providing the whole of the run be well covered, and aboa a fodt of dry litter or earth be kept on the ground for scratching. It is an economical breed and asso- ciated with profit. Every attention must be paid to strain; but, fortunately, record layers of this stylish little fowl are easily obtained, and if care is taken not to overfeed, an abundance of good-sized eggs will be obtained. With proper attention to shelter, and nourishing food neces- sary for keeping such fowls in vigorous health, Black Leghorns will be found a decided source of profit. The cost of feeding should not ex- ceed one Denny per week, including soft food in the morning, and grain as the last feed, not for- getting scraps and green food at midday. Whilst admittedly of little use for flesh production, as far as eggs are concerned this variety will answer all requirements, the eggs being large, often 21oz. and 2ioz., which, considering the Email size jof the Leghorn, is most satisfactory. If kept in ceaseless activity, and not overfed, it stands winter weather well, and individual records of 200 eggs per bird per year have been numerous during the past few years.

KILLING CHICKENS. . For ordinary purposes killing by dislocation of the neck is the better plan, but .where some- thing very special is being produced the other method, by knifing, may be recommended. rKilling a chicken is not really so disagreeable a task as may appear. Both the above methods are instantaneous and painless. In the former instance the bird is held in the left hand by the hocks and long flight feathers, while the head is gripped between the first and second fingers of the right hand, and firmly but gently extended in a downward direction until the neck breaks. Immediately the jerk is felt pulling should cease; otherwise the head will come away, which is undesirable. No blood whatever is seen when killing in this manner, and plucking can com- mence at once. When knifing a bird rather more care and experience are required, besides which it is a somewhat dirty method, the blood flowing from the wound very freely. The bird is laid upon its back on a table and the mouth opened; the point of the knife is inserted into the slit in the roof of the mouth, and by means of a steady press the brain is pierced. The chicken then requires to be hung up by the leg for a few minutes in order that the blood may drain away, and so leave the flesh white and of a good flavour.

UTILITY POULTRY CLUB COMPETITION.

The appearance of the manager's report for the sixth period of four weeks reminds us that this competition has now run nearly half its course, and it is a good moment to take stock of the results achieved and to compare the working of the two systems. The small house and run section consists of thirty-one pens of four birds each, the houses being 12ft. long by Bft. wide, 6ft. flin. high in front, Mt. at back, and are divisible in the middle by a partition with a door at each end; an ample grass run is obtain- able. Each half house accommodates two pens, and the floor space is all available for scratch- ing. In the other section the thirty-one pens, which it is of interest and importance to note are of the same breed and entered by the same competitors as those in the other section, are penned in a large house 40ft. long by 20ft. wide, 9ft. high in front, sloping to Mt. at back. The floor is covered with flin. of litter, so that the whole floor space, except a portion under the perches, is available for scratching purposes. There is a grass run of an acre, into which the birds are let out in suitable weather. The competition, which has been financed by the Development Commissioners, has arranged

TRAM MEN'S WAGES.

A Farthing Per Hour Advance is Again Withdrawn.

ITEMS FROM THE COUNCIL.

The question of whether the tramway em- ployes should receive one half-penny or one farthing per hour advance is again in abey- ance. At the meeting of the Town Council on Wednesday evening the Tramway Committee's minutes stated: "The Chairman reported that at the last meeting of the Council he had with- drawn the following resolution with reference to the increase of wages of the motormen and conductors: 'An application from the motor- men and conductors for an increase of wages, in consequence of the loss incurred by the altera- tion in the Sunday service, was considered.— Resolved, that the wages of the motormen and conductors be increased by id. per hour.'—Re- solved, that the same be re-submitted to the Council."

Dr. JOSEPH said the tramway employes, since the last meeting of the Council, had approached the committee, asking that they might send a deputation to discuss the question of the in- Grease. The committee had no objection to meeting a deputation, and on account of that lie asked permission to withdraw the resolution, so that it could be further considered. Leave was given to withdraw the resolution. APPOINTMENT OBJECTED TO. Councillor Ganon objected to the appointment of Mr. W. Healey as an assistant weights and measures inspector under the Markets Com- mittee. He moved the matter back, on the grourd that the voting had not been fair to the other applicants.

Alderman bans EvArrs strongly repudiated this, and said the voting on the Markets Com- mittee was scrupulously fair. Mr. Healey was appointed by six votes to four given to the next candidate.

Councillor Gough did not find a seconder and the amendment dropped.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 16, 1914.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Is there a Possibility of a Successful Cycleplane ?

THE MOTOR CYCLETTE.

At this time of the year there are always a number of people hovering between the pur- chase of a new bicycle or motor cycle. Expense usually decides for them, but the lack of mechanical knowledge and a wholesale dread of " such noisy things" also have their influence. The high-powered motor cycle is certainly a rather tough proposition to the lady rider or the novice, although even this has reached such a state of excellence as to require a very minimum of attention. All auxiliary sets are open to one very grave objection; their very nature means an increased strain on a machine designed by the maker for quite .a different usage. Whether additional power is generated by an auto-wheel fastened to the rear of the machine, or by a motor set attached to the frame, the difficulty

tiler erosion. For dressing the faces of the platinum paints a simple home-made tool can be used. A strip of thin steel, such as a piece of a hackshaw blade is taken, and fine emery cloth is glued to each side. It will smooth up I)oth' surfa6es at once, and if the work is care- fully done, leave them parallel. This latter point is important if long life is to be consid- ered and freedom from burning experiefited.

, Reliability Trial in the Austrian Alps. For some reason or other the reliability trial is out of favour in this country. What seems to be required is the rejuvenation of the sport- ing element, even if it means that the purely commercial side must suffer. During the last few years every single trial seems to have de- generated into a huge advertisement campaign. Number one used somebody's tyres; number two swears by somebody else's lubricating oil; the makers of such and such a car now hold the world's record for the best performance for a low-priced vehicle; and so on indefinitely. No wonder the sporting motorist fights shy ! The forthcoMing Austrian trials will, on the con- trary, be an event after his own heart, even if commercialism is not entirely absent. Given some of the most magnificent scenery in Europe, indeed, in the whole world, and a good car, what more can the heart of a sportsman desire? Why for hilly work there is nothing at home to com- pare with the ascent of the Katschberg, and much of the district is on a par. There is no need to speak of the trial as an advertising medium; that is obvious enough, as Continental manufacturers are quick to recognise.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

AN IMPROVED OUTLOOK. The rain came in time to avert permanent injury to field and garden crops and fruit plan- tations, says "The Times." The downpours, especially on Thursday, were fairly general, but fortunately were heaviest where they were most needed. Correspondents in the home counties write hopefully of the prospect now that the drought has ended. Although, the relief may be only momentary in a physical sense, its in- fluence upon vegetation and the general outlook is far-reaching and important. The value of a change in weather depends largely upon the time at which it occurs, and it was because of its opportuneness that last week's rainfall was productive of such beneficent results. While May has retrieved the situation in some respects, it has had an opposite effect in others. Serious injury to potatoes and other ground crops due to frost on Saturday week is reported from Worcestershire, Shropshire and Yorkshire. In the Vale of Evesham 9deg. of frost were registered and at Ludlow lldeg. The damage done_.to early potatoes, peas, and in some in- stances to bush fruit and plums is described as irrevocable. Most crops can withstand 6deg. or 7deg. of frost, and will even recover from Bdeg., but 9deg. or over is usually fatal.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCARCITY OF PIGS.

Students of agricultural economics are not seriously perturbed by the reduction in the aggregate of pigs, because they have always been subject to sudden and pronounced fluctua- tions. There has been nothing approaching stability since the country has been accustomed to receive supplies of pig meals from abroad, and, having regard to the fecundity of the pig, which makes It a simple matter to repair defi- ciencies, and renders production liable to jump suddenly from one extreme to another, it would be useless to count upon any material change in the future. Pig-raising as a commercial concern is too subject to emphatic reaction to be exempt from the characteristics of specula- tive• enterprise—at any rate in a country with open ports and attractive markets. , The question of immediate importance for pig- owners is the extermination of swine fever. This plague has been a dominating factor for a full generation, and pig-owners have shown their readiness to co-operate with the local and central authorities in their efforts to stamp it out by the patience with which they have accepted and complied with irksome restrictions upon the movement of animals. There are signs, however, that their forbearance is almost exhausted—a circumstance that is not surpris- ing since success seems as remote as ever. The general opinion is that pig-dealers and the keepers of ordinary pigs have much to answer for in propagating and spreading the disease. It is 'contended that if the authorities concentrated their energies upon the supervi- sion of trading and marketing methods the dis- ease would be held in cheek and possibly extir- pated. There have been isolated outbreaks among pedigree pigs, but the general immunity of high-class herds is one of the most significant features in the history of the disease. This cannot be due entirely to the isolation of these herds, for in the summer months their best representatives are exhibited all over the country.—" The Times."

THE ROYAL AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY. The committee appointed by the Royal Agri- cultural Society to consider .the question of increasing the membership of the society recommends that additional exclusive facilities be provided for members at the shows, and also that classes be included for cereals and other Produce. With a view to meeting the com- plaint of the arable farmer that the shows have been monopolised by stockowners, the committee suggest that a show 9f grain and seeds be held at Nottingham next October.

BRITISH DAIRY FARMERS.

The annual conference of the British Dairy Farmers' Association will be held in Devon this year. The conference begins to-day at Exeter, when the visitors will be welcomed by the Mayor at a reception at the Guildhall. Excursions will be made to various farms and places of interest in the county.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
POTATO WART.

In conducting what was stated to be the first prosecution in the North-west of England under the Wart Disease of Potatoes Order, at Kendal on Saturday, an inspector of the Board of Agri- culture stated that the matter was most seriously regarded by his Department, as it affected many farmers in the North who ex- ported potatoes to South Africa. In their regu- lations the South African authorities would not admit potatoes which were grown within five miles of an infected area.

SOMETHING LIKE A COW! The world's record for a dairy cow has, it is said, been broken by May Relma, a Guernsey, at Chesterbrook Farm, Pennsylvania, which in 365 days gave 19,639.501 b. of milk, containing 1,059.591 b. butterfat. Her year's work has been carefully supervised under the revised regula- tions for the Advanced Register of Guernsey Cattle, conducted by the American Guernsey Cattle Club. May Relma is producing more butterfat in her thirteenth month than in her first. She was dropped December 15th, 1906, and is milked three times a day-4 a.m., 12 noon, and 8 p.m. She is fed with 181 b. per day of the following mixture: 2501 b. bran, 100 lb. gluten, 501 b. cotton-seed meal, 501 b. oil meal, 501 b. hominy; also 3113. beet pulp and 31b. molasses; alfalfa hay, clover hay, and ensilage at liberty. Sixteen pounds of carrots were given daily from January 24th.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR FEDERATION. AN IMPORTANT NEW BODY FORMED.

A conference was held in the Miners' Hall, Millgate, Wigan, on Saturday, to form a new organisation, a federation of the various Labour parties in Lancashire. Mr. W. Speakman (chairman of the Newton Division Labour party) presided. It was stated that it was essential that the Labour parties in the county area should be combined and interlinked with one another to prevent overlapping in view of the fact that the work of the Labour party as a whole would in the future be very much greater than in the past, and that there would be more and more Labour representation on local bodies. There was no intention of clashing with the National Labour party. The conference resolved unanimously that it was desirable to form an organisation to be known as the Lancashire Federation of Labour parties, and it was decided that the objects of the newly-constituted organisation should be to help in every possible way to perfect the organ- isation and to promote the efficiency of the : Labour party in each Parliamentary Division, and to assist in the forming of a local Labour party where one does not already exist; to en- courage harmony and unity between the Labour parties in each division; to consider any dis- puted point that may arise, and to advise as to a settlement; to promote Labour represehtation upon all local and county administrative bodies and in Parliament; to prepare a list of speakeTs which shall include all Labour Members of Par, liament and adopted Labour candidates in Lan- cashire, and, when possible, to. arrange for special lecturing tours consecutively through the various constituencies. It was suggested by a delegate that the new,

organisation should include Cheshire as well as Lancashire within its area.

--- The Chairman gave his opinion that the new organisation should be styled as the Lancashire Federation of Labour parties, but added that parties operating on the borders of the county could, of course, be included. The delegate pointed out that if the name of Cheshire was included, this might be the means of bringing such places as Stockport and Hyde into the federation.

The Chairman said that, if it was considered desirable later, the area of the federation could be extended so as to cover Cheshire as well as Lancashire.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Calceolarias which have been properly hard- ened should be lifted with a ball of soil and planted in their flowering quarters. Give the plants a good watering before lifting and after planting. Make a sowing of Canadian Wonder beans. Sow lettuce, parsley, herbs, &c. Sow vegetable marrow seed in pots in a cold frame.

Kohl Rabbi is a good substitute for turnips, and a sowing may now be made in rich soil, in drills 12 inches apart. Thin out to 6 inches. Keep the soil well stirred between the rows. The pentstemon is one of the finest of our summer perennials, and in a mild winter, like the one just gone, keep alive in the open. It is not sufficiently hardy, however, to withstand a hard winter, and cuttings should be struck in the autumn and wintered in a cold frame. In the South of England it is the wactice to layer the shoots like carnations. A very fine variety is Crimson Gem, the flowers of which are carried on long spikes. There are several excellent varieties worthy of a place in any garden. Here is another slug recipe: Mix some old beer with a quantity of fine soil to form a paste. Put the paste on a saucer and place if level with the soil. The slugs will be attracted to the paste.

CULTIVATION OF THE POLYANTHUS. There is hardly a more popular flower than the polyanthus, which is a permanent variety of the common primrose. It exists in a multi- tude of varieties and being hardy and free- flowering, has been looked upon as a reliable bedding plant in many gardens. It is one of our earliest spring flowers. Of late years new and striking varieties have been introduced, carrying larger blooms of unusual colour. When grown in genial soil plenty of offsets are pro- duced, which may be separated from the parent plants in August and planted. The polyanthus is also suited for pots and window boxes.

PACKING CUT FLOWERS. Few people know how to pack flowers properly when sending them away. Many blossoms are ruined. The popular method seems to be to put the flowers into a box as loosely as possible, and then to sprinkle a little water over them. This is all wrong. As long as the specimens are not actually crushed the more tightly they are Packed the better. When put in closely the dowers do not knock about in transit and they do not lose moisture rapidly. This is very im- portant, and no amount of external dampness will save them from fading if they are freely exposed to the action of the air. Moreover, drops of water are apt to turn the blossoms a rusty colour. Wooden boxes are better than those which are made of card, seeing-that they help to keep the moisture in. Best of all are light tins, in which the blooms will travel comfortably, and be fresh at the journey's end, even though they may be a week or more on the road.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

SUNFLOWER CULTURE.

Hitherto sufficient attention has not been paid to the commercial advantages of the sunflower. It is extensively grown in Russia, the United States, and other parts of the globe, but not so ' much in this country. This mammoth flower flourishes in a damp situation in a moderately strong soil. The seed is usually sown from 3 feet to 3f feet apart, and from 2 inches to 3 inches deep. The young plants are generally thinned out, so as to be from 12 inches to 18 inches apart. The quantity of seed required per acre is about seven pounds. The heads are gathered before they are quite ripe. In America quantities of seed are cultivated for poultry purposes. The chickens like the seed, and fatten quickly upon it, owing to the rich oil it con- tains. Smallholders who have odd corners of land unsuitable for ordinary cultivation will be well advised in experimenting in sunflower cul- tivation. The stalks may be burnt, and the ashes returned to the soil as a fertiliser.—"Agri- cultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

BORDER AURICULAS. Border auriculas are so easily raised from seed that it is hardly worth while to trouble about dividing and increasing the old plants, unless the variety happens to be an exceptionally good one. A few scores of seedlings occupy but little space, and it is therefore advisable to sow a pinch of seed every year, and thus have a strong, healthy batch of young plants always at hand.

When such is the case, the old, worn-out plants may be thrown away. Seed can hardly be obtained and sown too soon after it has ripened. Sow in shallow boxes in fine sandy soil, then gently water, cover with brown paper, and stand in a frame or greenhouse. Auricula seed, as a rule, takes longer to germinate if kept till the spring than it does when sown as soon as ripe.

If any readers grow the " Dusty Miller" they should go over the plants if they wish to save their own seed and mark a few of the finest flowers, harvest the seeds, and a week or two later sow, wintering the seedlings in the boxes and planting out in their permanent quarters.

ONTARIO FARM LABOURERS.

SYMINGTO c©FgEraimikiENCE

The Cefee Maid, of Symington's, says: " Up in the morning, sleepy and cold, presto, change !—and you're cheery and bold, What's the magic? How is it done? just try a cup of SYMINGTON." THOS. SYMINGTON & CO., Edinbirrth and London.

The Ontario Conducted Party of farm labour- ers which left for Haldimand County, Ontario, last month, were all placed in positions imme- diately on arrival, and a number of vacant positions had to remain unfilled for want of men. It is two years now since conducted parties for Haldimand County were commenced, and the record of the party which went out last year is worthy of notice. Ninety-four per cent, of this party are still in the positions provided for them when they first arrived, an,il three of the remaining 6 per cent. are now farming on their own. A number of these passengers Were assisted in their fares, and have since repaid all the money advanced. It is a compliment both to the quality of the employers and of the Jaen who left England, that one year after they arrived they should still be in the same posi- tions.

is still there. thc light-weight motor cycle or the inoto,_. cyclette, as it is being called, is in quite artothcr catevry. It is primarily a motor cycle with the human power as auxiliary. For ladies or riders getting on in years there is nothing more suitable, and the most energetic rider can pedal to his heart's content up hills or against the strongest winds, if the motor pace is too slow for him. Another tangible ad- vantage of the motor cyclette is the price—from .C2O upwards.

Pedal Bicycle Sidecar. One of the latest innovations is the sidecar attached to the ordinary cycle; but from what 1 have been told of the exPerienes of actual users, I do not fancy that it will ever succeed in mak- ing itself popular. Under ordinary conditions cycling is often sufficiently hard without any additional burden. Such a contrivance would take all pleasure out of my spins. I cannot imagine anyone tackling a steep hill, or facing a strong wind, with a sidecar and passenger. I have always considered the sidecar in the case of the motor cycle an unnatural arrangement, but I should think with a push bicycle it would be an impossibility. The alignment of the best- made cycle in the world would be affected by such an attachment in a very short space of time, even if no greater damage were to be done to the machine. The old trailer was rejected on account of its weight and .its liability to turn turtle, and even if the latter point does not apply in this case the first is sufficient to doom this suggestion to failure. In the majority of instances all pleasure would be destroyed for the persoil who was doing the pedalling. No, I ion't think there is any future before the cycle sidecar.

A Cycleplane Club. There has been a considerable amount of talk during thq past few weeks with reference to the formation of a club for experimental purposes. When a journal of repute such as " Cycling " lends its columns for the discussion of such a subject, it is surely a sign that something is about to be done. The whole problem of flight resolves itself into a question of ,the relation between the weight of the engine and its power. The human machine is defective in this respect. The latest type of internal combustion engine develops one horse-power for every 21b. weight; therefore, in proportion a man of twelve stone would have to generate over 60 horse-power. This is an impossibility, as is even the half or quarter:. In other words, a person is useless as an aeroplane engine. Both .scientists and prac- tical aviators hold the opinion that there is no future before the cycleplane; hence, in my opinion, to establish a club for this form of flight can only result in a successful means of spending a considerable sum of money, without the gaining of any useful information.

A New Petrol Engine.

The coming of the internal combustion engine brought revolution in its train. For some years after its introduction it was realised that this type of engine would provide the necessary power for aeroplanes, but it was not until the own flywheel. The wTliileiciliattehset Gnome engine was built that any great progress was made. The Gnome engine has eliminated the flywheel by the simple process of making the cylinders—seven in this case—rotate btuhteminseslyueesh, thus acting as their sraotmuerypieitiviele iisiasthbeee;Setmatbaoxd, a manner that it may be applied to t"he motor car. The cylinders here, varying from three to ten, are arranged parallel with and revolving round the crank axle. This arrangement is in- genious, since it results in less weight, decreased centrifugal force, and a more compact appear- ance. The weight is particularly low, for one engine exhibited at Olympia recently developed one horse-power for every 21b. of its weight. A five-cylinder, 44 horse-power engine, giving 1,200 revolutions per minute, weighed only 1721 b. In addition to this advantage the Statax engine consumes less petrol and lubricating oil than the radical cylinder type,

Hints That Pay.

The following hints are given in the "Motor in Australia," and as they are so practical I have pleasure in passing them on. For clean- ing tarnished metal work an ordinary ink eraser is very useful, as the grit in the rubber removes the dirt rapidly. It makes, however, tine scratches and should, therefore, not be used for delicate surfaces. To restore dull files to their original state they can be immersed in a solution consisting of equal parts of water and nitric acid for about five hours. When taken out of this bath they should be washed in sodium bicarbonate solution to hinder any fur-

===== 0002601_19140516_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
A TEMPORARY BACK AXLE REPAIR.

I was recently unfortunate enough to break the back axle of the car I was driving. The following particulars as to -how I got the car home to the garage may be of interest to other readers. The car in question was chain-driven, the differential being encased with the change- speed gears. The break was close to the inside of the off-side rear *heel, which was fitted with stlf-contained bail bearings. We first jacked up the car until the axle was about 3in. above the centre of the wheel, and then procured a piece of shaft about 2ft. Gin, long, of which one end fitted the ball races fairly well, and was turned down and screwed to take a nut for holding the wheel in place. The temporary shaft was clamped to the broken • axle by means of old Spring clips, a -strip of wood being placed be- tween the two as a distance piece to make up the depth of the collar on the axle. We then tightened the brake band on the off-side differ- ential-shaft; we could not replace the chain as the sprocket on the wheel was broken. When the foot brake: was held on it gripped the off- side differential brake drum sufficiently to keep it from revolving, and when the clutch was en- gaged the drive was taken through the differ- ential and the near-side chain. The car was driven like this for about two miles, and up a bill of about 1 in 10.—H.T. in "Motor Trac- tion."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
BY SILVER-STREET.

I have received the third map, sheet 34, from Messrs. Pearse, completing the trio comprising the whole Warrington district. Published by the Ordnance Survey, it is executed in their usual style, that is, almost perfection. It is on the one inch to one mile scale, and so the minutest details are given, even to the letter- boxes by the 'country roads. Liverpool fills the centre, Southport is its northern, and Chester its southern, boundary. It stretches westward to Holywell, and Sankey Bridges just comes in on the eastern side. Two shillings for a map like that on cloth cannot be called dear, and if anyone could afford 6d. more and get these maps cut in sections and folded, they are well worth the money. At the same time I would warn anyone who is thinking of buying the whole of , the map of England in this series that he is in for an expenditure of £l5 4s. Notwithstanding I its wonderful accuracy, I find the best road into Liverpool, and one that I have known for seven or eight years, is not indicated. It is the new road cut through a poor district from Broad Green to Edge-lane, and gives to Warring- , tonians an entrance to the great city almost without touching a tram line until Lewis's is reached. How long it will remain so favoured a route I cannct say—l am afraid they are pre- paring to spoil Edge-lane already.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
A DANGEROUS PLEASURE.

However harmless a practice it may be for a passenger to ride on the back carrier of a motor ..ycle along unfrequented country roads, it be- mines a source of danger in the busy thorough- fares of a town, not only to the two riders, but to other road-users as well. An inquest was 'ield at Wandsworth this week on a girl of 16, who was thrown from the carrier of a motor cycle which had collided with a motor lorry. The lorry was unseen by the young man who drove the cycle, as he was rounding a corner. I am glad to see the coroner roundly condemned the practice, and the jury censured the cyclist for negligence, although they acquitted him of all criminal blame.

We are in for a new German street clearer— the ding-dong bell, a craze that will be more useful and far less offensive than the creature of a few years ago that youths placed at the front wheel, and that sounded like the wail of a lost soul. The ding-dong is exceedingly simple in action, it is of good tone and very loud, and of enormous size, though not heavy. It is priced at is. Radium Lightning Conductor.

A new use for radium has been discovered by M. Szilard, whose invention was described at -a recent meeting of the Academie des Sciences. M. Szilard has found that by attaching a sort of screen treated with from one to three milli- grammes of radium to a lightning conductor the protective power of the latter is increased to an extraordinary degree, and its radius of action is multiplied vastly. Not only is the conducti- bility of the conductor improved, but the air all round is affected in such a way as to become a sort of extension of the apparatus.

John William Hogan (26), a chef, of New Bank-street, Ardwick, was on Saturday found with his throat cut at the High-street Baths, Chorlton-on-Medlock-, Manchester. He died shortly after removal to the Royal Infirmary.

The annual returns of the Lancashire Football Association, which will be submitted at the 36th annual meeting at Blackpool toKlay, show a credit balance of I:440, which is £55 less than the previous year.
