JOHN ATHERTON, PENKETH.

An Interesting Story of a Long Life.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
EIGHTY-FIVE YEARS.

We found Mr. John Atherton, of Penketh, the subject of our sketch, in wonderfully good health, better than many enjoy who are twenty years younger. Ile attributes his good health to hard work and temperate habits. Ile has lived a strenuous working man's life—a clean, sober, Christian life—and his reward is health and length of days. His long life covers an important period in the making of modern England. The year of his birth-1829—takes us back to the reign of George IV. He has thus lived in five reigns, a thing which few living to-day can say. When lie was born the famous Duke of Wellington was Prime Minister, and Daniel O'Connell was at the height of his influence in Ireland. The year of his birth saw the passing of the Catholic Emancipation Bill. Stevenson was building the Liverpool and Manchester Railway, by Newton, through Chatmoss; this was opened in 1830, and the statesman, Hutchinson, was killed at the open- ing. In the following year the first Reform Bill passed the Commons, but was thrown out in the Lords, though it was finally passed in 1832, when William IV. threatened to create peers. The next year saw the• abolition of the British slave trade, and the payment of £20,000,000 as compensation to the slave owners. In the same year the first effective Factory Act was passed, and the first grant given for Elementary Educa- tion. The National Trade Unions, as we now know them at this time, sprang into being. In 1834 the Poor Law Act was Passed, which pro- vided workhouses and did away with the efforts each parish' made to throw the responsibility of providing relief to individual cases on to its neighbour. When John was eight years of age William IV. died and Queen Victoria ascended the throne. The following year saw the rise of the Chartist Movement, also the formation of the Anti-Clrn Law Associtition; the year after Penny Postage was introduced. In 1842 Par- liament, under Sir Robert Peel, revised the Tariff downward. In 1845 there was the famine in Ireland, the result of the failure of the potato crop; and 1846 saw the repeal of the Corn Laws. The Crimean War began in 1854, and the Ameri- can Civil War (which lasted for about four years) in 1861. These were some of the stirring incidents which occurred in England during the youth and early manhood of Mr. Atherton. Naturally, as a working man, his knowledge of many of these events is very limited, except in so far as they affected the family exchequer: these are distinctly remembered. The Crimean War, for instance, with its drain on the national resources, and its effect on prices are recalled. Meat, he tells us, went up from 41d. a lb. to 10d. and is. John took little interest in these stirring events, but when the. glory and the shouting died, the poor folk had to shoulder the burden. Working men had no vote, and all John's energies were taken up in getting a living for himself, and the family of which he formed a member. But we must let him tell his own story :

of farming; she kept two and sometimes three cows that grazed on the Common. My father and I enclosed a good piece of the land off the Common and made a garden of it, and my brother-in-law, John Plumpton, lived there alter us and added more, but the landlord took it all away from the house when the Common was divided amongst the landowners. "To him that hath shall be given !" There was no com- pensation for improvements in those days.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME LIFE AND FIELD WORK.

You asked mote tell you something of my life. Well, if you think it will be of interest I will try.

I was born at Sankey Bridges, in a house in Richardson's-row, on April 9, 1829, so I am now 85 years of age,. and thank God I am still well and hearty: What schooling did I get? Not much. I did go for a time to the Old National School, in Church-street, Warrington. All attending the day school were obliged to go to church twice on Sunday—morning and after- noon. I used to take my dinner with me, gener- ally a pasty, which I ate in the churchyard, and washed it down with water from a pump near by. There was work done at home as far back as I can remember; I mean work was taken in, and my sisters and I used to make pins, or, rather, put the heads on, with the help of a treadle machine, which was hired from the pin-makers; a good deal of this was done about Warrington. There were several pin factories about, one at Atherton's Quay and one at Bank Quay. We were a family of eleven, and all had to work as soon as able. As a lad, working about 65 to/ 70 hours a week, I could earn eighteenpence. My sisters, Who were older, could earn double what I did. In homes of big families the mother turned out, as well as the father, to work on the land.

Women in hay-making time got Is. 6a. a day. Cutting oats, is. 6d. a day. Cutting corn with a sickle, women 2s. a day; men 2s. 6d.

Me and two sisters went gleaning, and also gathering potatoes after the ploughing, whieh we were then allowed to do. We brought the gleaned corn home and dried it on the hearth- stone, beat it with sticks, and mother made frumenty. Mother and sisters, for weeding in potatoes, got Is. a day, all weathers, and went out washing eight to ten hours for a shilling. Farmers allowed you to plant and • gather potatoes in-a small portion of their field simply for manuring the land, they preparing it, you setting the seed. They then got the advan- tage of the manure for their crops in the follow- ing years. We chiefly lived on bread and potatoes. Bread varied in price, according to the season's wheat crop. When 16 years old I provided my own food, and started by buying a 121 b. loaf .at 2s. 6d. These were baked in a big brown mug. A 41b. loaf cost Md. Potatoes cost 41,c1. to 6d. a score. We used black treacle instead of butter. Butter was 2s. to 25. 6d. a lb., moist sugar 10d. a lb., lump is. 2d. Tea cost ss. per lb. Herbs were used by poor folk instead of tea; these we gathered in Wales when off with the flats. They were dried, cut up, and a handful poured into the kettle and boiled. Pint bowls were used instead of cups, and the herbs sweetened with treacle; sugar was too dear. The herbs gathered for this were agri- mony, betany, and wild carrot. Beef was only about 4,1 d. a lb., till the Crimean War, when it went up suddenly to boo., and 14d. best beef- steak. Sometimes any mother used to get half a cow's head for Sunday, and these Sundays were great days. We removed from Sankey Bridges to a house in the hollow at the back of the house now called Penketh Grange. The floors were made of clay, there was no ceiling, only the bare slate whitewashed underneath. In frosty weather I have often seen icicles hanging from the slates. I've heard tell that the builder of this house, John Cowslip, enclosed a good piece of land during one day off the common land, which, according to the then custom, became his, but he had to do it in daylight and finish the enclosure in a day. In this house, on the left side of the fireplace, there was a great big brick oven. We used to gather the burnt gorse stalks from the Common to heat this oven; after it was hot the fire was drawn and the bread put in to bake. After this we lived at a house in Greystone-road. My mother did a bi;

still standing, but there was no town. Mus- pratt's was the .first big firm to commence making chemicals at Widnes. The St. Helens Canal used to go no farther than between St. Helens and Fiddler's Ferry, where the flats entered the Dlersey by a lock. In connection with this lock there was a basin or dock, which would hold a score of flats at a time, waiting for the tide. The hollow where this dock was can still be seen to the east of the present Sheep Dip Works. Formerly the canal used to take a sharp bend from about the present Fiddler's Ferry goods yard up the field to the west of the station; the low part of the field can still be seen. When the London and North-Western ; Railway was made the canal was straightened, and the hollow filled up with soil from the new stretch and the railway laid alongside. I re- member wcrking as a navvy at the straight- ening; we were paid half-a-crown a day. Where the canal bent back to the locks at the Ferry there was a landing-place, called Silver Bight, at which a large local trade was done. At the Silver Bight I have unloaded many a flat load of malt for the Brewery; that is, for what is now the Penketh Tannery. I remember bringing oak bark and unloading it at Carter's House Bridge for a tannery to the east of Widnes; this gave the name of Tanhouse- lane to the lane and station which now stand there.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
111

BECOMES A FLATMAN.

My father was a -flatman and ran a sloop, which carried goods to and from St. Helens by the canal to Runcorn, Liverpool, and North Wales. I joined him when I was fourteen, and worked almost all my life at the same kind of job. So you see I was navigating the river seventy years ago. The first flat I helped to sail was the " Heart of Oak," belonging to Captain Clare, and I believe this flat is still in existence.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SANKEY CANAL.

The only outlet for the goods from St. Helens at that time was by the Sankey Canal; there was no railway. Sankey Canal was the first of its kind in England. The Act for its building was passed in 1755; it has a fall of 60 feet in its 12 miles length. The canal was far busier then than now. I can remember as many as eight flats being loaded from one colliery in a day, holding 70 or 80 tons of coal each. We brought down all the chemicals that were made at St. Helens, and in those days we had to haul our own flats along—what you would call slavery. Where the canal now enters the river, and where Widnes (or, as it was then called, Wood- end) stands was all farm land. In addition to the farms there were a few houses, some are

===== 0002601_19140516_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
, v •

Photograph by Burrell. MR. JOHN ATHERTON.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
FIDDLER'S FERRY.

This used to boa far busier place than now. Fifty years ago we had two shipyards there, and same of the finest flats that ever 'sailed the Mersey were built by Wilkinson's at the yard nearest the Ferry Lock: The other yard, where the Sheep Dip Works now stand, was owned by John Clare, and afterwards by Hill and Grundy. The present Ferry Inn was occupied by John- sons as far back as I can remember; in fact, I believe it was in the hands of the Johnson family for over a century. There used to be a licensed ferry across the river and was used by people who wished to get to Halton and 'Run- corn along the Cheshire side. • I can remember walking along the Ferry footpath, over which there is at present such a lot of bother, eighty years ago with my father, and have used it hundreds of times since. , It has always been a public footpath in the memory of man. In those days there was a footpath right down to Woodend (Widnes) along the river used by us fiatmen.i We got ferried across at Widnes and took a packet from Runcorn to Liverpol to get to our flats, there being no railway through the Ferry then. I well remember my first railway journey from the Iron Bridge, Widnes, to St. Helens in what you would,now call a horse-box, hut which Ave used to think quite up-to-date. Coasting flats used to moor in the Perry Hole, and those that were too heavily loaded to get up to Warrington or in the canal used to tranship their cargoes for Warrington or St. Helens. I can remember when the river oppo- site Spike Island used to be full of fiats at neap tides. The water of the river was far cleaner than it is now, and was full of fish. Ido not remem- ber salmon being caught in my time, but my mother used to tell me of big quantities of these and spaxling being caught about the Ferry. THE BREWERY BECOMES THE TANNERY.

Arden's owned the Venketh Brewery and were afterwards followed by a family of Mathers. Carters looking after a cimplo of horses, week- days and Sundays, got 15s. a week, the best paid men except the foremen. The Brewery em- ployed about ten workmen, wages 10s. to 12s. a week. The brewery was bought, as you know, by the late Mr. Robert Garnett and turned into a tannery about 1881. I suppose these works now employ over two hundred men, and the wages per man are nearly three times as much as the brewers got. PENKETH VILLAGE.

When I was a lad the roofs of all the working people's houses in Penketh were thatched. The houses ecood back from the road, with gardens in front The oldest "public" was the Red

Lion. Mr. and Mrs. Davies (that is, the grand- parents of the present generation) kept a pro- vision shop for many years in an old house, before the present Sportsman Arms was built. John Gandy used to keep the principal shop, in the house now known as Riversdale, along Station-road, and we flatmen used to get our provisions there as we passed through. The first "Post Office" I remember was in Miss Garnett's cottages, and was kept by old Sally Cash.

The Cheshire Yeomanry Cavalry have been said to have used the outbuildings and the houses now in Angel-yard as stabling for their horses on their way to Liverpool. All the village would turn out to see them pass and cheer them as they went, and they were called by up lads the "Cheshire Cabbage Cutters." Why, I do not know. An old school used to stand in what was then called Meeting House-lane, now Heath-road, close to the present Quakers' Chapel. The Day Star School, at the corner of Station-road, now used as a dwelling-house, was built by old Mr. Robert Garnett over sixty years ago as a (lay school for the village children. I think Miss Gaskell was the first teacher. Afterwards the scholars were transferred to the school built by the Wesleyans about 1860.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
STAGE COACHES.

- _ When I was a lad there were a great many Stage Coaches, which went from Liverpool I think, as far as York. They changed horses at Warrington and then went on to Manchester. But these went on the Liverpool-road and did not pass through Penketh. This was the way Penketh folk got to Liverpool before the rail- ways came in.

THE COMMON OR HEATH. Heath-road was the northern boundary of the Common. It was very large; on the north side it was bounded by the stream which divides Penketh and Sankey; on the west the boundary nearly went as far as Sankey Church; on the south side the boundary went over what is now Hall Nook grounds for about half a mile, then swelled out like a shoulder of mutton and in- cluded a piece almost down to the canal; the east boundary was the brook, and ran from Penketh Grange down almost to the canal at Penketh Hall Farm. Prize-fighting used to take place on the Com- mon and on the marsh by.the river. I can well remember how us lads used to set the gorse bushes on fire on the Penketh Common so as to get old Warburton chasing us. He was a sworn constable and appointed at the Parish Meeting. There was no regular policeman in Penketh, nor, I suppose, anywhere else, in those days. There were .three large boulder stones, each weighing two or three tons, which stood forming a triangle near the present Friends' Meeting House. Us lads were told and believed that the Devil had thrown these at the Meeting House, but missed his aim, and they rolled into the ditch. They now tell me that these stones were dropped from melting icebergs. I do not know what became of these stones, but there is one very large boulder stone in the field oppo- site Greystone House, buried only a few feet, which is said to be well scored.

STOCKS. The stocks in Penketh, which gave the name to Stocks-lane, were on some waste ground about where Mr. Tunstall's joiner's shop now stands (formerly the Wesleyan Chapel, date still there, 1818), opposite the front of the present Co-opera- tive Stores. There were also stocks up in Sankey, where the present Post Office stands. I remember the side posts of these stocks as late as 1855, but the stocks had not been used for many years before this. We lads used to taunt the unfortunate people who had been put in the stocks. I've heard tell of the stocks being used for those who would not attend church, but this was before ray time.

THE PENKETH CORN MILL. A windmill used to stand along Stocks-lane, Opposite Meeting House-lane, where Miss Grice's house now stands. All the farmers round about used to get their corn ground there. This mill was kept by a man named Edmondson. [A favourite subject of George Sheffield, artist.] There was a penfold at Penketh-lane End, and on left-hand side going Warrington way, where all Strai'aniiiiids had to be taken. There were many beasts which strayed in those days, when fences were bad and cattle grazed on open com- mon land. Anybody could turn their live stock on to the Common to graze, cows, ponies, donkeys, geese, &c. Another penfold was be- yond the Old Barrow Hall. Very little remains of Old Barrow Hall; the moat is still there. The parish used to have its burley man (Burlaw man) to assess damage to gardens and crops caused by straying animals. SANKEY BRIDGES TOLL-BAR.

The toll-bar was at the Penketh side of the canal. The charges, as far as I remember them, were for broad-wheeled vehicles, such as farmers' carts, 3d., and for narrow ones, 4d., as narrow wheels were supposed to do more damage to the road. Sheep and cattle were charged so much per head; sheep went for a half-penny, and animals, like horses, cows, &c., were charged a penny per head. The toll-bar was closed about eleven o'clock at night, and any driver wanting to get through later had to rouse the toll-keeper. WESLEYAN CHAPEL. The building which was the first Wesleyan Chapel still stands opposite the Co-op. Stores, and was built in 1818. Formerly. lam told, services were held in a barn. Men and women sat on opposite sides in high-backed seats, as, I am told, was the custom in John Wesley's days. I 'remember an old tale being told of what hap- pened once when a Alissionary meeting was tak- ing place. A terrific thunderstorm came on and old Mrs. RObert Garnett, who was terrified and at her wits' end, cried out, "Pray, friends, oh, pray, pray !" I remember the storm very well. I was up at Newton with the flat and the light- ning was so vivid that I could quite easily see to pick up a pin on the cabin floor on my gat. I remember Archibald Creighton, a foreman of Mr. Garnett's, getting into the pulpit to preach, but had nothing to say when he got there. The Present Wesleyan Chapel was opened November 22nd. 1860. I have been connected with the Wesleyans all my life.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PALE-LIPPED GIRL.

Bloodlessness is the trouble of many girls who ought to be full of life and good spirits. Instead, they are pale, their lips have no colour, they have no appetite, their digestion is poor, and if they try to hurry in the street or run upstairs they are so tired and out of breath that their hearts beat as if to burst. Generally they are thin, flat-chested round-shouldered and sallow, with nothing attractive about them. If they do not get better they will have a cough every winter and presently it will be consumption. A girl should not be like this. • She should be plump, rosy, full-bosomed and full of life—able to dance and run with the best. But she must have plenty of good, pure blood for this. It is only through blood that we can feed any part of the body—nerves flesh, brain ,or spine. And the only medicine which will make blood is Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. Their effect upon pale- lipped, flat-chested girls is wonderful. The appetite improves, backaches and headaches no longer give trouble, and every man is attracted. In a few days the improvement begins to be apparent, and the anemic girl often becomes the belle of the family.

Your dealer can sell you Dr. Williams Pink Pills for Pale People; if not, send 2s. 9d. for one box (or 13s. 9d for six bops) to Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., 46, Holborn-viaduct, London—no charge for postage. A free book, "The Blood to the Book Dept., 46, Holborn-viaduct, London.

THE GREAT BUDGET.

Bills Necessary to Give it Full

Effect.

Mr. Lloyd George has explained that the only Bills made necessary by the Budget are the Finance Bill, a Revenue Bill, a Valuation Bill, and an Insurance Act Amendment Bill. An Education Bill will not be needed in order to increase the grant. The Government will in- sist on an equitable valuation of property sub- ject to local taxation, differentiating between improvements and site value, as a part of the scheme of relief distribution to local authori- ties. It is the Government view that the valua- tion will be sufficiently advanced to enable the money to be distributed on the basis of (1) relief for improvements, (2) no relief for site values, for the second half of 1915. In the meantime provisional arrangements will be made.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SPIRITUAL HEALING.

New Cult of Laying on Hands for Clergymen and Pastors.

ALMOST MAGICAL CURES.

The gradual growth in our midst of a strong belief in the power of spiritual healing was manifested on Tuesday night at a meeting held at Toft's Assembly Rooms, Bridge-street. This meeting, which had been called chiefly through the instrumentality of the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, had for its object the consideration of the advisability of forming a local branch of the Guild of Health. There was a good attend- ance, and many prominent local clergymen were present.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPPORTED BY THE BIBLE.

The RECTOR (the Rev. Canon Willis), who occupied the chair, drew attention to three passages in the New Testament. In St. Mark 6th and the Epistle of St. James we read of the Apostles "administering holy unction," and in St. Mark 16th, we were told that "these signs shall follow them that believe; in My name shall they cast out devils; they shall lay hands on the sick, and they shall recover." Ihit we had not only Scriptural authority. The Prayer Book plainly sanctioned the anointing of the sick with oil.

Referring to the recently issued report of the Committee of Investigation appointed in 1910, it was stated therein that divine power was only limited by the divine will, and that there should be recognition of medical and spiritual ministration. It must, however, be noted that distinction was drawn between functional and organic diseases.

Magical Cures -

The Rector referred to the experiences of his friend, Bishop Mylne, and his son. The former was cured, after the administration of unction by a priest, from a disease resembling cander, which the doctor had declared incurable. The Bishop's son was cured from lockjaw, brought on by a blow received whilst playing hockey. Discussing the subject in a letter to the speaker, Bishop- Mylne had expressed his belief that the laying on of hands was still bestowed as a purely personal gift, "the Spirit blowing where He listetli."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
A FACT FOR ALL.

• In the course of a long and convincing speech, the Rev. A. M. MITCHELL referred to the objects of the Guild of Health, of which there were four:

_ (1) The study of the influence of spiritual upon physical well-being. (2) The exercise of healing by spiritual means, in complete loyalty to scientific prin- ciples and methods. (3) United prayer for the inspiration of the Holy Spirit in all efforts to heal the sick. (4) The cultivation, through spiritual means, of both individual and corporate health. Spiritual healing ought to be a fact for all the Christian Churches. The Guild of Health claimed that healing power had been meted out to the Church of England, and, he thought, to all Christian Churches. The claim was no new one. The power to withstand had not left them. but unfortunately, Christians had neglected their power. in favour of materialism.

Faith Healing. The Church would fail in her plainest duty if she did not lift up the standard of faith healing. The many different sects swhich had gradually arisen in connection with the theory showed that there was truth in it. The Guild of Health did not desire to oppose the medical profession, but to assist it, The power was not restricted to the clergy; it was, perhaps, given more to the laity than the clergy. Many instances of healing had occurred, and he contended that whether these were exceptional cases or not, they nevertheless proved that tile power of heal- ing was still in the Christian Church. The report showed that spiritual bealing vas no longer to be regarded as .a fad of cranks. (Hear, hear.) It limited the healing to organic diseases. One Who possessedihe gift might per- form on his own Wily. and more possessed it

than knew what had been entrusted to them. Distinction should be drawn between the anointing with oil and the extreme unction of the Church of Rome, because the former was an anointing for life, and the latter cn . for death. The speaker concluded by expressing the hope that one result 4 the recent report would be that the medical profession would give a heartier welcome to the ministrations of the clergy in the sick room than it had done of recent years. (Hear, hear, and applause.) At the request of the audience, Mr. Mitchell read his recent letter to "The Examiner," after which the meeting was thrown open for discus- sion. •

===== 0002601_19140516_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THOSE OF THE LARGES

CONCESSIONS ON SMALL INCOMES.

In response to an appeal for consideration to the smaller unearned incomes, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, while rightly refusing to accept the endeavour to establish an identity between earned and unearned incomes, has made some important concessions. Unearned incomes between £3OO and £5OO will continue to pay at the rate of is. 2d. in the X, instead of Is. 4d. Incomes of £3OO and under will only pay Is. instead of Is. 2d. Consideration is also prom- ised in regard to the settlement estate duty, in cases where a man by will gave life interest to his wife and afterwards to his children. In passing, the ridiculous claim of the Opposition may be noted that when the Chancellor of the Exchequer, in fixing the death duties, favoured settled estates, there was a contract established with this specially-favoured class. Mr. Lloyd George was using very mild language when he declared that this was an indefensible position, and a most dangerous argument for any Chan- cellor of the Exchequer to admit.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
NO ANTAGONISM TO DOCTORS.

The Rev. W. BRACECAMP, in expressing his belief in,spiritual healing, was glad it had been made clear that they did not desire to work in antagonism to the medical profession. What the sick should do was to call in the best doctor they could, follow his advice, and then ask the Great Physician to heal them. Faith was the chief thing necessary, and no good could be done without it. He had of late tried to be more practical in his prayers, with wonderful results.

' "NOT GOD'S WILL."

Mrs. EDELSTEN declared that she had come to the conclusion that disease and illness were not "God's will," as many believed them to be. God did not want them in the world any more than he did sin. The Rev. G. VALE OWEN said all who had any faith in our Lord and His Word would sup- port the Guild. However, the question was not a clergy one, and he would like to hear the views of the laity. Mr. W. E. STOUT said experience taught him that all who were in sound health and lived a clean life possessed what he termed "magnetic" power. This was directed by the Spirit of God through the agency of some of His ministering spirits.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
PERSONAL EXPERIENCE.

The Rev. T. VICARS could testify from personal experience. Their chief task was to teach the people that nothing was impossible to those who believed.

Mr. W. H. SHERHIIIIN believed that spirit was the absolute master of matter. With reference to the doctors, he did not agree that their work would not be attacked, and they might as well confers at the beginning that a great deal of the medical trade would be spoiled. Those who joined the Guild of Health would have to stand a good deal of ridicule, but that did not matter. He thought it unfair that hOlders of office in the Guild had to he 'communicants. (Hear, hear.) At this point Mr. MITCHELL pointed out that the rule would probably be abolished before long.

"NARROW-MINDEDNESS." Several speakers agreed as to the narrow- mindedness of the rule, and therefore the 'Rev. W. BRACECAMP proposed that a separate society should be formed for the time being, so that those of any denomination could hold office. Mr. SHERBURN seconded. Miss TAYLOR proposed that a branch of the Guild should be formed. If this was not done, they would lose the support of all the other branches of the Guild. Mr. MiTcnEcr, seconded. A vote was accordingly taken, seven support- ing the latter proposition and five the former. Ultimately, in view of the smallness of the figures, it was suggested by the RECTOR that definite action should be postponed until a larger meeting could be called, to which noted speakers should be invited, and that Mr. Mitchell should have charge of all the arrange- ments in the meantime.

ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

l'he Anniversary Services. Great success attended the anniversary ser- vices held at St. John's Presbyterian-I.:laurel' on Sunday. The preacher, morning and evening, was toe Rev'. Alexander Wylie Blue, of St. George's, bunderlaud, with whom the Rev. Wm. Reid, the popular-pastor of St. John's, worked iu conjunction at Sunderland, previous to his call to Warrington. There were satisfactory .atteudances at both diets of worship, the churcu in-the evening being crowded, extra chairs hav- ing to Ile placed after every available seat had been occupied.

The eloquent and impressive discourses of Mr. Blue, who is a gentleman ,of striking presence and personality, were greatly appreciated by ,those who were privileged to hear them, and should the reverend pastor again visit St. John's many will gladly gather to hear him. Mr. Blue's subject of discourse in the forenoon was Peter's denial of our Lord, and his treat- ment of the theme was such as could not have failed to have been helpful to all who were present. In the evening Mr. Blue preached on the vision of Ezekiel, as narrated in the first chapter of the book of that prophet. The preacher pointed out that Ezekiel's pic- ture was a portrait of the ideal character. What made the Bible a book for human needs was that it was a picture gallery, or, it might be described as a volume of biographies. It found men and discovered them to themselves. It "described men and women of like passions to themselves; they could be put into the twentieth century and recognised now. The portrait which Ezekiel drew was very like the ideal image which they sometimes had looked upon and wished to see realised in their own lives. The living creatures had the face of a man, of a lion, of an ox and of an eagle. There was the face of a man, and the message that was to reach men must reach them on the 'lnman plane, must speak in the lan- guage of the people. The face of the lion re- minded them that there was a fighting element in their livOs, and religion had to do with that. They had to combat the forces of Satan wher- ever they were. The face of the cox was the sacrificial face. They must serve and sacrifice, spend and be spent. There was the face of the eagle, which spoke of soaring. To see the world clearly it was necessary to look down on it from a great height, and the most practical of all men was the one most given to prayer. Where the spirit went the living creatures went. The ideal life was that lived under the dominance of God's Spirit. At the end of the chapter was the description of the appearance of brightness. , Ezekiel was hinting at the presiding God, but could not draw the portrait of Him eye hath not seen. It meant that life of the perfect man [ with the likeness of a man, a lion, an ox and an eagle, was under the dominance of God, who made it, guided it and directed it. Special hymns were sung, and the choir, under the leadership of Mr. A. G. LewseY, rendered the anthem " Seek ye the Lord" (Roberts), the solo being given by Mr. J. Aitken. Mr. J. H. Owen was at the organ.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
"GROWTH OF A -CHILD."

The Rev. 11. Ogden's Striking Sermon. The Rev. H. Ogden preached at the Golborne- street Baptist Church on Sunday, on the sub- ject "The Physical, Mental and Spiritual Growth of a Child," taking for his text 1 Samuel ii. 28 "And the child Samuel grew on, and was in favour both with the Lord, and also with men."

The preacher said that the reading of the Bible narrative reveals one salient fact, which is that character-building has a way of eluding our methods. The two sons of Eli had been rejected of the Lord because of their wickedness and their iniquity. Eli, their father, was the high priest and a leader of religious worship. The problem is: How came such sons from such a father? As we have said, character-building has a way of eluding our established methods, and betimes the son of the manse is the scape- goat of the village. He is the author of all the village unrest and mischief, and there is no peace until he is shipped across the seas. M,ppily, however, in the training of children, general rules can be laid down which generally are effective. The exception, of course, will always.reraain a puzzle to us. There are three phases of the child's life that we should con- sider, the physical, the moral and the spiritual. Paul speaks of the body as the temple of God. Our ancestors treated the body as though it were an encumbrance, as though it were to be constantly mortified, and as though any state were good enough if only the destiny of the soul was secure. , • Professional missionaries pointed the way to Heaven, and all the time commerce was rattling us into barbarism. Women were reared as cattle, men grouped as herds, children clothed in dirt—because religion had forgotten that the body is the temple of God. Our great cotton- spinning centres boast of their proficiency in cotton-spinning, and boast of their immense wealth; yet statistics reveal that there are more ricketty children in those towns than in any towns of equal population in the British Isles. Commerce, as well as religion, had for- gotten that the body is the temple of God.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Plums of the Empire.

The preacher said he was struck by the aver- age height; breadth, weight and appearance of the Eton boys, All the plum aof the Empire were/destined for them. Physically and men- tally they were being equipped for consuls and rulers. Standing a little later at the factory gates of a great mill, the preacher was struck by the difference between the sons of Eton and the sons of a spinning mill. Height, breadth, weight and appearance were all wanting, and the prospect of these boys and i,ouths was "too old at forty !" It is surely patent that what is good for Eton can not be bad for a mill boy; that if a duke's son and the son of a millionaire must have these physical advantages, it could not be other than excellent for the son of a peasant or the son of a ploughman. The phy- sical does come. Sunday school teachers should follow their boys to the cricket fields, follow them to the football pitches, share with them the games of the institute; they might lend a hand to the building up of the body, which is a temple of God. The child develops mentally in his own pecu- liar way. He grows mentally by interroga- tions. That interesting little book, "Wee Macgreegor," is not only a book of fun, but the revelation of a boy's mind. AU his con- versation is in questions. He embarrasses father, mother, grandfather, and all his rela- tives by his perpetual queries. A learned bar- rister said, when a witness would persist in talking, "Let the witness talk. He will tell us , everything !". The man was a child in legal ways, or otherwise he would have been re- strained. Let the child talk, and he will tell us just what he is, revealing to us the way in which to deal with him. We have happily passed the day when the maxim followed in relation to all children was that they should be seen and not heard. The Children's Day, -

To-day is the children's day. We see an we hear, and, if we are wise, we learn. When a youth reaches adolescence, he certainly needs wise treatment. Probably he doubts everything his father believes, and believes everything his father doubts. Stanley Houghton emphasises that in his "Younger Generation." Stern re- pression is the guiltiest of folly. A vicar attempted to repress Bradlaugh's doubts, and succeeded in producing the most eloquent agnostic of his day. Young colts in time make steady horses, and the frisking lamb you will not know in twelvd° months. The republican mayor of Birmingham in later life becomes the leader of the aristocratic party. Have patienCe and tact with an honest youth, who is trying to satisfy the honest queries of his own mind! The boy is also a spiritual unit, though his spirituality is covered with the dust of his earthly being. A genius may forsake his din-

ner for his books, but the horizon of a hungry boy is bounded by the range of a dinner plate.

Nostrils v. Soul.

His nostrils arwe more alert than his soul, and would you have it otherwise? All life lies before him, and you cannot feed a boy from the bread of Heaven if his stomach is a vacuum. Our teaching on spiritual matters to a boy should be correlative to the present facts of the boy's life. What man in his senses would invite a class of young animals, such as robust boys are, to sing that hymn in Sankey, "I would like to die, said Willie, if my papa could die, too"? There may be very odd moments when a boy wants to die, but they are very odd. He wants, and ought to want, to live. He loves stories of adventure, pioneering and bravery. They are the stories he ought to love. They 'describe the deeds he would emulate, and unto the teacher is given the opportunity of giving a divine meaning to bravery and pioneering. Yes; correlate the life of the boy to the life he ought to live and to follow, and make clear to him that in doing right, acting justly, serv- ing God and trusting Christ, there is a happy land not only far, far away, but there is a happy land here and now.

LADIES CONDUCT P.S.A.

The open meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. Society, which was held on Sunday in the. Park Picture Palace, was entirely conducted by ladies. Mrs. Whitmore, who presided, spoke of the advantages to be derived from the Brotherhood and Sisterhood meetings. Mrs. Moore then addressed the meeting, basing her remarks upon the two conversions contained in Acts xvi. 14-31. From these she drew many useful lessons.

The musical part of the service was greatly appreciated. Miss C. Hastings sang "How lovely are Thy dwellings" in pleasing manner, after which Miss 31. ===== 0002601_19140516_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The Rev. T. T. James presided over a good meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sun- day. Mr. Gould, who gave an address upon "Courage," said that of the many sides there were to his subject, he wished to touch upon three. There was animal courage, of which not only the animals possessed a great deal, but men also. This could be developed for good or evil. Again, there was heroic courage, which all who occupied great positions must possess. Times came when we were called upon to show our heroic lives before others, often in the form of self-sacrifice for those near and dear to us. But most important of all was Christ-like courage, for that exerted an influence upon those around us. By living Christ-like lives and denying ourselves for the 'good of others we might do untold good.

In well-chosen words the Chairman referred to the death of the .Rev. Charles Silvester Horne, who had passed away so suddenly during the past week. His life had been an exemplary one, and his sound teaching would be missed, not only by the Christian Church and the Brotherhood movements, but also by those who participated, in the political and social life of the nation.

Upon Mr. James moving that letters of sym- pathy should be sent to Mrs. Horne and family, and the National Federation, the brethren assented by rising in their seats. Mr. B. Farrar, the secretary, added his tri- bute) The late President of the movement, he said, had lived a heroic life. Rest he would not take, but laboured for his Master until he quietly passed away to receive his reward. He was a man that could ill be spared:

During the afternoon Mr. J Poole sang "0 remember, Almighty One" and "A Dream of Paradise" in good style. Favourite hymns of the late President were also rendered. Mr. Payton presided at the organ. The collection to be taken at next Sunday's' open meeting will be in aid of the National Institute for the Blind.

ADULT SCHOOLS.

Opening of the Ramble Season. _

The. first OF a series of nature study and anti- quarian rambles was held on Saturday after- noon. The place of interest for the day was Bewsey Old ,Hall. Over a hundred members and friends met on the lawn to listen to Mr. Charles Madeley give a most interesting account of the history of the building. Mr. Madeley said that for three centuries it was the residence of the Botelers, lords of Warrington; being sold with the rest of thelr estates in 1587. There are no. remains of the Botelers' house, the present building being a fragment only of the house erected by Sir Thomas Ireland about the year 1600. Large additions were made in the eigh- teenth century, and pulled down again by the present owners (the family of Powys) about a hundred years ago. The great event in the his- tory of Bewsey Hall was the tragedy of the murder of Sir John Boteler in 1430. The con- flicting versions of the stoiy extant in oral tra- dition in an Elizabethan ballad and in modern literature made it very ' difficult to present a consecutive account, or to form an opinion as to the date or the cause of the murder, or who were the persons really concerned. At the close votes of thanks to Mr. Madeley for his lecture, and to Mr. and Mrs. J. W. Johnston for their kind permission to visit the hall, were proposed by Mr. Harold Gray, of the Bewsey School, and seconded by Mr. Frank Rennie, secretary of the Workers' Education Association, and heartily responded to. LONGFORD SCHOOL. - -

On Saturday evening the members of this new school held a " house-warming ", social in their new premises in West-street, under the chairmanship of Dr. McKee. After a substan- tial tea, provided by the Women's School, the presentation of a clock 'took place. A short address on "Adult School Aims" .was given by Mr. D. P. Boyd. of Birmingham, one of the organising secretaries of the National Council. Afterwards a characteristic free-atid-easy Adult School concert was held, in which the famous 1 " German" Band from Bewsey School provided 1 an abundance of mirth, not to speak of Mr. John Smith's "Polly-Wolly Doodle" and others. A most enjoyable evening was spent. Mr. John A. Ransome and the presidents of the other Warrington Schools showed their well wishes' by being present. I ACADEMY-STREET MEN'S.

A most interesting and instructive address was given by Mr. J. King upon "Clay, glass and metals" on Sunday morning. He prefaced his remarks by giving a lucid historical retrospect of the introduction of the manufacture of pot- tery. This class of work was first found out by the Pluenicians, and the fine quality of their work was probably due to the pure quality of the raw material to be found in their native land, Many people argued that the Romans were the first to introduee pottery ware, but the speaker .gave abundant proof that they were not the original inventors. The manufacture of the various kinds of china, glass and other articles were explained, from the excavating of the clay to the finished article. Japan produced the clearest china, but it is' not of the most ser- viceable kind, it being very brittle. If put into very hot water it would break. It was clearly demonstrated that our own country stood fore- most, as producers of excellent china, excelling the Japanese because of its stronger 'and more durable quality. Glass and its manufacture was also explicitly explained.

MYSTERIOUS STOMACH TROUBLES.

PRINCIPAL CAUSE REVEALED.

A great deal of mistery is made about stomach troubles, and, many people go in con- stant dread of life-long incapacity or the hor- rors of the operating table just because they ex- perience pain in the region of the stomach for which they cannot account. Instead of looking for the cause of the trouble, they seem only too ready to describe themselves as "martyrs to in- digestion" or "chronic dyspeptics." Excessive acidity is said to be the direct cause of more than 90 per, cent. Of all stomach troubles, and, according to many eminent physi- cians, the occasional use of a reliable antacid, such as ' half a teaspoonful of pure BISURATED magnesia in a little water after meals, will do more towards righting stomach derangements than all the patent foods and medicines ever in- vented, because this gets at the root of the trouble—stops fermentation of the food con- tents. and neutralises the acids which otherwise , would irritate and inflame the stomach.

The proceedings of the Synod proper com- menced in Bold-street Church on Tuesday and closed on Thursday. Most of the sessions were held in private. The Chairman of the Synod was the Rev. THE SYNOD'S DOINGS.

Slight Membership Decrease. ,

The Synod met in 80ld-street Chapel on Tues- day and the Rev. Isaiah Parker, of Waterloo, Liverpool, presided. Ile was supported on the platform by the Revs. Dr. Simon and T. G. Hartley. Deep sympathy was expressed with the Chairman in consequence of family afflic- tion and also with other members of the Synod who were absent..

_ Six ministers have died since last August, the Revs. Andrew B. Holliday (Waterloo) and Walter F. Mayer (Birkenhead) being in circuit Ripon, Joseph Newton, RWilliam Brooks, who had retiredwbr keafniCrdoimiti ihPceirßcauenivdts..T of each was read, followed by personal testi- monies. A letter of condolence will be sent to each Widow. The following ministers obtained permission to become supernumeraries at the approaching conference: The Revs. John Dairies (Runcorn), E. Morgan (Warrington), and A. Reeve (Run- corn). The total adult church membership is 24,764, net decrease 94 (this, however, is only the third decrease in the last ten years, and the average increase in membership for the same period is nearly 200); on trial 1,887, net decrease 195; junior members, 5,534. The new members fully received during the year numbered 1,834, 1,126 ceased to be members; in men's brotherhoods, &c., there were 549 and in sisterhoods 749. There were 5,975 members of Wesley Guilds, and 1,646 in other young people's societies. There are 162 declared Wesleyans in the Army and Navy in the eight military centres in the district. Ministerial probationers must take an annual examination and furnish a list of books they have read from an official list. The examiners' , reports were declared satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFICIAL SERMON.

The Rev. John S. Simon (an ex-president of the conference) preached the official sermon to a large congregation in Bold-street Church on Tuesday evening, and took for his text, "Behold, I stand at the door and knock. If any man hear My voice and open the door I will come in to him, and will sup with him, and he with Me" (Rev. iii. 20). He said it was only when some man, sensitive and sympathetic, heard the voice he knew, and opened the door that the Saviour came into His own church, and if lie could read the signs of the times he thought that he could say that was the position of Christ in reference to many churches to-day. The first cause which kept Christ outside the door of the churches was a luke-warm church, the church neither hot nor cold, wonderfully balanced and wonder- fully alocif from all extremes of emotion; a church without enthusiasm, a church that had lost its nerve and intensity. The passionate churches were the churches where "The Man of Sorrows" came to dwell; it was the worn-out and weary churches, with no intensity, that kept the Master outside. Where was their passion,

enthusiasm, intensity? He felt very much re- buked some years ago when the Rev. Dr. Dale, preaching in one of their Methodist churches, said: "I like to come and warm my hands with Methodist fire." He (the preacher) bowed his head and was humbled. It must indeed be very hard work for a man to warm his hands or his heart at the fire of some of the Methodist churches, and they must not suppose for a moment that they 'could carry on their evangel either in this or in foreign countries unless the old fire burned with an intense radiance in their hearts. It could not be done with the luke-warm churches composed of individuals who said they must have a liberal and charit- able outlook upon the facts of everyday life, the churches that tempered down their enthu- siasm until the world could find no fault with them. Did they think that they in that church could escape the criticism of the world. As soon as the world ceased to criticise them they were wrong. He welcomed the attack of the world upon the church, for it showed they were stand- ing for something. A church self-satisfied showed an absent Christ. To go to a service again and again and not hear a single word about Christ would account for Christ standing out in the darkness. If they were cursed with self-satisfaction they would never do anything in any department. Like the thinker and the reader, the preacher could never be satisfied. The blight of the preacher was when he consid- ered he had thought quite enough about the Christian truth. They had all to keep their minds fresh and be hungry for the knowledge of the truth. There was one man who could let Christ into that congregation. He did not care how humble he was; he could be the opener of the churches throughout the country and the world. A re- vival was coming, but it would not be brought about by a mass movement, but by some one man. Everything depended upon the heart as to the presence of Jesus Christ. There were two things the world must do before it .gained the organ of sight—it must love and obey, and when people were trained by love, a passionate love for Jesus Christ, He would come in. It would transform the interior of their Methodist churches, and their experience, if for one day every member would love and obey Jesus—if he had just one great day of consecration—and the gladsome cry of the churches would be, "Lift up your heads 0 ye gates, lift up ye everlasting doors," and the Lord would come in. "God send us that experience," concluded the preacher.

CHILDREN'S MORALS.

_ The Rev. R. F. Broomfield moved a 'resolu- tion, which was carried, expressing much con- cern at the facts revealed in the reports of the police at Liverpool and Birkenhead, proving, the low moral standard and criminality of chil- dren and young people, together with' serious criticisms of their parents. These facts re- vealed a condition of things that demanded the careful attention of the churches, and the reso- lution commended the consideration of the sub- jet to the earnest attention of the district Sun- day School Council, with the object of arrest- ing and preventing the evil. It was reported that the district Sunday schools numbered 332, with 6,857 officers and teachers and 56,158 scholars. The average at- tendance was 37,925. The Sunday school de- crease, especially on Sunday mornings, was much regretted, but there were Most encourag- ing Bible classes connected with the Boys' Bri- gades and similar movements. The need of im- proved parental control was emphasised. The temperance societies had a membership of 5,325, the Band of Hope 15,969, and the Abstainers' League 9,785. It was reported that the income for the year was ~€10,213, an increase of £260. In addition to this, the district contributed ..e21,843 to the Centenary Fund.

LOCAL CANDIDATE FOR MINISTRY.

The Synod closed on Thursday with thanks to local friends. The Rev. Isaiah Parker pre- sided, and three ministerial probationers were recommended to the Conference for final accept- ance and ordination. ' - The examination of thirteen carklidates for the ministry occupied several hours, and amongst those recommended for acceptance by the Conference was Mr. Peter Woods (cashier), 14, Kimberley-drive, Stockton Heath. Mr. Woods is nearly 24 years of age, and has been a local preacher for two and a half years on the full plan. At the quarterly meeting there was a unanimous vote on his candidature. The last public meeting of the week took place at the Bewsey-road Church on Thursday even- ing. Mr. George Weaver occupied the chair, and addresses were delivered by the Revs. A. Leathley Heap and E. J. Gawne. The Chairman said that meeting concluded the Synod, and he hoped it had been helpful to their church life; that every churchman and layman would go back to his work refreshed and encouraged. There never was a time when it was so necessary for them to apply their lives as individual units in their worship. (Applause.) The Revs. A. Leathley Heap and E. J. Gawne addressed the meeting. •

===== 0002601_19140516_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FAMILY PEW. Should It Be Filled With Drunkards?

A well-attended young people's meeting was held in Bewsey-road Church on Monday even- ing, when the chair was occupied by Mr. H. H. Posnett, of Runcorn. The Revs. Allen Holt, Trevor Davies and P. Warburton Lewis were present. The Chairman briefly introduced the first speaker, and the Rev. Allen Holt said they were there to appropriate some truth and use it, in the world, in order to transform the everyday surroundings of life. They were young people belonging to the Methodist Church, and as young people they must rediscover the religion of joy that made them glad and happy. It was his privilege to represent a church of young people with over 2,000 scholars, and he hoped there would come a new conscious realisation of the gospel of a great joy. They could save England if they were joyful. Many young people criticised the Bible more than they read it, but they should all remember the Bible was the book of life. The must re- discover the value of the individual soul. Were they desperately anxious about the saving of man? To-day the drunkard•was often outside the beautiful sanctuary. He would like to see his family pew occupied by the six biggest drunkards in the district, and then he would think the family pew was being used better than ever before. They had to fight against selfish- ness and the forces of the liquor traffic. Even the moderate Sunday Closing Bill could not pass through the House of Commons because the brewers whipped up their forces. They must capture the men and women and rediscover the possibility of a great world advance. They were only saved by consecration and red-hot enthusiasm. The Kingdom of God must establish itself in the midst of the hosts of evil. They must realise the power of personal experi- ence. They would not be .saved by conventions or committees, but by consecrations; every man and woman realising the fulness of the saving power of the Man of Sorrows.

INTENTION OF RELIGION. The Rev. Trevor, Davies dealt with "Loyalty to Jesus Christ in relation to the. Church," and said the only creative force in the church was to be loyal to Christ. As soon as they substituted anything for that they did a great deal of mis- chief to the church they were trying to benefit.

He did not trouble about the decrease in mem- bership. He was troubled more about the in- tention of religion than of its extension. He would rather increase 100 fold the religion of one person than add 100 names to the book of membership. Nothing could create a church except loyalty and devotion to Jesus Christ. Wherever they had a ,company of people meet- ing together in faith, love and service of Christ, they had a church. He could not do with the people who belonged to all churches. They were too broad to belong to any church in particu- • lar.

He had never found any help from them in his own church, and he.could assure them that it was no bigotry to be devoted to their own church. The man who .was loyal to his own church usually saw more clearly the best in other churches. They must feel they belonged lo a great church, for they had nothing of which to be ashamed in the tradition and influence of Methodism. Ile believed in High Churchmen, but the pity was that they were not high enough in their churchmanship. They could not formu- late any conception of churchmanship high enough to satisfy. Why, he asked, should loyalty to Christ lead men to association with the church? Why did they protest against Tol- stoy's dictum that there was a difference be- tween Christianity and Churchianity? The first reason was that it was a magnificent opportu- nity of confessing Christ. Religion outside churches was like a painting seen in the gallery in the twilight. The church shed the light on the painting. He had found the church an im- perfect witness. There were many failures and imperfections, but nevertheless it was the great- est witness which Christ had. Lord Morley was right when he said that for 1,900 years the spiritual life of Europe had tome from the flame kindled by the sublime mystic of the Galilean hills; but they must not forget that the only witness had been the church. If they did not confess their religion they would have no religion to confess. The church in the coming days was to be made . the only guardian of Christian ethics.

Sunday Taken Away. After referring to the bearing the New Testa- ment had in connection with laws on the Statute Book of this country, the speaker said that Sun- day had now been taken from them. It was being taken away from them by the municipali- ties for the pleasure-seeker. The day would come when nationalities and municipalities would be as cynically indifferent concerning the day of rest as they were concerning the question of temperance; as careless about the sanctity of marriage as they were about inter- national brotherhood. Then there would be only one institution left to safeguard those things, and that was the church. If in the com- ing days the advocates of the new morality had their way, and they were led to leave the plat- form they had occupied for the benefit of the people for so many years, then it would be in- cumbent upon the church to lay a still higher obligation on its members. The one institution that could ethically save the country would be the Church of Christ. They should do all they could to be loyal to Christ and make the church effective.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
AT 21 SYNODS.

The Rev. F. W. Lewis mentioned that he had attended 21 May Synods, and was Present at one in Warrington 20 years ago. He dealt with the subject " Christ and His Church," and said that as far as he could see, nothing else mattered except the Church of Christ. All the rest had its meaning only because it was related to that church. If this age was to be saved, it would be saved by Christ and His Church. An imperfect church led to imperfect work, and if they were to be men and women of Christ they must understand His mind and have His spirit. On the proposition of the Rev. W. A. Beckett, the chairman and speakers were heartily thanked.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH AND COMMUNITY. Mr. Parker Asks That Conflicting Ideas of Responsibility be Faced.

"No church can hope to live and grow that is

not resolutely striving to extend the Kingdom of Heaven over all the affairs of the life of men" declared Mr. C. E. Parker, who presided over an inspiring public meeting, held in Bewsey- road Wesleyan Church on Wednesday evening. There was a large assembly, and the speakers were the Revs. S. E. Keeble, W. E. Beet and Howard N. Burton.

MR. C. E. PARKER'S SPEECH. The Chairman said that on such an occasion he might appropriately refer to the fact that in the eighteenth century the effect of the Methodist Revival did a great deal to change for good the moral and spiritual condition of our country. He might tell them of the enormous growth of the Methodist churches in this and other lands. He might urge that as a denomina- tion they had been overfed with teaching, and that in consequence many of their people were suffering from fatty degeneration of the soul. Instead of dwelling on these things, which might be taken as read, he would put a practical question to himself and to them. • Is the Church Fulfilling the Purposes of its _

Being? Is the Methodist Church, and specially our local branches of it—are they to-day, here and now, fulfilling the purposes of their being? • Do we, asked Mr. Parker, in Warrington at the present moment really believe the thought suggested by the words in one of our hymns, "Thine is the forge, the loom, the mart"? Do we claim these things for Him? Do we really believe that Christ's Kingdom is intended to embrace all the affairs of man, such as employ- ment, wages, the houses the poor live in, the air they breathe, our recreations, our public-houses, our Town Councils, our Parliament? Let us ask ourselves to what appreciable extent are the Wesleyan Churches influencing the life which is represented by the forge, the loom, the market-plate of this town. Are we claiming for Christ His rightful Lordship over these things? In regard to the thought, the machinery, and the activities of. the Church, there was no ade- quate appreciation of the second of the two great commandments, our duty to, and our love for, our neighbour. What was done was of an irregular, spasmodic, badly conceived, and badly executed character. There ought to be at the very heart of their church life an organisa- tion for directing Christian service. He in- cluded "social" service, but the word (as gener- ally understood) was inadequate to express his meaning of Christian service, which included all forms of service. Now the recognition of .this obligation which ought to rest on the Wes- leyan Church, was at present discharged by organisations which were more or less on the outskirts of their church life. They had, for example, the Wesleyan Methodist Union for Social Service. The lack of interest which the Methodist Church takes in this organisation was an indication that there is something sadly wrong in the conception which the church enter- tains of its duty. There were also temperance organisations, the Wesley Guilds, some of which but only a few recognised in a practical way their obligation to the outside world. Such organisations were recognised by,.their Church, but were only sectional in their character. Their existence or non-existence in a circuit was all a matter of chance, and their vitality depended on varying surrounding ciraam. stances; they occupied a precarious position t the Circumference of their church life a contended that this obligation of Christiant liser- vice should not be left t 3 be recogniseric)ltisioei position church ignored, 0 organisation, should occupy ccty shoulda bcentral tr. o be control of the circuit ministers in charge, and of term n "poor should be the leaders' meeting. Further, the present psi. tion and duties of "poor stestewards"ild Chris- tian stewards,",enlarged, andsu ch instead officers of i u outside world Stewards. B this lis oli method the ol,ligaSteiorvicnoef the church toy t would become one of the prin cipa , if net the principal, duty of the church. Conflicting 'ldeas Lately, said Mr. Parker, I put these ideas into letters to the Methodist papers. I might as well have been a Prophet of Baal f ,or" There

was neither voice nor any to answer nor any that regarded." At Birmingham I put my con- tention before a Wesleyan meeting, and one of the Wesleyan ministers present expressed what seems to me to represent the prevailing con- nexional thought, both ministerial an! lay. He thought in the main I was wrong, and that the State, the municipality, and secular voluntary effort should discharge the obligation in regard to the things I mentioned.

These two conflicting ideas indicate the issue ,would like you to face. Which of the two lines of thought are to determine the church's future action? This minister's idea is the prev- alent one in many churches. And in such churches it is having this effect: Those who have active Christian sympathy and executive ability, the virile workers, are being gradually drawn away from Christian service under the direction of the church, and their energies are being absorbed in, say, the Labour movement, in Guilds of Help, and the like. This is having the effect of creating a condition in our churches by which more than ever before they are becom- ing composed of those who—without any dis- respect—l would call the inefficients, and—may I not add?—the idle. Perhaps I might put the matter in a nicer, but not so accurate a form, by saying that the Marthas are leaving us and the Marys are being left. There are no doubt some branches of services to the community which the State and the municipality can only adequately discharge, but outside these there is a very wide field for the services of the churches to the community, and especially to the individuals, and I contend that snip service inspired and directed by Chris- flan love is immensely superior to the service given through officialdom.

Not a Nursery for Coddling. But the main question is—and I come back to it—what obligation has our Master and our King laid upon His Church? Surely His King- dom cannot be established by organisations, which have alloWed themselves to be hidden away out of the main stream of the people's life, sheltered, and becalmed in quiet backwaters. No church can hope to live and grow that is not resolutely striving to extend the Kingdom of Heaven over all the affairs of the life of men. I know all about the difficulties and dangers of a more active, more extended service, but the church, if it is to be worthy of its founder, must be prepared to face all dangers, dangers that will come both from within and without; it must strive to fulfil the purpose of its King, and at all costs. And it is time the Methodist Church ceased to be a raiddle-class club, a nur- sery for coddling the few, and became an asso- ciation of workers, a camp of warriors, anxious to hear and eager to obey the call of the King, and to make His Kingdom world-wide and all- embracing. (Applause.)

GREATER CONFERENCE. The Views of Ministers. The Rev. B. N. Burton said there was a greater confidence in the Methodist Church, so far as they in the Synod represented it, than there had been in the days that were gone. They should all have confidence in themselves, for they could not do anything in the world unless they had it. The same power that moved the Methodist Church in the 18th century was at their disposal to-day, but were they getting too respectable, too superior, or afraid of the old methods of success. They. must renew confi- dence in the methods that were so successful. • If the young people renewed their confidence in Christ by personal consecration, perhaps even yet Methodism "niig-lit think they were worthy of it. (Applause.) A LAD IN WARRINGTON. The Rev. W. E. Beet said he was a lad in Warrington 30 years ago, and was pupil in the Grammar School, and he remembered well the Rev. Thos. Ripon, who had recently passed away. The Chairman had given them many suggestions which should give them cause for thought. He set the problem whether as a church they were, in some subtle way, drifting out of touch with and losing hold of the life of the nation? In all the churches there was a vast amount of reserve strength not being used; spiritual capital which had not become current, and it 'was one of the neat problems to get into currency that hidden power and draw out and make effective those latent resources. He could say that there would be such a movement in the Methodist Church that would shake the land from end to end. One of the dangers to the church was the cult of good. They were called to seek out the best, not merely the good. (Applause.)

DECREASED MEMBERSHIP. . The Rev. S. E. Keeble said it was a serious thing that for eight years they should have a decrease in church membership. He,, however, had not been discouraged once, and he was not discouraged at the present time. He would not be fundamentally discouraged if they had a decrease for four years. They must try to work Methodism, however, in order that there would be no decrease. It was because he believed in the church that he could not be discouraged, for he knew that it had gone through terrible times quite safely. When they had such a galaxy of grand scholars in the church who were so enthusiastic, there was no getting behind it. Continuing, the speaker said that the serious thing damaging the church to-day was that men had found an outlet for their higher humanitarian and ethical faculties and sympa- thies in the great social movement that had reached the world, and that accounted for the large depletion in the membership of the churches. A greater proportion of those who were not , found in the church to-day were found in the ' social movement. Re was not discouraged, , because he thought that the churches had come to see that the social movement was theirs, and they were going to take their part in it, and I occupy their proper places. It was a terrible thing that Christian men talked about eccle- siastical affairs, and never said a single word about the great movement of the people to secure for themselves rights, justice and a civil- ised life, as well as leisure to have access to the higher things which civilisation wanted. The churches were coming, and would come to see that these questions were theirs, and then the people would say "The church is living the Master's religion," and there would not be room enough in the churches to hold them. (Ap- plause). The chairman and speakers. were heartily thanked, on the pro Position of the Rev. W. A. Beckett.

Thompson, who took for his text St. Luke xi. 34, "The light d't the body is the eye: therefore when thine eye is single, they. whole body also is full of light; but when thine eye is evil, thy body also is full of darkness." The eye had always been a favourite metaphor With mystical writers. Although God. was a spirit, and dwelt not in temples made by hands, there was a spiritual Sense which was conscious of His presence.
