THE PASSING WEEK.

BURIED AT SEA.

Many Warringtonians, and especially those interested in local junior cricket, will regret to hear of the death and burial at sea of Mr. Arthur Dutton, son of Mr. Amos Daniel Dutton, 44, Arpley-street. The late Mr. Arthur Dutton, who was employed by Messrs. Greening and Sons, Ltd., and was 27 years of age, decided, because of the somewhat weak state of his health, to go to Australia, and had obtained work there with some friends on a farm. 9n the second of last month he started for his new ' home on board the Ss. Persic, subsequently became ill and died while passing through the Tropics, April 1.6. The deceased will be remem- bered for his enthusiasm and success as a cricketer. Originally he played for Moore and DaresburY, and won the batting average prize for that club for two years. Twelve months ago he played for Wilderspool Brewery C.C. in the 'Warrington League and was a consistently good bat. His father has been for a long time a prominent member of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club. The news of the death reached Warrington this week on the day the vessel arrived at its destination, Adelaide, capital of South Australia.

COUNCILLOR COUGH'S APOLOGY. At the adjoilrned Council meeting held on Wednesday night, Councillor Gough added an apology to his withdrawal of the week before for having said and done things—or refrained from doing things—which impelled the Mayor to break up the meeting almost before the pro- ceedings had begun. The apology was a model in its way. "If I did this, if I did that, and if I did the other," said Councillor Gough, "I apologise." Had the Mayor and Dr. Joseph allowed the speaker to reach the end of his sen- tence undisturbed, he would have reached the clinching "I apologise" in quick time, but his references to what had happened, although they were governed by the all-important "if," seemed to ruffle their feelings, and their interruptions ap.peared likely to lead to further trouble. But Councillor Gough went on his way serenely to the apology at the end, and if his speech is re-read, without the Mayor's comments, it will be seen how carefully the apology had been prepared and how impossible it would have been for him to depart from the form he had , studichisly laid down for himself. Councillor Gough had clearly "misunder- stood." No one in the Council, or in the town, would tolerate such preferential treatment as would insist upon the general body of builders using setts while one builder was allowed to use the less expensive tar macadam for the paving of streets. A resolution was passed, as a matter of fact, making tar macadam usable for private secondary streets throughout the borough. This resolution was on the agenda at the meeting when the " scene " occurred, but it was not reached till this week owing to the ad- journment. The committee are considering a scheme for grading the material to be used according to the traffic the streets concerned are expected to bear.

THE SUMMER SPORTS. King Football has been banished for a little while, but sportsmen have plenty of pastimes in which to interest themselves. Cricket and bowling matches and competitions are now in full swing. The Cemetery bowling team re- main to uphold the good name of Warrington in the Lancashire County Championship Com- petition, and we sincerely hope they will bring honours to the town.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
LOVE OF MEDICINE.

—..----- -- A remarkable feature has come to light in connection with the administration of the In- surance Act in Warrington. It has been dis- covered that insured persons have such a desire for securing bottles of medicine that the chem- ists' bills have exceeded the £6OO allowed for this purpose in the first:duarter of the year con- siderably, and the' Insurance Committee have, in consequence, to face a deficit of £213. How to keep this love of medicine within the limit of the finances available is the very serious pro- blem which confronts the Warrington Insur- ance Committee. They • may find a grain of comfort, however, in the fact that they are not the only body over-spent. It is a curious fact that every committee in the manufacturing dis- tricts of Lancashire and Yorkshire is in a similar position, and where women come within the scope of the Act in large numbers the de- ficits are the greater. This naturally prompts the question: Are women more fond of medi- cine than men? As yet no answer has been forthcoming. So. serious has the position be- come that a conference of representatives of Insurance Committees was held at Preston on Thursday. It was there alleged that doctors were over-prescribing, but so far as can be ascertained, no definite evidence of this has yet been produced. A sub-committee was ap- ' pointed by the conference to seek a solution of !what is a knotty problem.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
SHAKESPEAREAN PLAYERS._

..... Last week Mr. Byder Boys, a judge of acting as well as an actor of repute, paid the War- rington Amateur Shakespearean Players the compliment of attending their annual supper. Not only so, but he told them, with a sincerity which excused them from blushing, that they commanded the respect of all who were inter- ested in the great art to which they had applied thernselves. He also advised them, now they were on a firm basis, to do nothing that would allow the society to languish. His praise was all-embracing—it covered an actor's apprecia- tion of a genuine effort to make the best kind of play in the world live in a decadent age ;• of the first-class talent Mr. Charles Law has at his command for the work he has in hand, including Mr. Law's unsurpassed faculty as producer; and of the sound, business-like foun- dation upon which the society stands. We are glad to know that the society is in no danger of languishing. At the annual meeting, which takes place next Tuesday evening, "important business," in the curt language of an adver- tisement, is to be transacted. That important business, we are informed, will have to do largely with a- proposal to attract new mem- bers. There are many doubtless who would like to read and hear read in a proper manner the works of the world's greatest dramatist, even if they are not thirsting to give vent to their feelings of reverence on the stage. Young People will be welcomed to the meeting, and if they join Mr. Laws very happy family party, and their inclinations diroet them in that way; they wilt have the privilege of being instructed Hi stage-craft by one of the finest amateur producers in the country.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATED WOMEN AND THE LABOUR EXCHANGES.

Women who are capable of doing respon- sible clerical or administration work are to be taken under the care of the Board of Trade Labour Exchanges with a view to linking them Up with employers in need of such " high-clasS labour." There is an ample supply of compe- tent women secretaries, managers, accountants, shorthand-typists, advertisenient designers, for- eign corresponding clerks and the like, but em- ployers have found it far from easy to obtain this class of employe without undue delay. The Board of Trade have therefore created a Women's Department of the Exchanges, with a special woman officer, who, being in touch with ladies' colleges and training institutions, is able to arrange interviews for employers with suit- able applicants at the shortest notice. Women and girls who have spent time and money on securing a good training have now only to apply to the Women's Department of their local Labour Bxchange and employers have only to ring up or send a postcard to the same depart- ment and they will find former difficulties con- siderably diminished. We are informed that this new department has already met with con- siderable success.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
STRIKE AFTERMATH.

It is one of the finest traits of human nature that after men have fought out a battle of what- ever kind, they generally agree to let bygones be bygones, if they do not 15ecome friends because of the fire through which they have , passed. But it seems that the long struggle at Messrs. Fletcher, Busselfs work has left a sore which has taken bad ways, and those who were on the side of the union and those who were not are not working together very well. A union is a fighting force, primarily and perhaps ultimately, but it does not do to be fighting all the time. That would involve the loss of much valuable reserve of strength. We should like to suggest that now the struggle is over the quarrel should be forgotten, and the majority should show that success has not blighted their generosity. They will be all the stronger if their strength is ever again put to the test.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
A _HARMONIOUS DEAL.

The Wycliffe School Trustees having asked the Education Committee for the use of the school piano on Sundays, the Education Com- mittee have agreed, provided they can have the use of the harmonium on weekdays. We hope there will not be any discussion as to the rela- tive value of a piano for one day and a har- monium for five days.

OUR SECONDARY EDUCATION PROBLEM. It will be remembered that a month ago the Warrington Education Committee decided to consider the advisability of conferring with .the Board of Education as to the provision of sec- ondary education in the borough, upon hearing what the proposals of the Grammar School were with respect to the building of a new school. Le Board have apparently forwarded the re- quired. information, for the committee have agreed to confer with the Board and will ap- point the deputation at the•monthly .meeting on Monday night.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PARR HALL ORGAN.

„ .... A meeting of the Parr Hall Organ Committee was held this week, and although the amount promised to date is not so large as might reasonably have‘been expected, the committee are not without hope that they will be able to accomplish the end they have in view. When the instrument has been bought it will, of course, become the properly of the town, and will be at the service of the whole community. In these circumstances we do not see why, at a later stage, the Council should not vote a sum towards the cost, according to the suggestion we made some weeks ago.

SYNOD AND SUNDAY PICTURE SHOWS. Warrington this week has been honoured by the presence of the Synod of the Liverpool Dis- trict of the Wesleyan Methodist Church, and the gathering of such an influential body must have a beneficial effect upon the Wesleyan Cause within the borough and district. Many important topics were discussed in the Synod. Perhaps the most important was that dealing With the opening of Southport Cinematograph shows on Sundays. The protest raised in the Synod has not come a moment too soon, and it is to be hoped the protest Will have the effect of checking the speed of what threatens to become a national evil. There is one phase of ques- tion which the speakers seemed to have over- looked amidst the many points raised, and that is the matter of Sunday labour enforced upon the employes of such places. From a Christian and a trade union point of view these employes should not be compelled to work seven days a week for a six days' wage. On this ground, if not on other grounds, the trade unionists and Church can work in unison to check the spread of Sunday picture shows. For the fair name of Warrington, it should be made clear that the picture show proprietors in the town have no inclination towards Sunday opening.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR UPON CANCER.

The appeal which has been made by the com- mittee representing the citizens and the hos- pitals of Manchester for funds for the establish- ment of a radium depot at the Iloyal Infirmary points out the increasing prevalence of cancer, the difficulties in the way of checking it, and the hopes that have been raised by the discoveries connected with radium. "For some years past," says the appeal, careful observations as to the value of radium in the treatment of certain diseases, but more especially in the treatment of malignant (can- cerous) growths, have been made by many Brit- ish and Continental surgeons, which prove that it possesses the power in inoperable cases of fre- quently causing a disappearance of the primary focus of infection, of relieving pain, arresting hwmorrhage, and prolonging life in a way un- known with any other method of treatment at present in use.

- That is a guarded way of referring to the possibilities of this new-discovered agency for fighting disease. It is hoped that a fuller ex- perimental period will prove that radium will go beyond the alleviation stage, and will actually cure. It is known that the simpler class of cases have been cured, but it is wisely being left to time to prove whether the cures are permanent and final. More serious cases are proving themselves amenable to treatment. One of the most deadly forms is dealt with in the following paragraph taken from "The Times"

Treatment of Cancer by Radium.

The medical officer of Lambeth Infirmary re- ports a case of " apparent cure" of cancer in the case of a girl 23 years of age as the result of treatment by radium emanations. The case was one of sarcoma of the parotid gland (the large gland situated in front of the ear), and in ordinary circumstances must have been ex- pected to end life with rapidity. An operation was performed and as much as possible of the disease removed, and then radium emanations, supplied free of cost by the Radium Institute, were brought to bear on the remainder of the tumour. This disappeared completely and—after several months—has not returned.

It is necessary here again to repeat the warn- ing that sufficient time has not yet elapsed to justify the use of the word `` cure" without qualification. But the case is certainly very en- couraging, more especially in view of the age of the patient and the character of the tumour. The young woman is being kept under observa- tion.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DUTY OF WARRINGTON.

Will Warrington assist Manchester to raise the necessarily large sum needed to purchase the required amount of radium and set up its depot with its equally necessary expert Medical Radiologist? lt is certainly the duty of a com- munity like this, coming well within the Man- chester sphere, to take such a hand in the catise of humanity. We should be admitted to imme- diate benefit, and, as Warrington has its full share of cancer victizins, that is a vital consider- ation. The committee have power to admit to participation in the benefits of the scheme other hospitals, bodies and persons upon prescribed terms and conditions. The Mayor and Deputy- Mayor (Dr. Joseph, who has said that expense would nbt stand in the way of Warrington hav- ing radium if radium were necessary) may, we think, be trusted to draw up a scheme under which the town would benefit by this wonder- ful and promising discovery.

Thou canst not joke an enemy into a friend, but thou mayest joke a friend into an enemy.

If thou lookest too often in thy glass thou wilt not so much see thy face as thy folly.

Since other men's tongues are not within thy teeth, how canst thou hope to govern them?

THE POOR OR THE RICH?

The sad plight to which the Unionist party has been brought has been fur- ther emphasised this week during the debate on the Budget—the Budget Which has shown in such unmistakable fashion that in the future the com- munity that creates, or rather main- tains, the wealth we see around us is to have a more equitable share in the enjoyment of that wealth. Perhaps the best illustration of that sad plight was furnished by Mr. JOYNSON HICKS, who, on Thursday night, advanced once again the statement, which it would be an exaggeration to call an argument or a -theory, that if we tax the rich the taxation must all fall, sooner or later, on the shoulders of the workman. A superficial consideration of such a statement might lead one to ask—Why, then, should the rich and their spokesmen in the Houses of Par- liament, or on the platform, object to be taxed? If ultimately the poor pay, why should the rich mind the tempor- ary displacement caused by a con- scienceless CHANCELLOR, who insists on putting the charge upon their shoulders in the first instance? But, unlike most of the champions of the well-to-do along this line, Mr. JoyrisoN-HicKs gave a reason for his opinion, a very dan- gerous thing to do when you are not sure of the soundness of the opinion. At Grimsby he announced that in con- sequence of the Budget he had decided not to buy a motor car this year, but to have his old one done up. The work- men will, he says, lose by the difference between the money he will spend on repairs and that he would have spent on a new machine. Assuming that a wealthy man like Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS would really deprive himself of a luxury which he can well afford in spite of the incursions of the tax-gatherer, and assuming, good Tariff Reformer that he is, that he would not go abroad for his new article, if he got one, a fallacy still underlies his argument, which the merest- tyro in economics would.not fail to .see. If Mr. LLOYD GEORGE gets a few hundred pounds out of Mr. JOYNSON-HICKS'S treasury it will be spent on something. Supposing it be spent On the weakest-looking kind of " reproductive " material, a Civil Service clerk rendered necessary by the administrative calls of the new legislation. The whole of it would be spent—perhaps the last penny— upon maintaining a family, in which case it would be spent on things which would make work and " produce " beneficially, much more perceptibly, than it would had it been retained to be spent upon a single luxury by Mr. JOYNsoN-HICKS. Tariff Reform, the ready-made system for making the poor pay, has been thrown over— till a more happy season, no doubt—to such an extent that Mr. LLOYD GEORGE was able to declare that Tariff Reform is "still chained to its lonely kennel, and not even allowed tri• bark." An ardent spirit called out that he had been prepared to champion the claims of Tariff Reform, but had been unable to catch the SPEAKER'S eye, which called forth the congratulations of the CHANCELLOR upon the fact that one nurse was. still left to push the perambulator.

SUNDAY CLOSING.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., and the Temperance League. "UNABLE TO SUPPORT."

We have been requested to publish the follow- ing correspondence which has passed between Mr. Benjamin T. Parkes, honorary secretary of the Warrington and District Citizens' Temper- ance League, and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P.:- 8, Willis-street, Warrington, May 6. 1914. Dear Sir,—At a meeting held in connection with the Warrington and District Citizens! Temperance League, I was requested to write to you, to ask you to help us in our efforts for temperance, by supporting the second reading of the Bill dealing with Sunday closing of pub- lic-houses, which reading it is expected will take place on Friday, May 8, 1914. Yours truly, BENJAMIN T. PARKES, Secretary. To Harold Smith, Esq., M.P.

House of Commons, May 10, 1914..

Dear Sir,—ln acknowledging your letter of the 6th inst., I must express my regret that I was unable to support the Sunday Closing Bill on Friday last. In fact, I felt it to be my duty to offer stren- uous opposition to a measure which, in my judgment, would not reduce drunkenness, and which would, if passed, introduce into England the shebeens and illicit drinking dens, which are the feature in Wales and Ireland—for in- stance, in Wales, the number of illicit sale prosecutions is over ten times as great as in England, although the population is only about 1-16th. I prefer the English system, where drink is sold openly and under strict regulations on premises where a man is not allowed to take too much. Further, I would remind you that there is no evidence that Sunday closing results in less drunkenness. For example: The reduction of cases of drunkenness per head of the population is greater in Monmouthshire, where there is not Sunday closing, than it is in Glamorganshire, where there is. It is greater in the Irish cities than in rural areas—in the former there is no Sunday closing, in the latter there is. It is greater in England than it is in Scotland, where, as you know, Sunday closing is in force. I will do anything to induce temperance, but it must be real temperance. I have on several occasions backed a Bill con- taining most stringent regulations, with the idea of stamping out the drinking club, but I cannot point to any assistance received from either the Government or the temperance party. As this is a matter of some public interest, I am sending a copy of this correspondence to the local Press. Yours very truly, HAROLD SMITH. Mr. Benjamin T. 'Parkes.

REDUCTION OF HOURS MEANS LESS DRUNKENNESS. (cops.) May 14, 1914. Dear Sir,—ln reply to your letter of the 10th' inst., which, by the way, was Sunday, I accept your regret at not being able to support the Sunday Closing Bill, which would only have re- duced the hours of sale by three. In your second paragraph you write of the shebeens and illicit drinking dens. Well, lam surprised that any Member of Parliament should be found on the platform of a trade which, from his own correspondence, uses illicit methods for the sale of its commodity, rather than subinit to the Statute of the realm. I would also point out that the evidence given to Royal Commissions on this question, both in Ireland and Wales, was in favour of the continuance of Sunday closing. In reply to paragraph three, I will mention the following. In the year 1906 the hours of sale in the five cities of Ireland which are ex- empt from total Sunday closing, were reduced •from five to three hours, and the difference in cases of drunkenness on Sunday is: 1906. 1912. Diff. Dublin Belfast Cork Limerick Waterford

Totals 1309 55.00 per cent. Thus two hours' reduction of sale means less drunkenness. Further, all temperance organisations are agreed that clubs ought to be subject to the same regulations as to the sale of intoxicating liquor to which public-houses are subject. But Sunday closing does not seem to mean an in- crease of clubs, as England has a greater num- ber of them, according to population, than either Scotland, Ireland or Wales, namely, England one to 4,303 persons, Wales pne to 6,803, Scotland one to 7,581, Ireland one to 17,560. I submit that true temperance legis- lation is that which will help to stop. to some extent, the sale of intoxicating drink, which alone is responsible for drunkenness.—Yours truly,

589 720

BENJ. T. PARKES.

H. Smith, Esq., M.P. P.S.—I have forwarded a copy to local Press. —B.T.P.

- CHANCELLOR'S VISIT. Admission of Wardens and Sidesmen.

For the admission of churchwardens and sidesmen recently elected, Chancellor Chaloner Dowdall held his visitation at the Parish Church on Thursday. In the course of his address Chancellor Dow- dall said that many of the secular powers and duties formerly belonging to churchwardens and sidesmen were theirs no longer, but it was upon the zeal and ability of them and their col- leagues, the sidesmen, that the welfare of the church was increasingly dependent. Their function was no longer merely ministerial—it was fundamental and dynamic. Because the relation which existed between the church- wardens and the Church and between the Church and the nation appeared at the present time to be one of exceptional and critical im- portance, he would make observations on it. The ofilce of churchwarden was of growing import- ance. ' Much of the duty which once fell upon the Church—secular government, secular educa- tion, and the like—had now to a great extent been taken over by the State, and the whole national life was in danger of falling into a secular condition in which, though hands might be driven to co-operate, the hearts' of people would be starved in isolation. Not from the Church alone, but from every quarter it was seen that the palsy of materialism was laying hold of the nation as well as the individual, and it was not Churclipeople alone who looked to the Church to glorify and ennoble and enrich and invigorate life. What then was the position of churchwardens and sidesmen in this crisis? In every parish the churchwardens and sides- men were the head of the laity, in organic rela- tion on the one hand with the parishioners by whom they were elected and the incumbent, and on the other hand with the bishop. Leader- ship and responsibility rested with them, and 'there could be no doubt that the welfare of the Church was increasingly in their hands.

THIRTY-TWO PARISHES. The parishes included in that call were 32 and in all cases excepting six there were Easter offerings. An Easter offering was a great mis- take unless freely given by all concerned. If there were any difference of opinion it were better that it should not he. Where the 'feeling ()listed in favour of it, it was, he thought, a means of union, sympathy and pleasant recog- nition which should be encouraged. After speaking of the benefit of Church Councils, he said that the fabric of the churches included in that call appeared to be uniformly in good condition, and the insurance in many cases was fully adequate. In some cases it appeared to be below the basis he had indicated in his charge, namely, the basis of reinstatement and without "average" clause. He asked them, in conclusion, when making their returns as regarded population and seating accommodation, to consult with the incumbent, so that the figures in their report, and those in the Diocektn Calendar, might be uniform. The returns in that rural deanery had been made with great completeness and care, and that showed the excellent way in which the work was being done.

Scarlet Fever.

There were 59 cases of scarlet fever in the Warrington Hospital last week. Thirteen new cases had been reported.

Warrington Agricultural Society., The Warrington and District Agricultural Society announce that their 25th annual show will be held at Walton Park on 6th August.

Schoolboys' Swimming Inslructor. Mr.. J. Peers, the well-known Warrington swimmer aml water polo player, has been ap- pointed by the Education Committee instructor of swimming to schoolboys.

Died from Blood POISOIIIIIII. William, he 31-y-ear-old son of Erederiek Jones, works' labourer, of 3, Collin-street, died from blood poisoning in the Warrington Infir- mary on Sunday.

The Grammar School.

A deputation from the Warrington Education Committee is to meet the Board of Education to discuss the provision of secondary education in the borough, and the proposal for a new Grammar School.

Warrington A.C. Sports. The Warrington Athletic Club are to hold an athletic festival on August Bank Holiday. Splendid prizes will be awarded for many open events. Full particulars may be obtained from the Secretary, Mr. R. Bennett, 194, Gorsey-lane, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
The Tramways Profits.

The income of the Warrington Tramways for the year ending 31st March was £23,753, on which there was a net profit of £3,239. Of this, £2,500 is to go in relief of the rates. The motor 'bus revenue was £2,287, leaving a balance of £336, which is to be carried forward.

Salvation Army and the Blind. Adjutant J. Beaumont and the Senior Band desire to thank the many friends who assisted last week-end in tlm effort for the National Institute for the Blind. The result of the special march on Saturday night and the retir- ing offering on Sunday was ~C 4 9s.

Councillor Taylor. Councillor Taylor, our readers will be glad to know, is recovering gradually and satisfac- torily after his recent operation. According to present indications, it is likely lie will be able to leave Dr. Fox's Nursing Home for his own home during the next fortnight.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
Ragged Sunday School.

Mr. J. C. Parr gave his annual tea to 400 poor children in the above school on Monday. Tea was followed by an entertainment, consisting of singing and reciting, and this was very much enjoyed. Hearty cheers were given by the children for Mr. and Mrs. Parr.

Steam Dray Charges House. Yesterday a steam dray owned by Messrs. G. Smith and Co., Eccles, got out of control at Houghton's Farm, Paddington. charged through the garden wall, and was pulled up by coming into contact with the house. No one was in- jured. but the engine broke an axle anl was stranded in the garden.

Keen Bowling Game. An interesting bowling match was plaYed on the green of the Dog and Dart Hotel on Thurs- day afternoon between Mr.. P. Brown (the "Russet") and Mr. J. W. Savory (the " Duck"), both of Warrington. Savory received two start in 31. Brown scored six before Savory broke his " duck " with a single. After that it was a foregone conclusion, Brown running out an easy winner, 31-20.

Property for Sale. At the Blue Bell Inn on Wednesday evening Mr. Frank Hickman offered for sale by public auction two lots of leasehold property, the first being 14 houses in Hume-street and the second lot 10 houses in Steel-street. In neither case was the reserved price reached, but it is hoped that both lots ,will be disposed of privately. The solicitor to the vendors was Mr. Thomas S. Steel, of Mill-street Chambers, Warrington.

Railwayman's Death. A familiar figure at Warrington Central Station will be missed by a large number of regular travellers and the public generally by the death of Mr. Samuel Dutton, who for 26 years has been ticket collector. Ileonly gave up his work on Wednesday afternoon, having contracted a chill in attending his father's funeral at Prodsham a month ago. Pneu- monia set in and he passed away on Saturday morning. The funeral took place at Warring- ton Cemetery on Tuesday.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
Band Programme.

----- The 4th Battalion P.W.V. (South Lancashire) Regiment Band will play the following pro- gramme in Bank Park to-morrow, commencing at 3.15 p.m.: Hymn of praise, "To God, the Lord of All" (Gounod); Morcean Lyrique, " Songe Adore" (Fletcher); selection, "Faust No. 2" (Gounod) ; song, "The Rosary" (Nevin); Barcarolle, "Lee Contes D'Hoffmann " (Offen- bach). selection, "Don Sabastiano" (Donizette); cornet solo, "Il Bacio" (Arditti); march, "Let the hills resound" (Balfour) ; regimental march, and "God save the King." The bandmaster will be Mr. C. Wainwright.

May Horse Parade. Exceptional interest is always centred on the spectacular horse parade in Warrington, and on May 23rd the 14th annual event of this kind will be held. The entries close to-day. Ar- rangements have been made for the judging to take place in front of the Town Hall, and the competition is open to all drivers of vehicles within the borough, at an entrance fee of is. for each horse. One of the main objects of the parade is to improve the general condition and treatment of horses. After the judging there will he a grand procession through the princi- ' pal streets. Latchford Football Club.

Last night the annual meeting of the Latch- ford Football Club was held in the Cheshire Cheese Hotel, Latchford. Mr. T. Green, the secretary, in his report, said the season had been a highly successful one from every point of view. The team had done well in winning the Altrincham Amateur Cup and being in the semi-final for the Cheshire Amateur Cup. The Works' Medal Competition was a huge success, there being a record entry. The season was concluded with a balance in hand of £4. Amongst the items of income was one of .t. 12 for gate receipts, but when the ground has been considerably improved, and everything possible done to ensure the comfort of spectators, as well as making the playing ground perfect, it is ex- pected the receipts will go up.—Next season the team are endeavouring to get in the South Lan- cashire League, or the Liverpool County Com- bination.—The officials were elected for the season 1914-16.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 16, 1914.

ST. BARNABAS' SALE.

Church Workers Effort to Raise Money for Enlargement.

THE VICAR'S SCHEME.

With hopes of raising a substantial amount towards the enlargement fund of St. Barnabas' Church, the parish workers have this week held a two days' sale of work. Fresh surroundings were obtained for this annual bazaar, for this year it took place in the Patten Hall, Winmar- leigh-street. The well-stocked stalls were a mute but powerful indication of the hard work which had been necessary in the preparation, and the ladies who presided at these stalls proved themselves excellent business women. On Wednesday the opening ceremony was per- formed by the Mayor (Councillor Peter Pea- cock), who was accompanied by the Mayoress, under the presidency of the Vicar (the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth). Amongst others present were the Rev. and Mrs. B. Percival, the Rev. and Mrs. E. C. E. Carleton, the Rev. J. W. Glen- denning (a former curate), and Mrs. Parr.

VICAR'S HUMOUR. The VICAR said he had always felt himself more or less a fraud when he appeared at their sales of work, for he always mentioned in the objects to be benefited the church extension scheme, although he knew very little money would go to that cause—(laughter)—because when they had a second clergyman the money a Pay his stipend. This year he felt really perehaelllyd ohotnest, because lie was living in single blessedness. (Laughter.) His late curate rY, and he had been on his olej-tn him nce,infojracnuilarates were not so easy to get as blackberries in September. (Laughter.) He intended each quarter day to pay into the church extension fund the money that would otherwise go to pay a curate's stipend, so that at the end of the year they would have some- thing towards the fund. He had not any defi- nite scheme of church expansion as yet, but something would have to be done before very long. Whether have a new chance!, side aisles, and perhapscahettohwerer:hhe would enlarge the church e could not say, but before 'very long he was hoping to put before them n certain schemes for the approval of the congre- gation. He was pleased to see the Mayor and Mayoress present, and he thanked Councillor ,Peacock for past favours and for his gift of five guineas that day. (Applause.)

THE MAYOR'S SPEECH. The MAroa, in declaring the sale open, said it was a pleasure to meet the people of White- °t Ward. lie I,votild have liked to have attended their church on Mayor's Sunday, but he found it would not have held those who were in the procession. He would like to see their church extension commenced, for it was long overdue. Sales of work made the workers feel actual partners in the good work they had at heart. (Applause.) Mr. Bone proposed, and Mr. OXLEY seconded, a vote of thanks to Mayor. This was carried, the Mayor responding.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====

THE SECOND DAY.

The Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth again acted as chairman on the second day, when the proceed- ings were opened by Mrs. Joseph Harrison. In the course of a humorous introductory speech, the CFPIIRMAN stated that the amount raised.the previous day totalled £Bl 12s. 4d., of which was a contribution from Lady Greenall. The takings for the second day would include the sums of £5 from Colonel J. Hall Walker and £3 from Mr. J. E. Seeker. He owed Mrs. Harrison a debt of gratitude for relieving his mind about the opening of the sale, but he need not say much about her, her goodness was so well known. (Applause.) In declaring the sale open, Mrs. IlAuarsox trusted that the workers would be repaid for their labours by having nothing left to sell at the close of the day. (Hear, hear.) Mr. GLENDENNING moved a vote of thanks to Mrs. Harrison. He thought sales of work joined together the different branches of religion in the town, and hoped that the proceeds of the second day would equal those of the first. Mrs. Harrison took part in almost every good move- ment in the town.

Mr. H. V. MArnEw seconded amidst anplause. The proceeds from the second day's sale mounted to ,£6l 4s. Bd., making the total for both days £142 17s.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
GUN-RUNNER "TRAITORS."

Pointed Speech at Opening Meeting of Progressive Labour League.

TORIES AND HOME RULE.

The Progressive Labour League opened their summer campaign with a most successful meeting at Bridge Foot on Monday evening. The attendance was satisfactory. The speaker, Mr. Wm. Madgen, Manchester, dealt in a humorous vein with the civil war scare in Ulster. He was sure there would be no war, despite all the gun-running. Even the Member for Warrington, said Mr. Madgen, does not believe in civil war. He is going to get married. This sally was greeted with great laughter. Mr. Madgen showed that as far back as 1886 the Tories, who were then led by Lord Salisbury, had promised Home Rule to the Nationalists for the support of their votes in the House of Commons.

Mr. M. Llewellyn presided over the gather- ing, which was enthusiastic and most har- monious. There were only one or two interrup- ters, who were soon bowled out by the speaker.

The Conservatives, said the Chairman, who claimed to be the most loyal of subjects and who stood for law and order within the Empire, were now talking about the wonderful rebellion they had organised in Ulster. They had even started gun-running. (Laughter.) I do not know how they can reconcile gun-running with their pro- , fessed love of law and order. (Voice: "Rub it in !") When Mr. Winston Churchill was last in Warrington he used the words "They are on the run." I believe they are still on the run. They are running in every direction to find something to bring against the Government, and they are defeatet in every quarter. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140516_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE IN POLITICS.

! Mr. Madgen said no one to-day could say !politics were dull. There was plenty going on, with the promise of a great deal more in the future, as their opponents would learn. The fact that the Opposition had descended to abuse showed that they were beaten everywhere. A certain noble lord said he would prefer to sweep a crossing than sit with the traitors in the Ministry. That noble lord would be of more service as a crossing sweeper than as a member of the Progressive party. (Laughter and cheers.) Still it was strange that while such people were delivering vulgar abuse about the Government, the leaders of the Conservative party were fall- ing over each other in their eagerness to nego- tiate with the Prime Minister. (Hear, hear, and a Voice: " What about Swift Mac Neill?")

===== 0002601_19140516_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
Interrupter Floored.

An interrupter asks about Mr. Swift Mac Neill, Well,Mr.MacNeill is a professor of constitutional law in the University of Ireland. (Hear, hear.) That shows he is not a brainless man, and he knows more about Parliamentary procedure than anyone else in the House of Commons. Lord Selborne was going to make an attack on the Government, and he was shouting about " Marconis " until a letter appeared from Mr. Mac Neill, in which he showed that once Lord Sel- borne, when a member of the Ministry, was a director of the very company with which his department did business. It was Mr. Mac Neill who let daylight in on that Selborne record, and then his lordship made a quick climb down. (Laughter and applause.) Mr. Mac Neill is a Protestant elected to the House of Commons by Roman Catholics. (Voice: " Give it 'em now.")

Religious Bitterness. One of the Tory objections to Home Rule is that it is going to lead to religious bitterness and persecution. lam a Protestant, and I say there is only one thing worse than Catholics persecuting • Protestants, and it is Protestants Persecuting Catholics. I think we should realise , we all are citizens of one Empire, under one King, and that we should try to make matters as pleasant as possible for all. (Applause.) We are told that the North-east corner of Ulster is the money corner, the prosperous corner—under Free Trade, note—(laughter)—and the Tories say Redmond wants it, as it pays the taxes. Do you think it would be good states- manship of Redmond, or whoever else may be in power—it may be King Carson—(laughter)— to damage any part of Ireland that is pros- perous? It would be like killing the goose that lays the golden eggs.

Tory Home Rule.

- _ The Conservatives, now against Home Rule,. in 1886, under certain conditions to gain the support of the Irish, offered to give Home Rule. Thirty years ago the Conservatives, then led by Lord Salisbury, were willing to give Home Rule to get the Irish vote. What appears so terrible to the Tories they were once prepared to give. (Applause.) Mr, Balfour had with him a brilliant man, the late George Wyndham, whom he made Irish Secretary. He brought in a scheme for Home Rule under the name of Devolution, and he was chucked out of his job. Thus to the Tories Home Rule is not so dangerous or novel as they now seek to make out.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Time It Was Through.

We have been considering Ho-me Rule for 30 years, and surely it is long enough to get one measure through. (Hear, hear.) Why is it the Liberal party and the Labour party are anxious to get the Bill passed? They know quite well when the Home Rule and Welsh Bills are passed the way is clear for mething greater and better. (yoke "I don't think !") I do think, and that is where my friend and I differ. (Laughter.) I do think, and I am giving you now the benefit of my thinking. (Applause.) If we get these measures out of the way we shall get social reforms which the Tories do not want. When we give this measure of liberation to Ire- land we shall get a greater measure of liberation for English democracy. (Loud applause,),

The Member for Warrington. We are told a great deal of awful things will happen under Home Rule. We are told there is going to be civil war in Ulster. Even the Member for Warrington does not believe in civil war. He is going to get married. (Loud laugh- ter.) If I thought civil war was coming and that I would have to go to the front, I think I should postpone my marriage to see whether I survived the fighting. (Voice: "He won't go," and loud laughter.) All this talk about civil war is the purest bunkum. We have beard the same talk before from the Tories. When Sir William Harcourt introduced death duties the Tories said the country was going to ruin. Then, instead of removing them, a Tory Chancellor made them larger. (Laughter.) This cry about civil war was a similar bit of Tory bluff.

South African War Incident. The party that has tried so many shifts, that has played a game of bluff for so long without success, now seeks to make capital against Home Rule with the statement—l do not believe it— that the Irish cheered the British defeats in South Africa. Suppose the Irish party did cheer British defeats, were not the Irishmen the best fighters in the British Army. (Voicett " Traitors !") There are traitors in all nations, and some of the greatest traitors are to be found connected with the gun-running in Ulster. (Loud applause.) There can he no greater traitors in Britain than those who are now raising an army in Ulster to fight the British *Army, of which their King is head. (Applause.) These should be the last people in the world to shout traitor at anyone. (Renewed applause.)

Gun-Running " Traitors." The Tories have proved that conclusively by their recent speeches and actions. It is not be- coming of those who are now engaged in gun- running in Ulster to say anything about those

GUN-RUNNER "TRAlTORS"—continued. ---

whom they alleged cheered British defeats in South Africa.. (Applause.) Once Ireland gets Home Rule there will be no more loyal part to be found in the whole of the great British Empire—no brighter jewel in the crown of our King. (Loud applause.) The cause of fair treatment to Ireland and justice to England makes the granting of Home Rule a vital necessity to both, and when it is passed will come a new and better chance for the people of England, as well as for the people of Ireland. (Applause.)

Budget Principle. We have just seen a great Budget introduced with a great principle involved. Mr. Lloyd George says "instead of asking the common people to pay more taxation, I shall ask the people who are starving on £5O a week to pay." It seems more sensible than putting further tax- ation on working men with £5O a year. (Ap- plause.) It is not reasonable to expect, as the Tories do, working men to pay more than their share of the taxation. The property owners, the landowners and those of wealth get a greater share of protection from the army, navy and police, and the whole aim of the Budget is to tax the men in proportion to the share they get of the. national services. There is not a single political party who will dare to tax the working men any more. Even Tariff Reform has got its death blow in the Budget of 1914. (Loud applause.)

NOT "AS ELSEWHERE." The Chairman asked for questions, but as none were forthcoming he remarked:

Don't be frightened. We will treat you with perfect courtesy. We promise not to get off the platform as happened elsewhere, and pull off our coats to fight you if you ask questions. (Loud laughter.) We can answer your questions fairly and squarely.

There were no questions, and on the motion of Mr. Buchanan, seconded by Mr. Ashworth, the speaker and chairman were thanked, the meet- ing ending with enthusiastic cheers for the government.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
"HER NIGHT OF TEMPTATION." Forceful Drama at Royal Court.

We get something like the problematical situ- ation of an irresistible force meeting an im- movable post at the Royal Court Theatre this week, where, in "Her Night of Temptation," the hero, who has earned the reputation of "Never Surrender Jack," has as his rival a passionate and strong-willed Indian -Mystic, who has "no foe but destiny." For the ulti- mate victory of one the other . has to be destroyed, and this comes about by the orthodox self-destruction of the Indian in the last scene. Although there is nothing decidedly new in the general plot—the Colonel's daughter prefers a private soldier lover, who afterwards inherits a title, to a dashing, presumptive captain, or a hypocritical and diabolically cruel Indian, and war to the knife is declared by the rivals—there are many novel features about the production. The flying machine is introduced as a means I of chasing the abductors, and is also the medium through which the conspirators try to kill the hero. The play abounds in exciting situations, some of which are novel. Joseph Millane's No. 1 Company bring out all the features in a decidedly brilliant manner. Mr. Paul Lovett as Jack Harding is exceptionally good, his act- ing being forceful, yet in no way exaggerated. Mr. Joseph Millane has a thankless part as the Indian Mystic, but, nevertheless, he brings out all the traditional passion, lust and cunning of the Oriental in a striking manner. Miss Winnifred Willard, as Rose Vibart, infuses the part with just sufficient impetuosity, vivacity and tenderness to make a successful appeal. Mr. Henry Earlesmere and Mr. Roland Bridge are equally good as the dashing English Colonel and the unscrupulous Captain Wynne respec- tively. Miss Merwall Rosebrook is a capital Azeema, and Mr. Joseph Magrath, as Terry Miles, provides sufficient rich Irish comedy to relieve the heavier aspects of the play. The other _parts are in capable hands. '

===== 0002601_19140516_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL " STARS " AT HIPPODROME. A Fascinating Programme.

Even the most fastidious must be satisfied with the excellent programme at the Hippo- drome this week. A big bill is headed by "The Devil's Elbow," a Western romance, presented so admirably by John F. Traynor and Co. The sketch is brimful of "incidents," and deals with the eternal triangle—man, wife, and "the other man "—in a gripping and interesting fashion. Mr. Traynor makes a whole-hearted happy-go-lucky cowboy husband, and he is ably assisted by the other artistes. Susie Delmore presents a " turn " reminiscent of musical comedy, with her four gentlemen, who colla- borate with her in many pleasing numbers. Tom Lee causes much laughter in his smart musical monologue as "The Single Married Man," and one of his best numbers is his song about "Side." , Polly and Day present a pan- tomimical comedy act, in which there are many ludicrous situations. Their "crazy waltz" is the limit in burlesque presentations. Mdlle. Doria has trained her dogs in a perfect manner. and in the sketch "In the maid's absence" they display their sagacity in a wonderful fashion. Will Athlone and Cissie Sinclaire score with their drawing-room speciality, and Jose Delaine is indeed a very versatile lady, who

Happy Ending to the Recent Scene in the Town Council.

COUNCILLOR COUGH MARES AN EXPLANATION.

Peace was once more restored in the Council at the meeting on Wednesday evening. This was the meeting; adjourned on account of the "scene" which occurred a week ago. When the Mayor took his seat there was a hush of expectancy all through the Council , Chamber. Without comment on the past incident the Mayor started the busionss at the stage where it was interrupted. There was a dash of excitement in the air when Councillor Gough arose. The feeling was that one did not quite know what to expect. The dive branch, however, was held forth. Councillor Gough made hif apology, and this was accepted by the Mayor: Then the Coun- cil went on to discuss tar-macadam for roads. The members of the public, who assembled in expectancy of a renewal of, the scene, were disappointed, and as the Ccruincil went on to discuss the "dry " subject' of tar-paving, many left the meeting. It seems that the Paving Committee are con- sidering the question of cradling roads into three classes: (1) main, (2) secondary, (3) sub- sidiary. This grading will prevent future difficulties in regard to paving.

There was scarcely a quorum. present when the Mayor took the chair. Immediately after, however, members trooped into. the Chamber from the ante-room, where, apparently, an informal conference had been in progress- The MAYOR instantly rose and Said: The business before the meeting, is to com- plete the ordinary Council summons, We left off at last meeting at the point where Councillor Gough was proposing an amendment to refer hack the Paving and Sewerage Committee's recommendation: "That the cross-streets shown on the plan submitted for the erectim of houses near Westy-lane for Mr. Gordon Roberts and others, be paved with tarred inaczata,lm''

ordinary business. Alderman BOLTON: If he is doixg that I do not agree,.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
DID NOT RELIEVE IN IT.

Alderman BOLTO1c.; 9a.id he would second, be- cause he did not believe in tar-macadam. He had had to pave streets with setts for 25 years 4 and they needed no repairs at the cost of the Corporation. He thought, in fairness to pro- perty owners, who hack to pave streets to last all time, the standard should be kept up to, and he did not believe in the system in Stockton Heath, where they had jerry streets. The Paving Committee were going out of their way in trying to put third-class paving in first-class passages. The new buiTilers were as well able to bear the charges as the older builders. ("Oh ! oh !") It was a mistak-e for the Corporation to allow jerry back streets.

Councillor 0111131 ES thought the first step vras to rescind the resolution passed on 26th Octo- ber, 1880, in regard to paving. Alderman BENNETT appealed to Councillor Gough to withdraw his amendment. The streets which were subsidiary ones were of a class suit- able for tar-macadam paving. He had seen many streets so paved, ami they gave good results.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSURANCE WANTED.

Councillor GOUGH said if he got an assurance that tar-macadam would be adopted for second- ary streets generally he would have no objec- tion to withdraw.

Councillor HAWTHORN said the Paving and Sewerage Committee were considering the question, and the idea was to have three grades of streets: (1) for heavy traffic, with setts pave- ment; (2) secondary streets, with granited asphalt; and (3) a third grade, with paving suitable, such as tar-macadam. It would be an instruction to the committee, when plans were sent in, to grade the streets. Councillor lIENsaALL also-, asked Councillor Gough to withdraw his , amendment. Like• Alderman Bolton, he was-one of the unfortu- nate individuals who had to.pay for heavy pav- ing, but he thought the committee should be congratulated and not condemned for what they were now trying to do. (Hear, hear.) Finally Councillor Gough withdrew the. amendment.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ANCIENT RESOLUTION.

Councillor PEMBERTON moved "That all un--- paved private secondary streets be paved with tar-macadam of approved quality and that the following resolution of the Council of the 26th October, 1880, be rescinded: 'That no new streets made by, or at the expense of, the owners of property be adopted unless formed; in the following manner—Curbs 12 inches by 8 inches; tile footpaths 9 inches of hard material , or slag and a surface of setts.'" He thought the matter had been thrashed out fully. It was his object,to,get a better, cheaper and cleaner class of paving.. Alderman BENNETr, in seconding, said he had experience of the new class of paving, and was strongly in favour of it. The first step was to get the old, fossilised regulations out of the way.

Councillor HAwrnons thought that in view of the scheme of the Paving Committee the reso- lution should be withdrawn for the present. Alderman JAMES EVANS thought the resolutien only dealt with secondary streets, whereas the question was a much broader one. Councillor Prmararox-declined to withdraw on the strength of a promise. He had had past experience of the value of such promises.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
APPROVED QUALITY.

Councillor ITESSHALL said the resolution re- ferred to tar-macadam of "approved quality."' Who were to be the judges of the quality.? Councillor ROBERTS said the Paving Commit- tee were to he congratulated if they got to the stage of grading streets. He thought that a mini. mum cost for making a street should be fixed, chargeable against every individual who built streets.

Councillor Gomm thought one thing they. should discard' was pavements of chequer tiles; which were unsightly. They should use con- crete flags.

Finally Councillor Pemberton's motion was adopted, 18 voting for and two against.

Are Stockton Heath streets jerry? Alderman Bolton told the Warrington Town Council That they were, yet that Council are going To pave streets in the same way. Prithee, where does the Alderman's logic come in?

The "5.0.5." signal was sent out From a ship ashore on the Canal banks. One of the fussy little tugs went full speed To the succour of the stranded Irish liner. In a few minutes the distressed vessel Was safe on the placid waters of the Canal. "Don't go to that farm at Stockton Heath." So quoth Weary Willie to Tired Tim. "Why, are police also there?" queried the other. "Nay," was the answer, one to work."

Begging.—At an Occasional Court yesterday Thomas Mason, a tramp, was charged with beg- ging in London-road, Appleton, and was sent to gaol for seven days.

Dogs' Late Hours.—Several dog owners were summoned at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday for allowing their dogs to wander. during prohibited hours, and were fined 2s. 6d. each.

No Need to Beg Now.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday three tramps were charged with begging. One, an old offender, was sent to prison for a month, and the other two were committed for seven days. On the Bench.—The magistrates present on the Bench at the Stockton Heath Petty Ses- sions on Tuesday were Messrs. G. Dakin (in the chair), T. Baxter, C. Jackson Holmes, H. G. Roberts and Fletcher.

No Red Light—For not having a red light showing at the rear of a cart at Walton Superior, Thomas Massey, 3, Melville-street, Warrington, was, at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, fined 28. 6d.

No Control.—A fine of 2s. 6d. each was imposed

upon Joseph Whitlaw, 10, Clegge-street, War- rington, and Thomas Higginson, 13, Admiral-

street, Warrington, at the-§tockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, for not having proper con- trol over horses and carts.

Sunday Golf Petition.—Although the golfers have not yet started playing the game on Sun- days, as decided at the club's annual meeting, a considerable amount of feeling has been aroused in Stockton Heath at the club's proposal. A petition against Sunday golf has been prepared and is being signed.

Five Rabbits—Z2.—For unlawfully having five rabbits in his possession, obtained from land in Grappenhall, James Clare, a labourer, 39, War- burton-street, Warrington, was fined £2 and costs, with the alternative of a month's im- prisonment, at the Stockton Heath Petty Ses- sions on Tuesday. He had been charged pre- viously.

His Lamp Was "Pinched."—John Lomax, 29, School-brow, Warrington, was charged at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday with failing to have a light on his car in London- road, Stockton Heath.—P.C. Richardson proved the case.—Defendant said his lamp had been " pinched." and he had been told by _a gentle- man that it would be "all right."—He was fined fis. and costs.

•

' Motor Cars Collide.—A motor accident occurred at Stockton Heath on Wednesday afternoon, when two cars collided in Walton- road. Fortunately no body was injured; but the cars were badly damaged and had to be towed home. The cars belonged to Mr. Proctor, Sut- ton, Macclesfield, and Mr. Pitchford, Folly-lane, Warrington. Damage was done to a garden wall by the colliding cars.

Ship Ashore.—One of the Irish boats, the Wicklow, Vrom Dublin to Manchester, was carried by the force of the tide on Tuesday afternoon against the side of the canal and ran aground a short distance below the Swing Bridge. A tug was fortunately at hand and she assisted the vessel to get free from the bank. After 20 minutes' delay the Wicklow was able to pass on to Manchester without sustaining any voticeable damage.

The Home-coming.—For riding a cycle without a light on May 3, at Walton Superior, Samuel Worthington, of 4, Hanover-street, Warrington, . was fined is. at the Stockton Heath Petty

Sessions on Tuesday.—He said he had been with friends camping out at Frodsham, and they intended to stay there over-night, but On account of the bad weather they came home sooner than they expected. He was riding with two friends, who had lighted lamps, and they were only going slowly.

A Waggon Offence.—On Tuesday, at the Stock- ton Heath Petty Sessions, Henry Massey, con- tractor, Little Leigh, was charged with failing to have the weight of a waggon painted thereon. —lnspector Breeze said he was in London-road, Stockton Heath, On April 17, when he saw a heavy locomotive drawing three waggons loaded with straw. He stepped the driver and told him his master would be reported for not having the .weight painted on the last waggon.—A fine of ..€:1 and costs was imposed.

ing the Stockton Heath Council School have been successful in passing the written part of the examination to qualify for scholarships at the Lymm Grammar School. The scholarships, which are tenable for three years, are given by the Cheshire County Council. The scholars who have passed the examination are Dennis Menu- field, Leonard Rigby, Edwin Gaskell, Geoffrey Teare, Hewart Challinor, Ronald Watson, Frank Cross, Norman Peters and Evelyn Richards. A Danger Spot—The bridge over the Bridge- water Canal at London-road is characterised as the most dangerous spot in the district, and residents who §ee the enormous motor traffic which passes over it daily are surprised no accident has occurred. Such a. mishap was av'erted by inches on Tuesday. A motor car was going in one direction and a motor cycle in the other, and both made for the bridge at the same moment. Had they met on the summit a serious smash would have resulted. Forth- nately the motor driver saw the danger, stopped his vehicle and promptly evaded a collision.

Baited Out the Dog.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Robert Ernest Pike, 17, Fairfield-road, was charged with allow- ing his dog to be at large wearing a collar with- out having his name and address inscribed thereon.—Defendant said that the dog was not his; it belonged to his daughter, and her name, "m. Pike," was scratched on the collar, al- though he admitted there was no address on the collar. When the licence was taken out his name was inadvertently given. as the owner. —He was fined 2s. 6d.—Defendant I suppose I can appeal? What is the alternative?—The Clerk (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw) : A distress warrant. —Defendant caused some amusement by saying he had had to bail the dog out.

Window Smashing.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Francis Bettles, Warburton-street, Stockton Heath, was charged with being drunk and disorderly at Stockton Heath on April 29.—The police evidence showed that following upon receiving a report that a number of windows had been smashed at the back of a house in Fairfield-road, a police officer found the defendant in a drunken condition and using bad language. He cautioned defendant, and when he took him into custody he continued his bad language on the way to the Police Sta- tion.—Supt. Owen said that the defendant had made good the damage, and that was his first offence.—The prisoner, in a very loud tone, was making a statement about a bet, when the Chairman (Mr. Dakin) interrupted, saying it was not bearing on the case.—He was fined ss.

Sudden Death of Mr. A. H. Dunn.—With tragic suddenness Mr. Alfred Howard Dunn, a well-known Stockton Heath man, who used to reside at 47, Cawdor-street, died at his uncle's farm—Moat Farm, Bold—on Tuesday evening. Fie had partaken of a hearty tea, and while walking through the fields fell down and ex- pired. The doctor certified death as heart :allure. Mr. Dunn, who was 32 years of age and unmarried, was a son of Mr. A. H. Dunn, who for a long time was connected with a War- rington business.

YOUTHS AND THE GUN. Incident at Stockton Heath.

For shooting with an air gun in a garden at the rear of an unoccupied house, 4, Walton-road, Walton Inferior, Sidney Newall, chemist's as- sistant, Drackanfels, Walton-road, Walton In- ferior, and Reginald Warburton, builder's as- sistant. 7, Walton-road, Stockton Heath, were summoneti at the Stockton Heath Petty Ses- sions on Tuesday. The evidence of P.C. Rowlands was to the effect that at 12.50 p.m. on April 11, he saw both defendants at the rear of an unoccupied house in Walton-road, Walton Inferior. He saw them shooting with pellets, and when he asked Newall whose gun it was, he answered "It is mine." Witness asked Newell if he had a licence, to which he replied "I bought it so that I would not need any licence." Warburton admitted to the witness that he had used the gun. Mr. Crawford, for defendants, said it was stated that the boys were shooting at a mark on the door, and later that a branch of a tree was shot at. It was lawful for them to shoot with- out a ligence if they were on the premises of a dwelling-house. The gun had now been taken away, and it should not be used again. The Chairman stated they had no right to use a gun without a licence, and the defendants would have to pay the costs.

"NATURE'S VOICES." Sunday Scholars' Cantata.

" Nature's voices" spoke eloquently to a crowded congregation irk the Stockton Heath Wesleyan Church on Sunday afternoon. It was a charming sacred cantata rendered by the scholars on the occasion of the Sunday School anniversary, which always proves an epoch in the religious life of the district.

The rendition was perfect and great praise was due to the scholars for the talented way in which they did their respective parts. The recitations were finely delivered. The soloists, who charmed with their singing, were E. Hough- ton, D. Speakman, and L. Penketh. Mr. J. B. Baxter presided and gave a most interesting address on "Flowers and their lessons." His remarks were greatly appre- ciated by the seniors, as well as by the juniors. The arrangements in connection with the can- tata were skilfully executed by Mr. Penketh, who conducted all the services with his usual ability. Mr. J. L. White gave great assistance as organist. The special preacher for the day was the Rev. Thomas Miller, formerly of Warrington; and now of Sheffield, who is always welcome amongst Stockton Heath Wesleyans. He preached morning and evening. The choir once more did excellently in the anthems. Stainer's "Sing a song of praise" was well rendered at the morning service. A plea:sing feature was the quartet by the Misses Webb, Mr. Whitfield and Mr. Cliff a. The even- ing anthem was Dr. Clark Whitfield's "0 praise God in His holiness," and the solo was finely expressed by Mr. T. Brereton. The collections, which were devoted to the Sunday school funds, amounted to .218.

PENKETH.

Excursion to Chester.—A party numbering 56 travelled to Chester on Saturday in two char-a- banes, via Delamere Forest, returning through Helsby and Frodsbam. The afternoon was spent in various ways, according to the tempera- ment of the members. .Some held a friendly bowling main, others invaded Messrs. Dickson and Sons' nursery,.the remainder enjoying a sail up the Dee. Tea was taken at Williams' Cafe, after which they all made a tour of the city. Home was reached at 10.10, after a most enjoy- able day.

Death of Mr. Davenport.—Mr. Davenport, of St. Mary's-road, died on Tuesday morning, the remains being placed in the Society of Friends' "God's Acre," Meeting House-lane, on Thurs- day at midday. He was one of the founders of the Warrington Co-operative Society, and for some time found employment there. He left to take over the control of Arpley Station. Born in Scotland-road, Warrington, he lived in Pen- keth the greater part of his life. For the last eight years he was an invalid.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
GENIUS OF METHODISM. 'A Wesleyan Minister's Views.

The Liverpool District Synod held a public meeting on Wednesday night, when Mr. J. G. Withinshaw presided over a good attendance, and addresses were given by the Revs. E. F. P. Scholes and A. J. Costain. The Chairman said it was a great pleasure to have such a gathering of ministers. It was not often they had a Synod in this district. The Rev. E. F. P. Scholes spoke on "The Genius of Methodisng." Methodism brought men and women under its teaching until a great brotherhood of the Church was formed. Viewed side by side with other denominations, it held a principal position in the work of the religious world. Its methods and systems were advocated and adjusted for the material benefit of the workers especially. The itinerant system, for instance, was good for the ministers and also the congregations, the latter benefiting by the change of thought, therefore broadening one's views, and the ministers getting the advantage of a change of scene, which relieved the constant brain pressure, thus helping to retain the health of body and mind. The circuit system was really a mutual aid from the top to the bottom. Methodism was an antidote to suer- clotalism. We did not believe that this Gospel of ours is reserved for a certain section only, but universal. It was evangelical and evangelistic. No other church went so closely into open-air work. Class meetings were part of the system to develop the spiritual life of all those who accepted the Gospel. The Rev. A. J. Costain also spoke.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY,

,GREAT SAN KEY.

Band of Hope Concert—A large number of people gathered in the Sankey Schools to wit- ness the Band of Hope's last concert for the

season. The children had been well traified by Miss Clarke, and an interesting programme in- cluded a humorous recitation, "The butcher's boy," given by Master H. Goulden, and an operetta, "Old woman in the shoe," in which 50 children acted. Action songs, Our night out" and "Old soldiers," received encores when given by four girls and five boys respectively, and on the whole an enjoyable evening was spent.

MR. E. BONNELL IN AUSTRALIA.

EXPERIENCES " DOWN UNDER."

Mr. E. Bonnell, a plasterer, who formerly worked for Mr. William Furness, has written the following letter to that gentleman "Stanley," Glenmore-street, Naremburn, N. Sydney, N.S.W., Australia, Monday, March 30, 1914. Dear Sir,—

I have been doing .splendidly, and under no .circumstances could I wish to have done better. Work has been i&indant this last eight months and, needless to say, I have made the best of it. I and Mr. Bonnie, my partner, have now two months' work to go at, and prospects of more. Of course, the winter is now upon us, and we have already had a taste of it. We left our work on Friday night until Monday. We had a rainstorm and cyclone on Saturday, and when we got to work on Monday morning we found both houses partially demolished. The iron roof of one house was carried right away in the bush and also the chimneys. The light- ning had struck the other cottage at the back and cut the brickwork right in halves. The builder had the houses insured, but will receive nothing owing to a clause in the policy, which states succintly,. "Payment is made only in cases of fire, accidents and the like, and other specific causes not attributed to an act of the Almighty God." This builder comes under the latter, and therefore stands to lose one hun- dred and fifty pounds. The houses practically want ,replastering, and all the plumber's iron pipes were twisted like so much wire. The cost of plumbing in this class of house is fifty pounds (labour and material). These cyclones spring up without a moment's warning. You have a beautiful cloudless sky and the sun shin- ing brilliantly. Suddenly it goes as black as night. The wind comes in faint puffs. The rain falls in torrents and then follows the hurri- cane, spreading death and disaster in its wake. The Australians say they get these hurricanes every March, and they settle the weather for the winter. It rained for fourteeln hours with- out ceasing, and fourteen inches of. a fall was recorded for Sydney. All the water and gas Pipes were laid bare in the streets, the latter being very badly made.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
"Time for a Move."

It was quite an experience fOl• myself and I family, and my wife declared it was time we I were making.a move for old England. I am expecting to be under canvas this Easter at the seaside with my family. I purchased a tent, 12ft. x 10ft., and we often camp for the , week-end at the different beaches around Syd- ney- I hope in a week or two to be able to send you a pliotel,)f our camp life. We are all in Splendid health, although my wife finds the heat terribly trying at times, and she has by no means relinquished her determination to get home this summer about June. She has taken a great dislike to everything Australian, espe- cially the unconventionality of the people here. I have made no arrangements for returning home as yet, but I think my wife's wishes will be predominant at the finish. You hear of large wages being paid to all classes of artisans out here, but there is not 94 in every 100 men getting .24 per week. I have removed to another address, a Emir-roomed cot- tage, and my rent is now 188. per week, instead of 135., for the same size of house. The men strike for an advance of is. per day, they get it; the builder naturally wants a larger price for his houses, and the landlord, with business-like acumen, puts it on the rent. I fail to see bow he could do otherwise, unless, like Mr. Lloyd George, he gives something for nothing, which 15 just as unreasonable as it is impossible.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
Strike Drawback.

This strike business is Australia's great draw- back. One never knows where it is going to break out next. The slaters and tilers are now out for 14s. per day. The reason is not far to seek. The Railway Commissioner, recently raised the train and tram fares, so out came all the allied trades for is. per day extra, to meet extra cost of travelling to and from his work. A Labour Government, returned to powe on the promise of making huge profits on State- controlled works, to be used for reducing the rates, &e. Oh, the irony of it all"; the worker cuts off his nose to spite his face. To speak of " this to the average Australian worker would'be simply courting disaster. One can "only wait and see." It passed on to him as a consumer. We have been without beef for three weeks owing to the butchers striking for 10s. per week more and 48 hours weekly instead of 54. :They accepted the masters' offer of ss. per week ad- vance and 49i hours per week. We are now paying as much for meat here as we dig in Warrington, and in a country like Australia, where you could buy half a sheep for ss' six years ago. The slaters and tilers are still on strike, and in a week or two the entire build- ing trade will be held up, and I think my de- parture for England will depend on how long the strike lasts. Please remember me to old acquaintances. I read with great interest the controversy re raising of rents in Warrington and district. 6 Houses in Sydney that were Bs. per week six years ago, are now 18s. d. MV wife inquired after one that was vacant, and she had an inter- view with the owner, who told her the same house was only Bs. per week, sand lie now wanted 18s., after doing some slight repairs. It is quite common for three families to be living in a four-roomed cottage.

E. BOI'sTNELL.

LINES FROM LYNINI.

We are to "May Queen !" This is the latest decision: The committee are now on the hustle. They have to do three months' work Compressed into the space of three weeks They are hustlers and will do it. Who will be the May Queen? The lasses at the Grammar School Are all agog with excitement over the selec- tion. What is happening in the Council? Two sudden resignations look ominous. Thus we are faced with another by-election. 'Mr. F. Ingham is going strong in Heatley Ward. He should top the poll at the election a week hence.

Sleepers Out.—On Wednesday night the Llinni Pollee made a raid on the . brickworks at Statham, with the result that two tramps were charged on Thursday morning, before Mr. S. Davies, with sleeping out. One was sent to gaol for seven days and the other for 14 days.

For the Blind.—The Lymm Congregational Church have this week been endeavouring to raise funds for the blind. Aliss Egerton, a member of the church bau been actively en- gaged in collecting on behalf of the fund, and up to the present her efforts have been very satisfactory.

Synod Sermon.—ln connection with the Liver- pool District Synod of Wesleyan Preachers, which had been held in Warrington during the week, a serviPe was held in the Lymm Wesleyan Church on. Wednesday evening, when the Rev. S. Derham Robinson, of Lancaster, was the preacher.

Special Council Meeting.—At a special meet- ing of the Lymm District Council on Monday evening there were present: Councillors War- burton (chairman), Lockyer (vice-chairman). Gotobed. Groves, Kershaw, Critchley and Cow- per. The principal business was the classifi- cation of roads in the district.

Primrose Leaguers.—The annual meeting of the Lymm Primrose League was held at the Conservative Club on Monday evening. The balance sheet showed a deficiency of ,22 128. 6d. The following were re-elected officers : Mrs. W. Teter Ilylands, honorary treasurer; Mrs. Callen- der, honorary secretary; and Miss E. Moorhouse, assistant honorary secretary.

Sudden Death.—With tragic suddenness a respected resident from the Rush Green dis- trict passed away on:Wednesday, in the person of Mr. Isaac Thomason, at the age of 55 years. Re had been ailing for about two years, but half an hour befOre his death he was working in his garden, in which he took a great pride. The funeral will take place to-day. •

Gas Undertaking.—The scheme of supplying gas to Broom Edge, an undertaking of the Lymm Urban District Council, is proceeding satisfac- torily. Immediately after having laid the pipe under the Burford-lane Bridge, unexpected obstacles were encountered in the way of old sewerage pipes, &c., so that the work was some- what hampered, but now having passed that dis- trict rapid progress is being made.

Liberal Sccretary.—At a meeting of the Lymm Liberal Association on Wednesday night a letter was read from Mr. Barker, the secretary, setting forth his intention of resigning the secretary- ship. The meeting would not consider the resignation, believing that it was not in the best interests of Lymm Liberalism to lose so energetic .a worker, and a deputation was sent to Mr. Barker, who promised to continue in the office.

Ex-Sergeant Hon ou red.—The respect and esteem, in which ex-Sergeant Washington, of Lymm, was held by his colleagues in the Con- stabulary was responsible for a presentation of a clock, with a suitable engraving, at a Divi- sional Parade at Altiincham Police Station on Thursday. Superintendent Sutherland, who made the presentation on behalf of the police of the Altrincham Division, spoke in high terms of the ex-sergeant's 33 years' service, which had been a credit not only to' himself, but the Cheshire Police Force. Mr. Washington, in reply, said that the clock would indicate to him the regard in which he was held by his fellow- policemen. He thanked them all heartily for their kindness.

P.M. Anniversary.—Success waited upon the Lymm Primitive Methodist Sunday School anniversary held on Sunday. A pleasing and encouraging fact was ilat each service was attended by large congregations; whilst at the afternoon service extra accommodation had to be requisitioned. The special preacher was Mr. J. W. Renison, of Hartley College, Manchester, and his able discourses were followed with great interest. In the afternoon a special children's service was held- under the presidency of Mr, G. L. Welford, when the hymns and recitations rendered by the scholars called for much praise. The lessons at this service were read by two of the children, Master A. I'riestley and Master G. Davies. The celebrations were continued on Monday night, when a well-attended tea party and children's evensong was held, Mr. H. Champ presiding. Numerous pleasing items were.ren- dered by the scholars, and the Chairman gave an address upon Sunday School effort. The financial result of the anniversary was very satisfactory, for ..t29 was collected, of which amount the children brought in ,t 9. There was an excess over the previous year of about ,tB. Amongst those who rendered assistance at the tea were the following: Mrs. Champ. Mrs. Ed- wards. Mrs. E. Moss. Miss E. Welford, Miss M. Clare, Mr. G. L. Welford. and Mr. Chainn. Mr. E. Moss was congratulated on his splendid work as secretary.
