Mr. F. Ingham's Candidature.

The by-election in the Heatley Ward occa- sioned by the resignation of Mr. S. Davies has now resolved itself into a straight fight, for, as

Councillor Dixon joined the Council on April sth last year. Although his service has been short, it has been marked with earnestness and diligent attention to the Council's work. QUEEN RESURRECTED.

The Festival Arranged For At Last Moment.

CROWNING ON WHIT THURSDAY. Despite the fact that life had been found extinct and the "body" of the Lymm May Queen revels had been laid to rest, it has sud- denly jumped into life again and is actually rushing about engaged in cramming three months' work into three weeks! A resurrection meeting was held on Thursday night at the Fox Tavern, when the Treasurer of the old Commit- tee (Mr. Hudson) .presided. He said he was responsible for the meeting being called. It was with great soriow that he heard the old*committee had decided to dis- continue the festival, for, personally, he always looked forward to the event for the children's sake. His idea was to rim the festival On a much smaller scale, so as to get the expenses under .t5O.

In answer to a,lady, he Chairman said the festival would be held as usual on Whit Thurs- day if they decided that one should be held. it was only a short time, but it could be done if all on the committee would work very hard, with the one object in view, of giving he chil- dren a good time.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
LENGTHY DISCUSSION.

During a lengthy discussion, in which all were in favour of the continuance of the festival, the question of the children's tea was raised, and the Chairman stated that if they agreed upon the revival of the May Queen Revels perhaps they could have the tea within a month of Whit Thursday, hut it would depend upon the funds. If they took out the shopkeepers from the list of donations there was only :ots left.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
FESTIVAL TO BE HELD.

Ultimately a resolution setting forth "that the festival be held" was proposed by Miss liazlehurst, seconded by Mr. W. Copeland, jun., and unanimously carried.

A letter was read from Councillor Gotobed, in which he stated he would be pleased to see the festival held, but very much doubted the wis- dom of crowding three months' work into three weeks. If you do decide to go on, concluded the letter, my subscription, which has been re- turned, will again be at your service, but I fear I cannot offer my personal services.

GRAPPENH ALL.

METHODIST BAZAAR.

The Concluding Day.

The opening ceremony on Saturday, the con- eluding day of the Independent Methodist Bazaar, was marked with enthusiasm. There was general regret that Mrs. Long, of Thelwall, was unable to attend, but Mr. F. W. Monks was welcomed- as her substitute.

Mr. C. E. Parker presided, and supporting him were Mr. and Mrs. F. W. Monks, Miss D. Monks, -Mr. B. a Woods, and Couuoillor Henshall.

THE CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH.

Mr. C. E. Parker said it was with pleasure he took part in the opening ceremony that day, a pleasure which was deepened by the presence of Mr., Mgs. and Miss Monks. They had all been taken round that beautiful chapel, and what had impres,sed them was the splendid way in which the money had been laid out. The chapel was a credit to any denomination; it was a thing of beauty, and ,an adornment to the, neighbour- hood. When he had promised to preside at that ceremony he asked himself what he knew about the Independent Methodists, and he found he knew absolutely nothing. He felt ashamed of himself and he asked his friends—" What are these Independent Methodists?" Ire did not know anything about the details of their reli- gion, or for what grave reason they became a separate section and broke away from the old Methodist stock. However, he was not ashamed of his ignorance now, beemise, after all, they would acknowledge that those differences which appeared to be so vital to their forefathers, so vital as to bring about a split in their Church, had sank into comparative smallness when com- pared with the vital things that constitute a Christian Church to-day.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
Churches All One.

In all essential and important matters the Christian Churches were one, and in so far as that branch of the Church fulfilled the teach- ings of Christ, lived truly and lovely, helped the needy, lifted up the fallen, and brought light and help, moral and spiritual, into the lives of those who came under its influence, it was doing its noble work, for those were the important things that made all Christians one. (Hear, hear.) He was pleased to do his share in the effort, and trusted their church would be the centre of life from which would radiate the helping and healthy influence to all around. He also trusted they would be a working and active Church. He had hoped that a lady would have been present to say the necessary things about the bazaar; a duty which he always felt quite incompetent to do. (Laughter.) While they all regretted the absence of Mrs. Long, they agreed they could not have a better substitute. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140516_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. MONKS' COMMENTS.

...-... - Mr. Monks said that he was glad to be pre- sent because he looked upon efforts of that kind as praiseworthy, and if by acting in any way one could help forward a good work it was one's duty to do it. Some of his friends told him he was not a Christian, but he must have a certain proportion of Christianity about him, or he would not have been there in the position of the opener of the sale of work. During the last few years in his connection with the Unitarians he had come to the front in various ways, and although at times they did not want those positions, it was one's duty to accept them. One could not look forward to the future at times without feeling despondent at the future of religion generally, but occasions like that did make one feel that the prospect was not altogether dark or hopeless. In declaring the sale open he wished them every success. ' _

. _ Councillor Henshall proposed, and Mr. Platt, of High Legh, seconded, a vote of thanks to the Chairman and Mr. Monks, to which both responded. The effort realised .k 224.

MOORE,

Synod Service.—A special Synod service was held in the Wesleyan Chapel, Moore, on Wed- nesday evening, when the Rev. W. Parkinson, of the Liverpool Mission, was the preacher. There were also present the Rev. Thomas Witter, of Whitchurch, and the Rev. W. Griffiths, of Severn Valley. A large congregation assembled and the preacher secured their deep interest by a thoughtful and milst helpful discourse on the subject of the "Rich young ruler." The choir singing contributed considerably to the success of the service.

Late Mr. James Banner.—A very well-known and respected figure in Moore, in-the person of Mr., James Banner, passed away on Sunday evening, at the Chapel House. Mr. Banner, who was in his 69th year, had been in failing health since December last. Besides carrying on business as a coal dealer and general carrier, he had been caretaker at the Moore Wesleyan Chapel for 34 years. He took more than the usual interest in his duties at the chapel, being himself an active worker and a Sunday school teacher right up to the time of his last illness. The funeral, which was of a quiet nature, in accordance with the wishes of the deceased, took place at Daresbury Parish Church on Wednes- day aftepioon, and was preceded by a short but impressive service in the Wesleyan Chapel, Moore, which was conducted by Mr. Davies, who is leader of the Young Men's Class, in which Mr. Banner was keenly interested. Six mem- bers of the class acted as bearers.

TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

During the last few years crude oil has re- placed coal as a locomotive fuel on many of the railroads of Texas, Oklahoma, and Louisiana, and, in less degree, in some of the other states. More than 1,200 oil-burning locomotives are in use on the Southern Pacific and more than 800 on the Santa Fe. It is estimated that about 32,000,000 gallons of oil were used for fuel by the railroads of the United States in 1912, and that the total mileage of railroads operated with oil is now in the neighbourhood of 30,000.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
Welshman 120 Years Old.

... The oldest Welshman in the world is Mr. Thomas Morris, who lives at Westernville, Nebraska. Although he is now an American citizen he was born a subject of George 111., at the little village of Berriew, Montgomeryshire, on January 15, 1794. He is therefore 120 years old. His father was an agricultural labourer, and died when the boy was three years old. Morris was apprenticed to a cobbler, and fol- lowed his trade in this country until 1871, when, at the age of 77, he emigrated to America. The old man is very proud of the fact that he has lived in three centuries. Morris can still walk with the aid of a stick and see with the aid of spectacles.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
Saving by Slot Machine.

Budapest is the first city in the world to set up slot machine savings banks. The machines are arranged for the receipt of two coins, the crown, worth about twenty cents, and the 20- filler piece, worth four cents. They return a ticket for each coin deposited, and these tickets bear interest from the date of their issuance. The rate paid varies with the current bank rate and ranges from three to four per cent.; 110 tickets may be exchanged for a bank book. At present there are fourteen machines in opera- tion in Budapest. The total deposits for the first year have amounted to £2,639.

Glass Cooking Utensils Promied. A New York glass manufacturer is experi- menting with the making of cooking utensils of glass. Already glass percolators and stewpans have been made which, although they rest on the flames, give no evidence of cracking, and have proved heat-resisting and non-expansive. These utensils are apparently not affected in any way by the intense heat under them or by the contrasting temperatures of the articles which they contain. Glass is for many re.asons superior to either enamel or aluminium, being affected by none of the acids or alkalis formed or used in cooking and not subject to the in- sanitary cracking which is characteristic of most enamelled ware.—From the April Number of " Popular Mechanics Magazine."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
Highest Railway in the World.

i! In no other way can the visitor to Peru view such magnificent scenery or experience in a day ' such varied climatic conditions as by making a trip of 138 miles over the highest railway in the world, says "Peru To-day." The standard gauge Central Railway climbs without racks from sea level at Callao to pierce the Andes through the Galera tunnel at an elevation of 15.665 feet. dropping thence to Oroya on the Atlantic slope at 12.180 feet. There are 106 miles of steady four per cent, gradient, sixty tunnels. thirteen "switch-backs," and sixty- seven bridges, including the "Infiernillo" (Little Hell), spanning a deep gorge between vertical faces, the train emerging from one tun- nel only to dash into that opposite. The Moro- cocha branch crosses the range at the great ele- vation of 15,865 feet.

Insurance Company's Novel Scheme.

. __........ The introduction of a system to secure longevity among its policy holders by the New York Equitable Insurance Company, by the es- tablishment of bureaux where medical advice may be obtained free, may be considered by the leading English insurance companies (says the "Standard"). It is, however, felt that the establishment of such a system in this country might not be used to any profitable extent by insured persons or welcomed by medical men. Friendly societies, too, are of opinion that such a scheme would not be feasible. Mr. Alexander Lawson, manager and secretary of the Gresham Assurance Society, said that the scheme had proved popular in the United States, and had been watched with much interest by English life assurance companies. So far nothing in that direction had been done here, and he did not know of imy British company that was likely to try the experiment. He said that it might have many advantages.

Insects Hold Up Trains. , The earwig which held up the London Dis- trict Railway service recently, by short-circuit- ing the electric-Signalling apparatus, was un- wittingly emulating the spider which on June 4, 1908, dislocated the traffic on the Irish Great Northern Railway for nearly two hours. The electric staff instrument failed, and ultimately the cause was traced to a spider which had lodged itself between the lever and the contact points. Last year a train was derailed on the London and North-Western Railway owing to a hedgehog having been caught between the point and side rails and preventing them from clos- ing properly. A. Mississippi steamer was re- cently stopped by an invasion of willow bugs, which made for the engine-room and clogged the engines; while a swarm of bees in December, 1912, took possession of- a South African railway station, 'drove off the passengers and officials, and prevented any trains entering until they took their leave in the evening.

• - -- I remember Archdeacon Pelham saying, "It is impossible to set any limits to a faithful min- istry." I thought of this as I read the short record of Winwick's venerable Rector, Canon Penrhyn, and his 62 years' ministerial career. Ooleridge was wont to declare that our clerical households formed ," the one idyll of modern life," the most perfect type of domestic peale, and a centre of civilisation in remote villages. Undoubtedly we are all greatly indebted to them, for, as one who had taken the trouble to compile a list pointed out some years ago, among the poets who were born in clerical homes are Ben Jonson, Fletcher, Marvell, Otway, Thom- son, Tickell, Cowper and Tennyson; among the historians, Alison, Fuller, Hallam, Robertson, and Froude; among the novelists, Jane Austen, Charlotte Brontë and her sisters, Mrs. Gaskell, and Charles Kingsley; among the essayists, Ad- dison and Hazlitt ; among the scholars, Casau- bon ; among the travellers, Arthur Young; among the men of letters who achieved an equal success in many branches of literature, Oliver Goldsmith, Samuel Taylor Coleridge and Matthew Arnold.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 16, 1914.

COMMON WORDS AND PRISES.

SPECIAL COMPILATION & REVIEW.

BY, AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT. When a local admirer of Warrington's worthy Mayor is constrained familiarly and affection- ately to exclaim "Good old Peacock," it is gener- ally understood that he expresses himself in quite a modern phrase; but a popular historian tells how a Pembroke man greeted Henry 11. Lt Gode olde Kynge," and how the slang was in. terpreted by his Majesty's esquire. The phrase "The man in the street," which speakers and writers nowadays find so useful, is by some supposed to have been coined by Ger- trude Compton Reade, niece of Charles of that ilk; but Emerson employed it in 1841, and it is on record that it was in use ten or a dozen years earlier still. Will a certain notorious and much-debated borough project be completed by midsummer? I don't think. That is another catch-word of vener- able age. Tom Pinch quotes it ("Martin Chuzzle- wit," chapter vi.) as having often been uttered by John Westlock, and East uses it in " Tom Brown's Schooldays" (chapter ii, second part).

" Got him on a string, you see," which, thanks to a musical comedy of the period, had a certain vogue perhaps % dozen years ago, has its equivalent in Bacon's " History of Henry the Seventh"; and " Pip,pip," which the modern street boy is supposed to have obtained from the pneumatic cycle horn, was penned by Gay in his " Trivia" wellnigh two centuries before that invention was thought of. At least twice as old is the word " nipper " as applied to a youngster. "At daggers drawn" carries one back to the days of the king maker; "As fine as fivepence" may be met with in very old books; and "By the Lord Harry" strikes one as having originated in the times of Henry VIII.

Now for a few Parliamentary sayings that have long been famous. Probably most "Ex- aminer" readers are aware that it was John Bright who so refetred to the "Cave of Adul- lam" that the phrase stuck. "England, the mother of Parliaments," is also his, as well as "muddling through"—among others that Mr. Chamberlain has been wrongly credited with.

_.„ Mr. Disraeli made effective use of" Peace with honour "—indeed, many of his disciples seem to have imagined that it was his "very own," whereas he might have got it either from " Coriolanus " (Act iii. scene 2), or Cromwell, or Burke.

In this twentieth century, Lord Rosebery has given us "clean slate" and "lonely furrow," and we have had Mr. Balfour with his precious "half-sheet of notepaper." Coming to mem- bers of the present Government, we have Mr. Asquith's "Wait and see," Mr. Lloyd George's "robbing henroosts," and, better than these, Mr. Winston Churchill's "terminological inex- actitudes."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
MISAPPLEED WORDS.

Note the utterances of any of our local men who speak often in public. You will find that all such have their pet words and phrases. One old clergyman of my acquaintance is sadly too fond of the word "absolutely." Another of my friends rings the changes on " consequently," and (strangely enough) " The only point about it is just here." The Mayor of a neighbouring borough makes extraordinary use of the word " even "; sometimes it occurs in such associa- tion as to be Dositively embarrasing to some of his hearers, but his Worship is wont to flow rerenely along in blissful unconsciousness of the effects unintentionally produced by his unfortu- nate trick of speech. There are people who after the same manner trot out the phrase "of course" when it is not, a matter of course at all. One of the most amusing sayings that have come my way is that of a tradesman who is given to retailing (among other things) the latest of sen- sational rumours affecting the character of some neighbour; he never fails to add, "Whether it is so, of course, I'll leave it; but that is what a party told me.".

By common consent, such words as purity, liberty, 'happiness, harmony, heaven, love, divine, hope, sympathy, nobility, radiance, honour, joy, faith, modesty, melody, splendour, adoration, eloquence, virtue, and innocence are among the most beautiful in the language; and it is well now and again to protest against the debasement and misuse of any such. Modern slang has played havoc with some of our fine old words. A simple illustration sufficeth to in- dicate how serious is the degradation. In the course of his description of Paradise, Dante refers to "the lauighoor on. those blooming shores," but one may well hesitate to quote it because of the perpetual modern misapplication of the particular adjective. In his "Fiction, fair and foul," Ruskin, whose sense of the fit- ness of words all must allow, writes: "Corrupt language is that gathered by ignorance, invented by vice, misused by insensibility, or minced and mouthed affectation, especially in the at- tempt to deal with words only half undestood." He proceeds to elaborate and to give instances. Here, however, is something that may better serve the present purpose: A man of the period asks a lady to go in to Supper in this way: "Any one taking you to feed; shall we toddle down together?" The lady answers, "Right 0!" "Well," he continues, "we'd better barge along at once." People speak of a "good sort," of a " rotter," of a "waster," of "bounders," and of "wrong 'uns "; say that this Or that natural thing is a "freak," or that., it is "quite all right," and "ripping." They say that a beau- tiful thing is " a dream," or use "absolutely," instead of "very." They call that of which they. approve "topping," and what they dislike "weird," and pronounce a person " impbssible " who is so little "impossible" that he is an actual fact.

One who happens to dislike the weather for the time being speaks of. it as "rotten "—a lazy and most disagreeable mode of expression if one gives it a moment's thought. To use a word which I heard the other day, all this is heart- rendering. However, this brief consideration of the sub- ject as a whole makes one wonder what it feels like to, wake some morning to find that a phrase which one happened to coin on the previous day has hit the popular fancy, is being repeated all over the country, and bids fair to become • per. manently embedded in the language.

THE WORLD OF LETTERS.

New Books.

The Economics of Enterprise. By 11. T. Daven- port. London: Macmillan and Co., Ltd. 10s. net.

This work is by the Professor of Economics in the University of Missouri. It is a restate- ment, and in large part a reconstruction, of economic principles, greatly at variance with orthodox theory. The author conceives himself, he says, in questions of economic theory to be "in essentials rather a conservative than an innovator." Whether his readers will agree with him in this we are not at all sure. But of this, however, we are certain, that many of them will be grateful to Professor Davenport for the fresh and hopeful light which he has thrown on the thorny and complicated problems of Economics. And though he "disclaims all theoretical sympathies with the Socialists," we think that some of his readers, at least, will feel, as we do, that he has exposed many of the well-known, and some less generally recognised, iniquities of , "Competitive enterprise" with quite as much Phis and force as the Socialists themselves would do, and has given as much ground for the belief that competitive enterprise in its present form cannot last for more than a comparatively short period. Professor Davenport commences his analysis f mmp eotet vne orderenterprise s a -With the fact that the pecuniary order. "Mainly, under present conditions," he says, "we pro- duce for the market, for exchange, despite the coo fact that a few generations ago the contrary was the truth. And at present we produce in the I larger part for a competitive impersonal world- market. This is the era of free individual ' initiative under private property for private I gain. So far, indeed, is this the truth that I even combination and monopoly may be regarded as merely secondary aspects of com- petition, and of individual initiative. Strike this fact of competition at its very centre of I tone, and we discover that we are in a regime of I price. Money is the focusing point of modern business affairs. It is the standard of values simply because in a society producing for ex- change it is the one established intermediate commodity. Therefore, as medium of exchange, it is the standard of immediate and of deferred payments. Through credit, the money economy lays hold upon even the distant future I Much money is power over many things. Money is the standard of value in the sense that all values of all exchangeable things are expressed. ,in terms of it. • And this holds, not only of all commodities and services, but of all incomes and of all capitals. The capital of a banking house, or a factory, or a railroad -company, is not a congeries of tangible things, but a pecuniary magnitude—s° many dollars. All economic 1 comparisons are made in money terms, not in terms of subsistence, or of beauty, or of artistic merit, or of moral deserving." . .

—Holding this view that the central fact of the present order of society is this regime of price, it is natural that Professor Davenport should disagree with the current definitions of Economics. He suggests this, as being nearer ,the facts than any other, "Economics is the ' science that treats of phenomena from the standpoint of price:" Following this defini- tion, all the problems of Economics become price problems. The problem of distribution, for ' instance, is dealt with in this way: "All dis- tribution takes place as a price process. Each particular product is a price item and is due to the joint employment of different human agents and of a wide range of instrumental or property items—land, Machines, raw materials, patents, franchises, and the like. The price of the joint product—not the joint product as such—is the amount which is to be divided, distributed, among the various co-operating factors; and each of these factors receives its distributive share as a price queptuoi. Not only, then, are the forces and processes under which and by which the sale price, of the product is adjusted and determined to be studied as particular pro- blems in the theory, but also the distributive shares derived from it are equally price problems." Professor Davenport then proceeds through the whole field of Economics, all the problems being treated from this price standpoint. They are thus all linked up, and become parts of one comprehensive system. Professor Davenport's analyses are most keen and searching in char- acter. We think that one among many of the ser- vices which this book will perform will be that of showing that what is called the " unearned " increment in profit is Much larger—enormously larger—than is generally supposed. Professor Davenport has challenged all the established concepts of the science and we think it will not be without some difficulty that his objections will be met, if they can be Met at all, and he has given ,to progressive social workers that ultimate basis in economic theory which, as he says, is theirs by right of truth.

THE EX-SOLDIER'S GUN.

Sensational Shooting Affray at

Burtonwood.

A REMAND FOR A WEEK.

Looking pale and bewildered, a prisoner, John Weston, ex-soldier, 42, Jackson-street, Bur- tonwood, appeared before Councillor 11. Roberts other :ail t, ehr o e personslo a c e caisi with hf Court,intent Warrington, on Mon- d eo nt it gto Emma Wood and do themn bodily harm.

----- Supt. Foster stated that on Saturday some boys, including Isaac Rothwell, were teasing the prisoner's dog, and making it bark. Prisoner, who was under the influence of drink, told the boys to go away, and said if they did not he would bring his gun and shoot them,

-- - He went into the backyard and re-appeared with the gun. The boys scampered off, and some 49 yards away were standing Mrs. Emma Wood, 21, Fairclough-street, and her daughter Hannah, aged 14 years; Mary Adamson (14), Winifred Adamson (4), and Teresa Adamson (18 months), of 2.5, Fairclough-street.

• The Gun Fired. Prisoner, continued the Superintendent, walked 29 yards and placed the gun against one of his legs and discharged it in the direction of those people and also in the direction of a boy Who was running. is The shots struck the people, and Mary Adam- son, who was 14 year of age, received no less than 14 shots. Mrs. Wood, who is at present in delicate health, will, it is feared, suffer in health through the incident, and she would not be fit to come to Court for another fortnight. P.C. Moulding, who arrested the prisoner, said that Weston said he only did it to frighten "young Ikey Rothwell." "I told him if he did not go away I would shoot him. I fired in the air to frighten him. I thought the gun was loaded with a blank cart- ridge," he added. The officer said that nine more live cartridges were found in the house, but no blank ones were discovered.

In reply to the charge accused aid, "I had no intent whatever."

Woman's Condition Serious.

Dr. Valentine said he attended all the injured persons, and Mrs. Wood's condition was serious.

Literary Gleanings.

BEAUTIFYING BRICKS AND MORTAR.

The movement for brightening by floral aid our drab, congested urban centres, consistently advocated in these pages for many years, is be- ginning to make satisfactory progress. Through the courtesy of Councillor Win. Bashforth de- tails have been forwarded of the City of Sheffield flower garden and window-box competitions. These competitions were originated last year on the suggestion of Councillor Bashforth, and were so successful that they are to be repeated this year on a more extended scale. The total number of entries was made up as follows: Flower gardens, 469; window boxes, 531; and inside window plants, in special areas, 22. Last year two grants of £lOO and £2O respectively were voted by the council from the Corporation tramway profits. The council were so pleased with the results that this year they have voted a sum of £2OO for prize money and expenses. London is also taking a keener interest in im- proving the outlook of some of the blocks of stern and forbidden-looking buildings. The Marquis of Northampton is supplying seeds for the window boxes in the tenements on his Lon- don estate. A pleasant array of bloom will be furnished from some of the reliable One and All seeds, for which his lordship has placed a very large order. This noble landowner's kindly in- terest in his tenants might advantageously be copied by other Metropolitan landlords.—" Agr- icultural Economist."

THE GREATEST CITY STATE.

"The Doges of Venice," by Mrs. Aubrey Richardson, tells the fascinating story of a State that from beginning to end endured for 1,100 years—longer, that is to say, than the Roman Emperor. The purest and strictest oligarchy that was ever devised, it yet avoided the twin rocks of tyranny and revolution. Sprung from the very smallest beginnings, it kept through- out its characteristic form as a city-State, and exercised on the history not of Italy alone, but of the whole world an influence decisive and ad- mitted. It is no wonder that Venice always ap- peals to the romantic side of the historian. It is no wonder that it has given rise to more pic- turesque writing than any other community_ with the possible exception of Athens—that ever existed. Did Venice succeed because of the character of her citizens or because of the char- acter of her constitution? Did she fail because the instrument of government was bad, or be- cause those who used the instrument were in- competent or dishonest? Mrs. Richardson ob- viously leans to the " personality " view of his- tory. In a sense she is right. Without the great Doges, Venice would never have been the place she was. But it still remain g true that it was her constitution that brought the Doges to the front, gave them the power to serve Venice, prevented them from using that power to serve thmselves. And the constitution of Venice and its working /must be set down to the character of the people. The folk of Venice had in them a sense of patriotism that the modern world does not know, that marches rather with the city States of the ancient world than with any- thing else. They had also, what Athens had, a dominant sense of liberty. There was nothing that Venice abhorred so much as the suspicion of tyranny. These qualities were at the root of Venetian government, but they existed in a peculiar form. There was in the city a sense of the picturesque, almost theatrical, which made both the patriotism and the hatred of tyranny peculiarly effective. That was what trans- figured Venice. The ceremonies and rituals of Government (such a thing, for instance, as the wedding of the Adriatic) were so devised as to keep the patriotism of the city at a high price. The barriers against tyranny were of the kind to appeal to an imaginative people. Above all, it was always possible for a great man with a great cause to gain the ear and fire the spirit of the. city. Such were the qualities which made Venice great. Those qualities had their defects. They led to a certain want of balance, a certain im- patience of control, and disorderliness of spirit. But so long as the Venetian character remained sound, the advantages outweighed the defects. It was when the national character began to decay that the instrument of government ceased to be effective. The decline of Venice was, in reality, as gradual as its rise. Its faults were precisely the faults that its constitution had fostered. The instrument had not changed very much. It was the men behind it who had changed. The changed people were choosing a different order of chiefs.

THE SECRET. 'Twas a chubby little tomtit With a pudgy little cheek— And his heart was full to bursting In hip eagerness to speak— For he'd found out a secret, Which he did not want to keep, And he meant to tell the robin Just as soon as day should peep.

So all night he chirped and fluttered, Nor one minute gave to rest, Until every other birdie Began stirring in its nest. And a drowsy toad beneath him Muttered, "What a fuss you make, Oh, you cruel, crazy tomtit, Keeping all of us awake !"

But the tomtit did not hear him, For he caught a burst of song. And he knew to what brave singer That sweet carol must belong. "'Tie the robin! 'Tis the robin !" And across the field he flew. " Robin ! Robin! Stop a moment, I have such good news for you.

"Spring is coming! Spring is coming! Yes, the brooklet told me so, And he heard it from the leaflets, Just a little while ago." But the robin kept on singing, "No," he said, "you silly thing, Listen to me, darling tomtit, Don't you know that I am spring?"

How Pot-Luck Photography May be Practically Abolished.

VISITS TO GRANNY.

One of the pleasantest memories of my younger days is that which I am 'going to tell you. _

Once, a long time ago, I stayed at Granny's, who lived-in a big tumble-down house standing on a narrow cobbled street. I recollect the cir- cular steps leading up to the front door, and the old-fashioned "link extinguisher" in the wall. There was a huge brass knocker on the front door, with a quaint Latin inscription on it. _ _

I 'remember, too—dimly, though—staggering along the cobbled street, clasping our big yellow cat which had got out of bounds.

The old garden wall passed high up along the back of the garden, with the spires of the min- Ater peeping over

The next time I went to Granny's was much older. AS I passed over the familiar cobbles I saw the old house again, a little older; a little more decayed, but with the knocker as bright as ever, and the link extinguisher, its cap cocked at a confidential angle towards the knocker. This second visit is quite easy to remember, for it is only about five years ago. Auntie met me At the door, and inside was Granny, in her big rocking chair by the dining. room fire. Here again was a little change—a little greyer, a little more bent, was Granny. I was presently taken up the back stairs to my room. I spent my holiday pleasantly enough, admiring the lovely stained glass win- dows in the minster on the river banks, and on rare occasions going out to a staid tea-party with my aunt. • •

I came away with such pleasant memories that I was overjoyed, three years later, to get a gracious letter from my aunt asking Mother to allow me to pay them a long visit in the sum- mer. The day came when I was to go, and I was all excitement and conjecture. Would my aunt be improved in her dress, or would she still be wearing that horrid yellow and brown bodice, as of yore? Would Ibe treated as a guest of honour, and be allowed to go up and down the front stairs now that I had pigtail and long skirts and fine kid shoes? Wasn't it possible that I should have a serviette at dinner, instead of keeping on my pinafore? Such thoughts came buzzing through my head as the train rushed along. I wondered vaguely if the five old ladies (all sisters) who lived at the next house would have tea with us, and if so, if I should be expected to kiss them. I sin- cerely hoped not, for the two eldest had whis- kers! _ _ .

At last we arrived, and I was being driven over the old familiar " kidneys" to the grey old house where Granny lived, and there, sure enough, were my old friends, the knocker and extinguisher, just the same, to all appearance, as ever. Out I jumped, paid the man, and ran up the steps. My aunt was out in the hall, but no sign, thank goodness, of any visitors. I saw at once that Auntie had on a new silk dress, and she looked quite nice as she held out her arms in welcome. « * * •

, It was just as I had hoped it would be. I was taken without more ado up the front stairs; the carpet was so soft that my footsteps made no noise. At the top another surprise awaited me. I was to have the best spare room to sleep in! Then, as I turned to thank my aunt, I saw she looked rather wistful and sad, and—l couldn't help it—l hugged her! And we became the best of friends. After removing the stains of travel I went in to see Granny, who was in bed not very well. As time went on I was made keenly aware that Granny was getting weaker and weaker. I hovered in And around the sick room, not taking any interest in anything but Granny's welfare. Then came a day I shall always remember. It was quite early, a Sunday morning in June, and I stole into Granny's room to look at her and feel her pulse, for some instinct—is it a nurse's?—seemed to draw me to the bed of sick- ness. The curtains of spotted muslin waved to and fro in the breeze—the sweet melody of the minster bells came in at the open window—the peaceful garden lay below, bathed in sunlight, and God's spirit radiated from the 'sweet, pale face on the pillow.

BY SILVER-STREET.

The amateur little knows the pleasures of his hobby until he uses an exposure meter. The light varies so greatly, at different parts of the day, or of the year, or in different situations, that it is almost impossible for a beginner, aye, and for many an old hand, to judge the ex- posure necessary to make a good negative. I had this very same advice given to me after three months' work in the dark, as it were. I friend showed me how to use Wynne's meter;! purchased one, and immediately my successes tripled. In fact, photography was now prac- tically a certainty, instead of being a mere "pot- luck" affair. Of course, ss. 6d. seems a lot for a little instrument very much less than a watch, especially to an amateur not 'overcharged with money; but it certainly means to many all the difference between successful work and utter waste. The money is very soon saved in material. And now I am glad to see a new edition of the Wynne's meter is being made. Just like a hunter watch, this little handy meter opens with the pressure of a small knob on the pendant. It is thus kept safer from light and from the moisture of the pocket, both of which invalidata the results. The price is 7s.

PORTRAITURE AT HOME.

Lecturing on portraiture at the Royal Photo- graphic Society Mr. Corke pointed out that an excellent plan for out of doors portraits was to work with sunlight on the face. The best effects are to be obtained an hour or two after sunrise, or the same interval before sunset; of course, it will usually be the latter. Though there will be a distinct shadow, the actual light will hot appear to be so hard.

Another hint gathered from the same lectnre was to place a large mirror inside the room and just in front of the window, not quite parallel with the floor. This reflects the light of the sky entering the window on to the ceiling, and so produces a good diffused light in the roam. He drew attention to the mistake often made of putting the sitter close to the window under the impression that this reduces the time of exposUre. The shadows are just as dark with the person close to the window as when he is some distance from it; while the more we take the sitter back into the room the more will the light diffuse round into the shadows, and the result will not be so black and white.—" Phot- ography and Focus."

WIDNES AND LATE LIBERAL AGENT.

The Liberals of Widnes have under consider- ation the opening of a subscription list for the dependents of Mr. Joseph Baskerville, the local agent of the party, whose death, a few days ago, removed a comparative youth of exceptional political promise, and one who had the esteem of friend and opponent in the Wid- nes Division. Mr. A. E. Calvert presided at a meeting of the Widnes Women's Liberal Asso- ciation on Saturday, and at his suggestion the members adopted a resolution cordially approv- ing of the proposal and expressing their own readiness to assist in collecting.

Sir Edward Russell, who addressed the meet- ing, said he must add his words of regret and condolence in reference to the death of Mr. Baskerville. He (Sir Edward) had met him several times, and had always ben struck with his enthusiasm and devotion to the cause. He heard that he was held in general esteem and affection as much by his own riolitical friends as by those who were not cf his own way of ' political thought. Liberals would testify their appreciation of what he had done and express, their sorrow at his death. The proposal of the. Chairman was one which, he was sure, would meet with general sympathy and lead to a suc- cessful accomplishment. •

ST. HELENS HOUSING CASE.

At St. Helens Police Court on Monday, the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. Turton) asked for the further adjournment of a summons by. the Health Committee against Mr. Alfred Griffin, or Prescot-road, calling on him to demolish a ruinous building, No. 27, Liverpool-street. He had submitted such a scheme, which had been ap- proved, and the work was going on. The fur- ther adjournment was granted.

WORK-A-DAY WONDERS.

V.-HELPING THE MINISTER'S WIFE TO SAVE MONEY.

"Of course, I went up to the Manse on the Friday as promised," said Pattie, "and when I got there I found all the preparations made for a real Diamond-dyeing day. The woollens for dyeing (they were mostly little frocks and things which were being 'made down to fit the chil- dren) had been nicely washed in soap-suds, and left damp, ready for the dye-bath--the silks were in another pile, and the linens and mixed wool and cotton in another. Then there were the different Diamond Dyes for wool and cotton all set ready, and the Diamond Dye Book of Directions left out in case we should want to turn to it.

" Mrs. Garrett pit herself and me into cotton overalls and we just set to work and Diamond- dyed and dried and pressed as busy as two bees —only stopping for meals. And if we proved one thing that day we proved this—that if you want to get any REAL satisfaction out of Home Dyeifig you must set to work in the Diamond Dye fashion.

I couldn't have thought it possible,' said Mrs. Garrett, that anybody would have helped me so much as you have to-day, Mrs. Prue. Why, you've helped me to save POUNDS, and I'm more than grateful. Do come up on Tuesday afternoon and have a rest and a quiet chat.' "Shouldn't I have liked to go! But a promise is a promise, and I had given my word to poor old Mrs. King, who lives at the end of our ter- race, that I would help her Diamond-dye her parlour curtains that very afternoon." WOMAN'S WORL

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

House Decoration. Museums and exhibitions serve curious pur- poses in these days. Bethnal Green, in the heart of London slumdom, has added " a 'good taste" room to its museum, with the laudable object of teaching the East End what to avoid in the way of inartistic home decorations. Other and more aristocratic localities might follow this example with benefit to the community at large. New York, on the other hand, has opened an ex- hibition which displays the bad taste of former generations in household arrangements and fur- niture. Its exhibits consist of all kinds of domestic horrors of the past. The French people have discovered that there is much virtue in black, and black and white combinations, for the interior decorations of houses. Such an effect sets off beautiful furniture and ornaments to their best advantage, and makes a superb background for choice toilettes and a display of jewels. Such a sombre note, however, is only possible in the large and lofty rooms of the keuses• of wealthy people. It has been intro- duced in some of the West End houses in Lon- don, but these, as a rule, are decorated in white, or a soft dove grey. For external decoration, London has no such system of painting whole streets or districts at one and the same time, as is enforced in Pari2. It paints itself when and how it likes, but there is fashion in this as in everything else, and every year there is a change of colour. This season Fiench grey is most effec- tively employed, but many odd effects are to be attributed to the influence of the gospel of colour, as preached by the Futurists.

Women Agridulturists. stoasteterkaicntged by. the a career re, but, of course al unr ed Many o ft the competencemla ennd, awirnei t han goarwivcaiudelaiur study and learning are necessary to the success- ful working of a farm. To provide such facili- ties, the lion. Rupert Guinness established, some three years ago, a school at Woking, where edu- cated women may obtain this knowledge and ex- perience. It is under the direction of a Cana- dian lady, for it aims at fitting women to be- come successful agriculturists in the Dominion, or in Australia, as well as at home, and the curriculum includes a practical knowledge of domestic work of all kinds. Women students join the demonstrations and other dasses held at an adjoining school for farming for young men. Both institutions are thoroughly up to ' date, and the training is the best of its kind in the country.

Watering Golf Greens. " The Hunstanton Golf Course, where the com- petition for the Ladies' Open Golf Championship took place this week, is famous for its greens, which are kept watered by a Merryweather port- able petrol-driven " Ravensbourne " pump. This draws its supply from the river Hun, and is capable of delivering 1,500 gallons in fine spray per hour from two distributing nozzles. When the course lies distant from the river, the water is obtained from a series of Abyssinian tube wells, which have been sunk by Messrs. Merry- weather, and by connecting these up with the pump, all the greens can be dealt with. A system of this kind is comparatively inexpensive and is well worth the attention of golf club com- mittees, when the course in question is much affected by dry weather in summer time.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
The Fashions of To-day.

There have appeared lately in the Press the most sweeping condemnations of present styles and fashions in women's dress. Such tirades clearly prove the ignorance of the writers on the clothes worn in former periods of our history. It must be conceded by any unprejudiced stu- dent of dress in the past that the lighter under- clothing worn by women to-day is from the hygienic point of view a vast improvement on the heavy garments of our grandmothers. sus- pended from their waists. Doubtless, the great weight they carried and the custom of com- pressing vital organs in order to attain the height of their ambition—an unnaturally tiny waist—accounted for the vapours and swoonings and delicate health from which they so generally

suffered. Any form of exercise, not to mention. games, was impossible in the clothes of three generations ago. Whilst some fashions may merit censure, the work-a-day dress of the streets, and of middle-class families, as a whole, was never saner, more sensible, and suitable, nor even prettier than it is to-day. Two Silhouettes.

Seldom in the history of fashion has there been such a medley of styles and periods and influences as at present, and never have fashions followed each other so swiftly as to-day. Twenty years ago, a new model for a mantle, for example, was used in a West End workroom two years following at the least, but nowadays a model is old at the end of six weeks. The re- sult of the introduction of such diverse styles, and the adoption of such swift changes is an infinite variety in raodels and modes for the season. In fact; it may be said that two dis- tinct silhouettes are to be seen, and, that each is favoured by leaders of fashion. The older of the two is the vague outline, in which the skirt of the dress is the least important component part, for it is hidden, or almost so, under a long full tunic, that is to be still longer as the season advances. The corsage is draped loosely, and the waist is altogether undefined, as far as an inward curve is concerned. The sides of the body are quite straight and there are no hips.

COMPLETE EXAMINER STORY.

CALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] THE HOUSE SURGEON.

EDGAR WALLACE.

I reached out of bed, and turning down OA ramp, tried to sleep. The knowledge of the fact that one has, by a single foolish action, placed him 12 on the verge of bankruptcY, is scarcely conducive to mental quiet, and it was only after what seemed to me hours of wakefulness that I sank into a fitful sleep. I could not have been dozing more than half an hour when a knock at my door aroused me. A knock at such an hour, could only mean one thing to a house surgeon, and getting out of bed, I opened the door to ad- mit, as I had expected, the night porter. " Case just come in, sir," he said tersely. " Dr. Thompson don't think he's likely to live." • " What sort of case," I asked, sharply, for even one liable to end fatally did not neces- sarily require my attention, ." he brought in and when nas ?" The porter was rather taken aback at my brusquen2ss, for amongst my subordinates, I believe, I bore a reputhtion for courtesy. " Brought in a few minutes since, sir." said the man. "Came in a cab with a police- man. 'Pears as how he was goin' home from his club, and passile down Holborn the horse bolted and came bang against a pillar at the corner of Chancery Lane, rent was thrown out of the cab and fell against the kerb." By this time I was ready, and after bath- ing my face to waken Inc I followed the por- ter down the dimly lighted corridor that co-A- fleeted. my quarters with the hospital, along the broad, stone-flagged central hall, with its many glass doors opening into various wards, till my guide stopped before No. 11, the ac- cident ward. I entered and softly closed the door behind me. Screened off from the rest of the ward, on an operating table, lay the form of a man, who was in evening dress, the coat of which and had been removed to allow the surgeon to commence operations. By his side stood the s Ini night nu_ went nearer who nodded a nearer to the table and looked into the face. Good God! Newton!—Newton whom I left a few hours before in the bloom of health—and now! For the moment I forgot our last interview when I had left my club, a ruined man, with the hann'iiug remembrance of N'ewton, a sympathetic smile on his florid face, making a bundle of the note and bills that I, in the true gambler's spirit, had plunged with to recoup the trifling losses of the precious 'evening. That sympathetic smile was part of his stock-in-trade, and was the result of long practice. Believing in that ancient fal- lacy, luck must turn, I had plunged and plunged, till I suddenly realise that I had tost every penny I had in the world. .. In that eveninri I had entered the nrivate

card room a comparatively wealthy min, and had left it with my year's salary mortgaged. I had read of such cases, but had always ac- cepted them with a grain of salt. For how, I argued, can a man continue, to play a losing game ? Why does he not desist when he finds luck against him? Even now I couldn't say why I hadn't; I only knew that I had played doggedly on, expecting my luck to turn. In the bitterness of the moment I had said some hard things concerning Newton's " luck," which I more than hinted, was as- sisted by the lucky one's dexterity. He had retaliated with words that had made me wince; he had told me that by such fools as I—those were his words—he managed to make a livinoo:. There were other stinging, maddening things he had said, such as only an educated man could have said, but now for the time I forgot his insults, forgot that he had ruined me, and only saw the poor battered form that lay awaiting my skin. "What are the injuries ?" I asked, regain- ing my composure. " The scalp wound that you an see," was Thompson's reply. I nodded; one could not help but see that ugly gash that stretched across the head from eyebrow to ear. " Any- thing else ?" ' I haven't looked," he answered. "I thought I'd wait till you came. The bleeding ceased soon after his arrival. I have had him washed up. Will you examine him ?"I took off my coat, and, rolling up my sleeves, proceeded to inspect the wound: It was, I could see at first glance, a seri- ous case. I ran my finger gingerly along the bare skull that lay exposed till I felt a little swell- ing beneath my fingers; unlike the ordinary bump, it was splintered aeross in two or three directions; I looked up and met Thompson's enquiring eyes. Fracture ?" was 'the question. " Yes," I answered slowly. A small fracture above the brain. Just see if there's anything else." . Carefully and tenderly Thompson passed his hands over the body and limbs of the in- sensible man: " a fractured rib," he mut- tered, half to himself as surgeons are wont to do. "That's nothing; face cut up, evidently from a sharp stoke—that's nothing, other limbs, seem all right. What are you going to ieid do ? This last remark was addressed to me. gave the wound another glance and then de ed. " Get the instruments ready for tre- panning," I said, " it must be done at once, or it will be too late. There is, •undoubtedly, a compression which must be removed." As my assistant and the nurses hastened noiselessly to obey my orders I was left alone with the inanimate form airym ostweaarldlouwsa go buesrtv er. , It was a scene that could not have' o imThpreesslotnhgesufficiently lit to allow the attendants to move about with- out injury to limb. The first faint streaks of the dawn were struggling through the win- dows at the further end of the ward, its ghastly light making the flickering night lamps a bilious yellow. This was no novel ex- perienee to me; I lived my life amidst these rows of sufferara.

The New Silhouette and the Pantelettes.

_ • The new silhouette, if it becomes general, will lead to a drastic change in corsets, for it goes back to the modes of 1870, and consists of a tight- fitting, one-piece dress, a perfectly plain corsage, which buttons down the front, as also does the skirt. It is a trying style to the average woman, and since it admits of less freedom of movement than the straight silhouette, it may never come into general use. At present it is the latest thing, and as such has its following.

The Chinese influence is said to be responsible for the revival of pantelettes, which remind one of the long white drawers, coming to the top of the shoes, as worn by little girls in 1830. The dress of the Chinese women is more rational and comfortable than any other feminine costume in the world, and it has not changed for hundreds sof years. It consists of wide, loose Pyjama trousers, below which are these pantelettes, and a long, handsome, loose-fitting coat.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
New Ruffles.

All kinds of small and dainty ruffles for the neck are to be seen for summer wear, and many of these could be made at home. Quite a good effect is obtained by using a pretty check silk ruche, edged with a single pleating of plain material and finished with long tassels. In any transparent material this idea may be carried out, or stripes may take the place of checks. Kilted black tulle frilling with cut edges, four and• a half inches wide, make a very light and pretty ruffle. A piece 'of ribbon velvet goes through the centre and forms long ends, on which blossoms to match the colour of the dress aze stitched. This looks equally well with a black or coloured toilette.

A Woman Lecturer and Traveller.

Only one white woman—Mrs. Herbert Bul- strode—has ever travelled through Mongolia, which is practically unknown to Europeans. Mrs. Bulstrode was a spectator of the religious ceremonies and rites at Lamasseries, never seen before by a white man or, woman. She took most interesting photographs of every place she visited, and of the inhabitants; some of the grimmest depict the prison life of Mongolia. In the autumn Mrs. Bu'strode intends to go to Canada on a lecturing tour, after which she will return to continue her researches in the Far East.

Even the sweet scented flowers that stoot amidst the queer shaped instruments on the nursing sister's table were so inseparably as- sociated with the internal decoration of a hospital, that a visit to the fairest garden amidst the most picturesque surroundings, I had no other effect than to recall the scenesl of suffering that I had left behind. They were so familiar too, those neat cots with the overhanging pulleys, and the little scriptural texts above each patient's head. I looked at Newton. How still he lay! To all intents and purposes dead. I had served a long and hard apprenticeship, and suffering and death had long ceased to cause me men- tal pain. There had been a time, when the sight of a blood-splashed knife had made me feel sick and giddy, and the memory of the patient's agonised cries had haunted me through the long hours of the night. These times had passed, but in passing they left few lines on my forehead, and -had given a to tinge of grey a. my hair as a souvenir. In spite of practice a feeling of pity came over me for this unfortunate man, who had pro- bably only a few hours to live. His " choker " collar and his white tie had been removed to allow of his breathing, and from the front of his dress shirt, crumpled and stained, a single brilliant winked and glittered. I turned my head with half a sigh, when almost at my feet, I saw what, in the dim light, appeared to be a sack. Methodi- cal, even in my abstraction, I stooped, with the intention of removing it to the outside of my ward. The moment my fingers touched it I real- ised that what I had taken for a sack, was a dust coat—evidently Newton's. if switched on the electric light under which his head had been placed: the coat was soiled with that mixture peculiar to London street accident—mud and blood. As I turned to put the coat with the remainder of the clothing, on an adjacent locker. sometl,,ng fel! from the pocket; it was a pocket-book. .I. bent down and picked it up, and was about to replace it, when, like a flash, I remembered that in this book were the bills and notes that Newton had won from me the same evening. For a moment I stood irresolute. Here lay a man on the point of death, a man who 'had as good as confessed that he lived on .the wits of fools—such fools as I. He had not, I knew, a single relative in the world, no plying heirs io raise awkwaid questions. Halt unconsciously, I press:d back the clanp, and opened the flap. Yes, there sums and crisp, lay the little roll of notes and papers: in two or three places I could see my ‘ signature, Donald Fraser, written at the foot of divers bills that spelt ruin to me. Met a fool I had been! And what a fool I was. Within niy grasp lay all that I desired, and who was to know `7. lie had left the club at the same time as I. I knew there was only one small chance of his recovering; if by that chance he lived, I could restore the money, and if he died—. After all, was not this money mine P Yes, I would take it. This enil-torle should be a lesson to me for all lire- time. I would never again touch a card.

Thompson would soon be returning, I re- fastened the clasp of the book, and, raising my eyes, I met the fixed gaze of—Newton ! Par a ma:.;:r.zxt I waz specelalers; the aliock cf -rtoounterine the glare of those eves that I

aaa tnougnt ciosea in insensibility, produced a momentary paralysis. For the moment I imagined that I had been speaking my thoughts aloud, but recovering. my mental equilibrium, my professional instincts came to my rescue. " You must remain perfectly quiet," I said in a subdued tone; " you have met with a bad accident." He did not speak, but his eyes rested for a second on the pocket-book, and then rose to my. face, into which, in spite of a determined effort, and for the first time in my life, a blush of guilt was creep- ing. He evidently noticed my distress, and mistaking the cause, he said faintly. all right, Fraser, don't trouble, I'm not afraid of your holding these things. I know , you are straight!" The blush deepened, and I tossed the ac- cursed thing that had made me forget duty, as a gentleman and a physician, on to the heap of clothing. If be was a sharper what was I, a thief? Hacl the tables been turned, and he, instead of I, been the loser, should I have considered his feelings ? I looked at Newton; he lay with half-closed eyes, breath- ing very quickly, and I could see he was laps- ing again into insensibility. Thompson at this moment came up with an apology for keeping me so long; he bore a tray contain- ing the instruments. The nurses who accom- panied him, placed the necessary waters and antiseptics in the various basins, and all was ready. Thompson looked curiously at the patient. " Has he regained consciousness ?" he asked, proceeding to divest himself of his coat, and fastening back his cuffs. " For a short time, yes," I answered. " He is half-couscienks now; these periods of hs-- cidity are not uncommon in cases of com- pression." I felt the pulse--:-it was normal; with a stethoscope I examined the heart, this too was sound. . "I shall give him chloroform," I said, by way of explanation, " and endeavour to ele- vate the splintered bone." Newton opened his ,Teyes,, "This nd;s Doctor flr,ohmoniopnsoent;o, the other; at last his enquiring gaze rested on Thompson. I said, think- ing to inspire him with confidence; "we are going to operate on you, there will be little or no pain—" He stopped me with an almost imperceptible movement of his head. " I want to talk alone," he said, and there was a suspicious thickness in his voice that warned me that any interview would have to be short. "I want to talk alone with Dr. Tampon." I staiitql in. aaonishment, for I knew they were not even acquainted. "Do you mean me ?" I asked, thinking that the iiiitry might have affected his brain. " No, no !" he said almost impatientl7, mean that gentleman." It was a strange re- quest, but I could see that every moment now was precious. At a motion from me, Thomp. son took his place by the side of the patient E head, and the nurses, with myself, withdrew. Why should he wish to sneak to Thompson ? eauld see them from where I stood, the pa- tient speaking slowly and evidently pain- fully, and Thompson's nod of acqutesceace. Suddenly the, conversation came to an end, and beckoning one of the nurses to him, my colleague walked to the heap of clothing, and picklng up Newton's book, placed it in his pocket-, and, resuming his place by the pa- tients side, continued the conversation. MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

The Training of the Child.

"Surround the•child with that environment which seems best suited to his individual needs. If the public school is fashioning him to a cer- tain standard, it may be necessary for the mother both to see that he gets the most good from his school and that his character building continues at home. We cannot have too many scientifically trained, thinking mothers."! That is how Dr. Maria Montessori sums up her theories in regard to the proper surround- ings for small children, of which one hears so much nowadays.

"Study the individual child," says this edu- cationist, who is also a physician, and first started her now famous school in Rome. "Do not try to pour him into a mould and expect him always to come out a perfect casting. Even iron poured into a Mould will often show defects —sometimes has to be sent back to the furnace. How much more ridiculous to suppose that a living, mobile organism with a mind and a soul different from millions of others can be moulded to a set pattern I" The Teacher is a Student.

In the Montessori " -children's house" in Rome, the children are occttpied in varied ways. The teacher, the passive director of their activity, is on constant watch, making observa- tions of a psychological nature and along scien- tific standards. She is a student in a laboratory, where the phenomena which she observes are the acts of little children, quite as much a part of nature as the phenomena observed in the chemical laboratory. She sees the actions and the re-actions. She observes what causes will bring certain results, and she tabulates 'all this knowledge for future reference and use. _ _

_ "In the home there is opportunity for just such a laboratory," says Dr. Diontessori. "Study the individual child and use your best judgment to provide him with the environment which seems best suited to the needs of his pro- gression."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0277.txt | included=true | included =====
COOKERY CORNER.

A Few Useful Recipes.

A good crust for pies is made thus: Rub a little salt into Ilb. flour, and then work in five ounces of vegetable butter; stir in a little less than 4 teacupful of cold water, and roll out. You will find it quite sufficient for the upper and under crust for a large pie. When making rhubarb or any other fruit pie, dust a little flour over the bottom crust, to absorb, and slightly thicken the juice, so that it wiil not boil out.

Cucumber and Onion Ketchup. "Ohio ketchup" is another name for this ketchup, which has only to be known to be popular here with us. Peel and chop into small pieces one dozen cucumbers and three large onions, sprinkle over them four ounces of fine table salt, and drain them on a hair sieve for eight hours. Add one-third of a teacupful of mustard seed and half an ounce of white pepper. Mix all well together and put into a stone jar and cover with the strongest vinegar. Tie up with bladder. It will be ready for use in a few days. White Rice Soup.

Here is a recipe which you will like—it is neither expensive nor troublesome to make. The foundation is two quarts of water in which a breakfast cup of haricot beans have been boiled —the liquor being strained off for stock. 'Wash four ounces of rice, and put it into boiling water; continue boiling for five minutes; drain it, and add it to the (boiling) water, drained from the haricots. Season with salt and pepper, and add a very little mace. Let all simmer till tender, add pint of milk, boil up once, and it is ready to serve. This is a suitable soup for children.

'To- morrow, the whole world—my world, would know that, in addition to being a ruined gambler, Donald Fraser, Doctor of Medicine, had violated ,the sacred laws of humanity, had dishonoured his noble iprofession, by at- tempting to rob a patient . I made my way back to the table. Thomp- son 'wired curiously at me, but I avoided his glance, and by a mighty effort of will all emotions were hidden for a time, and I took my place at his side, not as the man whose reputation was at his mercy, but as a sur- geon.

I took up the conical inheer that lay on the tray, and, bending over the head of the dying man, for dying now he undoubtedly was, placed it over the patient's mouth and nose, and gently shook a few drops of the anwsthctic over its porous sides. The sick.y, penetrating odour of the chloroform rose, and, as he felt the first whiff of it in his lungs, Newton opened his eyes, and, looking at Thompson in a meaning manner, said the one Word, " remember.- Thompson nod- ded. and again glanced from me to the pa- tient, who, closing his eyes, was gently in- haling. Now and then, as is usual, when pa- tients are sinking under the influence of the chloroform, the breathing for a moment ceased, but a slight pressure upon the unin- jured side would cause him to resume his re- spiration. After a while, I lifted the eyelids, and gently touched the pupils. Yes, he was well under. I handed the inhaler to Thomp- son and, dipping my hands into the antisep- tic lotion, I commenced.

It was 'a difficult job, even more so than I had anticipated ; the fracture extended fur- ther than I had thought, but I had not been working long before I realised the hopelcss- ness of my task. But, to a surgeon, the adage, " Whilst there's life, there's hope," has a special significance, and the fact that to all appearance my work would be useless did not deter me from doing my utmost. The end came very quietly. Thompson, who was administering sufficiently small doses of chloroform to keep the patient insensible, suddenly dropped the inhaler, and, picking up the stethoscope, applied it to the heart. I stopped my work and leaned, red-handed, on the table, awaiting his decision. "You can stop," he said after awhile "he's dead." I took the instrument from him and listened. It was as he said! Newton had gone—to face his Maker. Hastily washing and drying my hands, I slipped on my coat, and made my way back to my room.

Nov that it was all over, a dreary feeling of my own helplessness came over me, 'and as I drew a basket chair up to the fire that the porter had replenished in my absence, I thought of the misery I should have to face on the morrow. I glanced at my watch; it was three o'clock. To-morrow, then, was to- day. To-day I should die—the social death. I never realised till that minute how dear this little world of mine was to me. The functions that I had held up to ridicule as senseless and time wasting, -seemed new to be clothed with a dignity, and refinement unthought of. The profession I loved so well, and God knows, no mother Vould euffer payre at the

II LESS GAS AT WIDNES.

At Widnes Town Council on Tuesday the Gas and Water Committee reported a decrease in the consumption of gas during April of over 3,000,000 feet, and this in spite of the recent outlay of .t 26,000 for elaborate improvements in gas manufacture. Alderman Smith stated that thesdecrease was largely due to the reduced con- sumption by the works, and instanced a further factor in the extraordinarily bright weather. Mr. G. H. Davies complained that on nights when the moon was supposed to be at full the greater portion of the town had been in dark- ness. He quoted one case in which an alderman had been called out to have definite evidence that there were only two dim lights for an entire ward. Alderman Smith said they had not universal lighting in Widnes, and at times the moon failed them. (Laughter.)

CURIOUS CLAIM AT ST. HELENS.

Patrick Burns claimed compensation at St. Helens from Mrs. Hannah Rowlands, of Dam House Farm, Clock Face.—Burns stated that in September of last year, whilst employed by the defendant mowing grass he cut his left hand, and a finger had to be amputated.—Defendant swore that she never heard of the accident until some time this year. Burns was never em- ployed by her, but called at the farm and she gave him a few coppers on three occasions. If he had cut his,hand as alleged he ought to have gone to the farmhouse and had it properly dressed.—The judge said he was not satisfied that there had been any accident, and gave an award in the defendant's favour.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
Apple Chutney.

This delicious chutney will, I believe, appeal to you all. Take four pounds of sour apples, two pounds of Demerara sugar, three pints of vinegar, one and a half pounds of raisins, two ounces of mustard seed, one ounce ,of salt, one ounce of garlic, one ounce of ground ginger, and a quarter of a teaspoonful of cayenne pepper. Peel and cut the apples into dice, stone and cut up the raisins, peel and chop the garlic. Put all into an enamelled pan and boil for two hours, stirring frequently. Bottle for use.

Sandon received a bye in the second round, and, from all accounts, the Cemetery men should take the green with odds in favour of them.

A TIE.

Unique " Works " Result.

It is not often in the Warrington Works Association Competition that a match results in a tie, but nevertheless such was the result between Longford No. 3 and Whitecross (Gal- vanising Department). The handicapping proved to be just right, although several of the players had very few woods to get home to win. Not a mail was handicapped below five, and five of the Longford men received seven start so that the battles for a few points were short and sweet. J. Appleton and J. Wright had only eight points each to score, and at the. sixth end Appleton led by 12-8. However, Wright man- aged to score two doubles and one single before the finish. J. Fox (5) gained a well-deserved victory over W. Taylor (7), and was the better man all through. He got away from the first, but after leading 10-9, 12-9, 12-10, 12-11, 13-11, and 14-11 he was brought to boot, and Taylor peeled with 14. Fox sent a better wood up and won by the odd `'un. The other games were somewhat more tame in character, but, nevertheless, taking everything into considera- tion, the bowling was not so awful as one would imagine from players with such handicaps.

PAINTERS AND PLUMBERS RETIRE. The P. and K. Engineers set out to win, and win they did. The 23 points was a just margin, for in the individual games the Engineers were just that little better which counts for so much. T. Ellison (7) beat A. Bennett (7) by one point in a game of eleven lengths, although after five ends Bennett only wanted three to win. Ellison buckled to, and after peeling with 14 got the winner home. J. Massey .(5) beat J. Pollard (6) by three points, after having peeled with 10 and 12, but J. Pemberton (4) put the "top hat" on when he clean-plated Howard (7), of the plumbers. J. Budge (4), P. and K., and W. Ellsworth (4) were a fairly well-matched couple and played steadily all the way. There was nothing much to choose between -them, and Unsworth had no chance to slack to win by a couple. J. Boardman (4) and I. Gibson (4) played an equally good game, for only one point divided them at the finish. Boardman looked like winning when he led 14-12 at the four- teenth end, but Gibson got in a good double and single. SHIPPING TEAM BEATEN.

Crosfield's Shipping team went under to the Greening's men by 10 points, although they strove valiantly to hold their own, if not to win. Floyd (3) played a good game for them, but he could not hold Graham (5), who went to 10-6 in no time and 14-7 in eleven ends. Then he stopped, for Floyd ran his woods well after his jack and landed three singles and a double before he was beaten by three. Bridgen (3) was in no way afraid of Hughes, although the latter had seven points' start, and by consistent play he won by two points in a 17 ends' game. Greening's should do well in the competition if they maintain their form.

ASYLUM TEAM "NETTED." With such a big handicap the County Asylum (No. 2) should have won„ but Whitecross (Net- ting) were one toe many' for them, or, rather, three too many for them, for their ultimate victory was by a. trio of points. Wakefield (7), Whitecross, was clean-plated by J. Bramhall (7) in six ends, but H. Wright (4), the White- cross player set his followers a good example when he beat J. Hodnett, the opposing skipper, by 15-13. HOdnett only scored one single point in the last seven ends. The other games were of a similar nature, although F. Dean (4), the White-cross captain, had hard lines to be beaten by J. Farmer (7) by one point.

RYLANDS' v. LOCKER'S.

Despite the odds -i-igainsi.-tfiena-,..t1;e Rylands' men, who had only three players with a handi- cap over one, had little difficulty in accounting for Locker's to the tune of 23 points. They only lost four games and these were not by large majorities. T. Clare (1) made E. Lawrence (7) wonder when he won by 15-11, and W. Rough- ley (scratch) surprised R. Howard equally as much with his 15-10 win. Other fine wins were those of W. Cottam, T. Platt—who, after being behind 14-6 at the eleventh end, won by one point—H. Langley, T. Humphries, W. Lang- ley, T. Hewitt, W. Ashton, J. Gleave, and A. Ford.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO POINTS' WIN.

The Corporation men were the best set of players when they met Forshaw's, for they were handicapped lower and won by two points. There was nothing easy about the victory as the game between J. Gleave (4) and J. Legge (6) will indicate. Legge went to 10-7, but Gleave peeled with 10 at the eighth end. At the 15th end the Corporation trundler was 14-13, and he had to use skill and judgment all through to win by two points. Another narrow thing was the victory of A. Hadland (4) over W. Taylor (3). Taylor -led 13-11, and looked a certain winner, but Hadland got the winning wood on the jack after having peeled with 14. It took 20 lengths before T. Laws on(3) beat W. Swarm (4) by one, the players peeling at 10, 11, 12, 13, and 14, It was a good match and well worth
