WHITSCROO v. CROSPIELD'S.

In this match Whitecross (No. 1) were the victors over Crosfield's (Soap Department) by 28 points. Although they were handicapped with more generosity, the Soapery men could not stand up against the splendid play on the part of the Whitecross players, who only re- turned four losing cards. SOllle keeti thgsles were witnessed, and in both teams there were no absolute tryros to be carried on the baclis of the better players.

===== 0002601_19140516_art0292.txt | included=true | included =====
AMMEURS OR PROFESSIONALS? Action by Amateur Association.

As Mr. Ball (the chairman) said at a meeting of the Warrington and District Amateur Bowl- ing Association, in the Junior Conservative Club on Thursday evening, they gad unpleasant business to do, and this was in connection with Rules 27 and 28 of the association, which states: "That any member of the association who plays in public competitions for money, or who is in the habit of playing matches for money in pub- lic, whether gate money is taken or not, shall be deemed ineligible to take part in the Asso- ciation Competitions; • and such club will be liable for expulsion, and the committee reserves the right to refuse any team or player."

===== 0002601_19140516_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
BREAKING AMATEUR RULES.

..... ..----. The Chairman said there were names that had been brought before them that were without question breakers of the amateur rules, and, again, there were names that might be passed. The sub-committee would not take the respon- sibility of passing or rejecting any players, and they left it to that meeting. The Secretary answered Mr. J. Willett in the affirmative when he asked if they would be within the bounds of the British Crown Green Association rules.

Mr. Willett poinied out that one of the rules of the B.C.G.A. stated that if they played a man. knowing him, to be a professional, they forfeited the match.

The Chairman said they could suspend a man who transgressed the amateur rule.

The meeting rejected: amongst others, those who had played in the Blackpool handicap, and the Chairman expressed the hope that that would be the first and last time they would have to do such a thing. ,

A sub-committee was appointed to visit the various greens in regard to the handicap next month, and get the best offer they can.

. BATTLE OF GIANTS. There will be -a battle of the giants in the second round of the Championship on May 30th, when last , year's finalists—Cemetery Hotel— meet the Senior Conservative Club contingent. The draw for the second round was made as follows: Rylands' (No. 1) v. Woolston ; Walker's Brewery v. King and .Queen; Cemetery Hotel v. Senior Conservative Club; Hutching's Tan- nery or Vulcan v. Liberal Club; Croft v. WaL:on; Whitecross or Rylands' (No. 2) v. Fearnhead or Crosfield's (No. 2); Penketh or Junior Conser- vative Club v. Earlestown Viaduct; Bull's Head v. Crosfield's (No. 1). A start will be made in the Ch.N.mpionsbip Competition to-day, the draw for which has been published in "The Examiner." The next meeting will be held at the King and Queen Hotel on June 2nd.

LIBERAL CLUB MAIN.

The first monthly main was played on the Liberal Club green on Saturday, when there was a large number of entrants. some fine bowling was witnessed on the part of the competitors, and particularly in the semi-final and final. At one time the Crosfield Cup winner of last year, 6. W. Jeffery, was lookeu upon as the winner. but was defeated after a strenuous game by J. Archer in tne semi-final. The new secretary of the green, Mr. G. Marshall, was looked upon as the nest best to Jeffery, his bowling being char- acterised by superb judgment. The finalists were J. Archer and J. I,awless, both of whom had played a consistent game all through. Mr. Lawless was the ultimate winner. The com- mittee who were in charge of the main are de- serving of great praise.

WARRINGTON'S TWO WINS. W. Bennett and Ilannam, Bon I Well.

Warrington secured a dual victory in their week-end matches. • The Seniors, at St. Helens, defeated the Recs, of that town somewhat easily. The Juniors, at Arpley, experiendod a close and exciting finish in their match with St. Helens Recs. Reserve, and won by four runs, the scores being 100 to 96. The Seniors disposed of their opponents for a cost of 66 runs. Then they went in and com- fortably knocked up 115 for the loss of seven wickets. _ .

W. Richardson still maintains his fine form With the willow and he had compiled a nice 40 When he gave his first chance, which was Promptly taken advantage of by Walker, and the Warrington batsman was caught. J. S. Broome was discovered also in batting humour. He knocked up a safe 34, when he was stumped by Gray. H. Hawthorn (15) and Hannam (10) were the other two who reached double figures. The victory was undoubtedly due to the fine form displayed by the two Warrington trundlers W. Bennett and Hannam. Bennett, in 13.3 overs, six of which were maidens, took six wickets at a cost of 31 runs. Hannam, in 14 overs, three of which were maidens, secured four wickets for 33 runs.

The details of this match were:— St. Helens Recs.—J. E. Walker c Bradburn b Bennett 2, J. Harris c Hawthorn b Hannam 0, R. W. Harper b Bennett 11, J. WINWICK CRICKET CLUB.

- - . Winwick Asylum entertained the L. and N.-W. Recreation Club on Saturday, and won by 61 rung. P. Hilton, Dr. Stone and G. Daw- son were the ones chiefly responsible for their side's success. Hilton claimed the honours of the day. Going in first wicket down, be carried his bat out for 30, which, considering the state of the wicket, and the quality of the bowling, was more valuable than the _score shows. • Hilton also shone with the ball. He bowled two overs, taking 3 wickets, all clean bowled, without a run, being scored off him. Dr. Stone rendered his side valuable assistance with a well-compiled 20. Other double-figure scorers were: Fawcett (13), Kenwird (10), and "Walsh

LYALNI URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTION, 1914. TO THE ELECTORS OF HEATLEY WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— A vacancy having occurred in the Heaney Ward owing to the resignation of Mr. Councillor S. Davies, and having been nominated by a number of influential ratepayers of all shades of opinion, I have decided to place my services at your dis- posal. I am fighting this contest independent of party lines. I feel that the best interests of this Ward cannot possibly be served if one is to allow his judgment to be dominated by political feeling, as good measures are liable to be introduced by either party and over-ruled simply by the majority of the party which happens to be in the ascendant, consequently arresting the progress of measures which would possibly be beneficial to us all. This is a point in my address which I feel very strongly about. One reason which strengthens my claim on the district is that I am in business in the Ward, and my work takes me out daily throughout the whole of the Lymm Urban District, and enables me to see a great deal of the requirements and come in close contact with all classes of the community and learn their views on the various questions which arise. In the event of you returning me as your mem- ber, it is not my intention to criticise and find fault with the work already done by the Council, until I am conversant with the facts which -have induced them to arrive at their conclusions. What I promise you is to look after your inter- , ests, end when questions arise which affect our general welfare, will bring to bear the best intelli- gence r possess to deal with them. Hoping I than receive your hearty support on 144 y 3rd, I mil, your f aithfully, F. INGHAM. Iteatley.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 16, 1914.

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE. Exceptionally High Tide. I The tide last Sunda at Warrington Bridge ' was the highest for Zconsiderable time past, and rose twenty feet. A similar tide will not be experienced until August.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS. " RAFFLES " AND A BENEFIT. Mr. Warburton's Great Programme for Next Thursday. Raffles, the amateur cracksman, pays a visit to the Royal Court Theatre next week with Mr. Gerald Alexander's Company, including Mr. A. W. Tyrer, in Mr. Gerald Du Maurier's famous impersonation of the gentlemen thief. An amusing story is told with regard to the present company. While playing in a provincial town the early part of the year, a police detective inquired of the manager of the theatre if the company playing "Raffles" were quite nice people. The manager replied, with surprise at the question, that they appeared quite a nice company, and asked why the detective made the inquiry. The detective replied that on a previous visit of "Raffles" there had been four burglaries, but on this visit the burglaries had been six. As the play centres round the exploits of a gentleman thief, the allusion was obvious. The manager assured the detective that however well the company performed the parts of cracks on the stage they did not carry it into their private lives. It has been suggested that the powerful auting of the company induced the "local , cracks" to greater endeavours. There will be a special matinee on Monday at 2.30 o'clock. Twice nightly-6.50 and 9. MR. WARBURTON'S BENEFIT. Thursday is set apart for Mr. 11. L. Warbur- ton's benefit. The beneficiaire makes an ideal acting manager, and neither Sir Joseph Beechath, as proprietor, nor Mr. Loader, as general manager, nor the general public, could be better served than they are by Mr. Warbur- ton. The Royal Court Theatre, under his

f'U BL I C AM USE M ENTS—continued. captivates with all her songs. The Three Macjahns present something entirely novel in their daring comedy bar act, and their tricks are characterised with celerity and precision. Next Week's Programme, Leslie Brothers, in a comical musical act, "Music Mad," and The Three Duckleys, in electrical juggling novelties, head a splendid programme for next week, which will include the following: Four Flying Julians, in "The Intoxicated Gymnast"; Guest and Newlyn, quaint comedians; Randolph Sutton, light comedian; Minnie Muir, comedienne; St. Just and Vandalle, in vocal comedy scena. I_,l Y M M the election, for, as he asked, " Wliat good can politics serve in a ward election?" HINTS ON HEALTH. Some Useful and Common Remedies. The following simple medicines should always be found in the medicine press—a liniment for colds, costing, a whole bottleful, 6d.; a cure for toothache, at 2d.; and an alleviative for that woman's special bane, neuralgia or sick head- ache. The liniment for ontward application in case of cold on lungs, in the throat or bronchial tubes, is made of two yolks of eggs, a gill of acetic acid, a gill of turpentine, mixed well together and kept corked. Before use, shake thoroughly. Toothache Cure. Toothache mixture: Equal quantities of strong tincture of aconite and iodine. Shake well and keep in a blue bottle with an orange label on it. Remember, this is a poison. Paint the %um round a troublesome tooth with this mixture and pain disappears like magic. Headaches. For sick headache: Get a lemon and squeeze its juice into a tumbler. Add a tablespoonful of sugar and a small saltspoonful of baking soda. I If a headache comes on after reading or other close work, of if the letters run together and blur, then the headache would probably be due to eye trouble and the want of suitable glasses. A headache without any of the eye symptoms such as are mentioned usually points to dis- turbance of digestion, a sluggish liver, or con- stipation.

MEDICINE LOVE. Are Warrington Insured Persons Drinking Too Large a Quantity? DEFICIT ON CHEMISTS' BILLS. Are we becoming a nation of drug takers? The Insurance Committee in the North of Eng- land, more particularly in Lancashire and the manufacturing districts of Yorkshire, are be- coming alarmed at the amount of drugs con- sumed under prescriptions granted by Insurance Act doctors. The committees, in common phraseology, are "broke," and cannot meet the chemists' bills for the medicines consumed. To find a way out of the difficulty an impor- tant conference of representatives from Insur- ance Committees was held at Preston on Thurs- day. Warrington Insurance Committee sent three delegates, as Warrington finds itself in a position similar to the experience of other Insurance Committees. A Deficit of £213. An important statement upon the subject was submitted to the monthly meeting of the War- rington Insurance Committee, held in the Council Chamber on Tuesday evening. The statement' showed that the chemists' accounts for the first quarter of the present year amounted to £Bl3 10s. 11d., compared with £582 us. 4d. for the same quarter of last year, an increase of .t 230 19s. 7d.70n the corresponding quarter. The statement added "The approximate amount available for the first quarter of 1914 is £6OO, which means a deficit of approximately £213. It should, how- -1 I ever, be borne in mind that the accounts for the whole year will be Calculated together, and it is very probable that the prescriptions for the next two quarters will be considerably reduced in number. Number of Average Prescriptions. value. 1 Ist quarter, 1913 ... 19,813 7.14 pence. 2nd quarter, 1913 ... 14,636 7.64 pence. 3rd quarter, 1913 ... 14,782 7.88 pence. 4th quarter, 1913 ... 16,478 8.35 pence. Ist quarter, 1914 ... 22,536 8.67 pence. "It will be seen that the increase in the average value of the prescriptions for the first quarter of this year over the corresponding 'quarter of last year is 1.53 pence, the increase in the number of prescriptions being 2,723. If the average value of the first quarter of 1913 had been maintained during the past quarter, the accounts would have totalled to ,e 670 Be. 11d., or „e 143 2s. less than the accounts as rendered." The statement was not discussed at the In- surance Committee meeting, only received, but the whole question of chemists' charges was discussed at the conference on Thursday. • LOVE OF MEDICINE. "The Examiner" representative had a chat with a doctor about the above. "The quarter shown," he said, "was the one in which the greatest amount of sickness gener- ally prevails, and that undoubtedly helped greatly to augment the chemists' bills." "Has a love for medicines arisen?" he was asked. "Perhaps there has," was' the answer. "Cer- tainly more medicines are required. It is well known that they are consumed in unnecessarily large quantities by insured persons. Eight- ounce betties, which should last a certain num- ber of days, are usually finished in half the time, and they come back for more. The novelty of medicine taking will, however, die, and perhaps then matters will come to a normal level." THE PRESTON CONFERENCE. A Sub-committee Appointed. A conference was held on Thursday at the County Hall, Preston, to discuss the question of over-prescribing and. to formulate a scheme for the checking of the prescriptions and their scrutiny, so that it might be known if any par- ticular doctor had been over-prescribing. Under the present system it is possible for three inde- pendent checks of each prescription to be made; by the Insurance Committees, the chemists, and the doctors. Several opinions were expressed at the meet- ing, and eventually it was decided to appoint a sub-committee to inquire into the possibilities of the three committees—lnsurance, Pharn•a- ceutical and Panel—uniting, to check and scrutinise the prescriptions at the same time, each section bearing a proportion of the cost. Six members of each society were appointed on the sub-committee, agreeing to consider the feasability of the scheme and to present a report at a future meeting. Before this can be done, the various Insurance Committees will, of course, have to be consulted. Warrington was represented at the conference by Dr. G. W. Joseph and Mr. A. Tilling (Insur- ance Committee), Messrs. J. Rymer Young and j. S. Hill (Pharmaceutical Committee), and Drs. Adams and Sutherland (Panel Committee). Dr. Sutherland was appointed as representative of the doctors. Mr. S. P. Vivien, one of the Insurance Com- missioners, was present at the meeting.
